Joint ergodicity - 40 years on Thanks: The author was supported by the National Science Center (NCN Poland) Sonata grant No. 2024/55/D/ST1/00468 and would also like to acknowledge the Foundation of Polish Science (FNP) Start stipend.
Borys Kuca

Abstract
. Recent years have seen dramatic progress in the study of joint ergodicity, i.e. a scenario in which a multiple ergodic average converges in norm to the product of integrals of individual functions. This survey, accompanying the talk given by the author in the Perspectives on Ergodic Theory and its Interactions conference to celebrate Vitaly Bergelson’s 75th birthday, aims to summarize these recent advances, outline crucial new tools, present various open problems, and highlight the main challenges currently faced in the study of multiple ergodic averages.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Multiple ergodic averages: what are they, and why should you care?

Furstenberg’s celebrated proof of the Szemerédi theorem
[
72
]
initiated the systematic investigation of
multiple ergodic averages
, a class of multilinear operators of dynamical origin that arise in the search for arithmetic patterns in sets of positive density and other “large” sets. Given some integer sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
, we are interested in the (norm, weak, or pointwise) limit of

\displaystyle A_{N}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}):=\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{a_{\ell}(n)}f_{\ell}

as
N\to\infty
, where:

•

\E_{n\in[N]}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}
is a finite Cèsaro average;

•

(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
is a
system
, i.e. a collection of measure-preserving transformations
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
acting on a standard probability space
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
(which throughout this survey we assume to be
invertible
and
commuting
unless stated otherwise);

•

f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
are bounded functions.

Multiple ergodic averages have been investigated for many classes of integer sequences, including polynomials, generalized polynomials, Hardy sequences, tempered functions, sequences involving primes, and random sequences.

Broadly speaking, the
study of multiple ergodic averages can be split into the following classes of problems:

(i)

(Multiple recurrence) Show that for any
E\in{\mathcal{X}}
with
\mu(E)>0
, there exists
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
for which

\displaystyle\mu(E\cap T_{1}^{-a_{1}(n)}E\cap\cdots\cap T_{\ell}^{-a_{\ell}(n)}E)>0.

(ii)

(Convergence) Show that
A_{N}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell})
converges as
N\to\infty
, whether in
L^{2}(\mu)
, weakly, or pointwise almost everywhere.

(iii)

(Structure) Describe the limit of
A_{N}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell})
as
N\to\infty
.

While the multiple recurrence problem is strictly speaking not about multiple ergodic averages, the ergodic way to prove it is by showing that the average

\displaystyle\E_{n\in[N]}\mu(E\cap T_{1}^{-a_{1}(n)}E\cap\cdots\cap T_{\ell}^{-a_{\ell}(n)}E)=\int 1_{E}\cdot\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}1_{E}\cdots T_{\ell}^{a_{\ell}(n)}1_{E}\;d\mu

has positive liminf as
N\to\infty
. Such results have yielded new extensions of Szemerédi’s theorem when combined with the Furstenberg correspondence principle, a variant of which is presented below. This transference principle serves as the main gateway between ergodic theory and additive combinatorics.

Theorem 1.1

(Furstenberg’s correspondence principle (cf.
[
11
, Theorem 1.1]
))
.

Let
k,\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
. For every
A\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}
there exists a system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
and a set
E\in{\mathcal{X}}
with the following properties:

(i)

\overline{d}(A)=\mu(E)
;

(ii)

for all
{\mathbf{n}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{n}}_{k}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}
given by
{\mathbf{n}}_{i}=(n_{i1},\ldots,n_{i\ell})\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}
, we have

\displaystyle\overline{d}\mathopen{}\left(A\cap(A-{\mathbf{n}}_{1})\cap\cdots\cap(A-{\mathbf{n}}_{k})\mathclose{}\right)\geq\mu(E\cap\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}T_{j}^{-n_{1j}}E\cap\cdots\cap\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}T_{j}^{-n_{kj}}E).

Our understanding of the aforementioned problems is sharpest for integer polynomials, for which the state of the art can be summarized as follows. Modulo some notable exceptions (see Problem
38
), the questions regarding multiple recurrence are largely resolved thanks to the works of Furstenberg
[
72
]
, Furstenberg-Katznelson
[
74
]
, Bergelson-Leibman
[
19
]
, Leibman
[
117
]
, Bergelson-Leibman-Lesigne
[
23
]
, and others.
The same holds for norm convergence, due to the remarkable result of Walsh
[
143
]
vastly generalizing earlier works of Conze-Lesigne
[
39
]
, Furstenberg-Weiss
[
75
]
, Host-Kra
[
88
,
89
]
, Ziegler
[
149
]
, Leibman
[
118
]
, Tao
[
138
]
, Towsner
[
141
]
, Austin
[
2
]
, Host
[
87
]
, among others.
By contrast, the pointwise almost everywhere convergence of multiple ergodic averages, a topic first studied by Bourgain
[
34
]
over 35 years ago, remains a challenging open problem despite impressive recent breakthroughs due to Krause-Mirek-Tao
[
110
]
, Kosz-Mirek-Peluse-Wan-Wright
[
102
]
, and others.

Although the proofs of multiple recurrence, norm convergence, and pointwise almost everywhere convergence differ in many aspects,
they share a common core: they all require
some
structural description of the limit of the associated multiple ergodic average. Hence the last of the presented problems, that of the structure of the limit,
lies at the heart of the others.
The most general known multiple recurrence and convergence results rely on a rather basic description of the limit.
Yet there remain strong incentives to describe the limits as precisely as possible. Knowing the limit is without a doubt more intellectually satisfying than simply knowing its existence; but if this reason is not convincing enough, there are a few “practical” ones:

(i)

knowing the limit can yield new recurrence results and combinatorial consequences (see e.g. Theorem
6.4
) as well as strengthen the existing ones, implying e.g. the existence of so-called
popular common differences
(see e.g. Proposition
1.13
);

(ii)

for many averages, finding the limit is both the most straightforward way to establish its norm convergence (see e.g. Theorem
3.26
) and can also serve as a step to prove pointwise convergence (see e.g.
[
49
,
92
]
);

(iii)

the predominantly analytic methods developed for this purpose can often be adapted to the discrete or Euclidean setting, reinforcing the fruitful interactions between the infinitary world of ergodic theory, the finitary realm of additive combinatorics, and the continuous Euclidean universe.

Given this motivation, it is clear that the study of the limiting behavior of multiple ergodic averages occupies a central role in developing new extensions of the Szemerédi theorem.

Up to a few years ago, the structure of multiple ergodic averages along polynomials and other classes of iterates
was fairly well-understood in the case of a
single transformation
(i.e. when
T_{1}=\cdots=T_{\ell}
). However, little had been known for commuting transformations other than for a few isolated cases. This changed substantially in the last couple of years, with the advent of potent new techniques capable of answering many previously intractable problems.
This survey aims to summarize these advances and highlight remaining open problems, some old and some new. We have chosen to do so from the perspective of joint ergodicity, which has served as the focal point of many of these recent breakthroughs.

1.2.
How to describe the limits of multiple ergodic averages?

Before delving into the definition of joint ergodicity, we briefly discuss the surprisingly intricate question of what it even means to
describe
the limit of a multiple ergodic average.

1.2.1.
Explicit description of a limit

Given a multiple ergodic average, we would ideally like to identify an explicit limiting function. This best-case scenario is exemplified by the mean ergodic theorem below.

Theorem 1.2

(Von Neumann’s mean ergodic theorem)
.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
be a system and
f\in L^{2}(\mu)
. Then

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}T^{n}f-\E(f|{\mathcal{I}}(T))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0,

where
{\mathcal{I}}(T):=\{E\in{\mathcal{X}}\colon\;T^{-1}E=E\}
is the
\sigma
-algebra of
T
-invariant sets.
In particular, if
T
is ergodic, then

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}T^{n}f-\int f\;d\mu\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0.

Unfortunately, the mean ergodic theorem is rather exceptional in the level of precision with which it describes the limiting function. In most scenarios, life is not that simple, and finding an explicit limiting function is either infeasible or unhelpful, as illustrated by the example below.

Example 1.3

(Local obstructions for polynomials)
.

Let
q\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
be the rotation
Tx=x+1
mod
q
on
X={\mathbb{Z}}/q{\mathbb{Z}}
. Then

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}T^{n^{2}}f=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}/q{\mathbb{Z}}}\frac{|\{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}/q{\mathbb{Z}}\colon i\equiv n^{2}\!\!\!\mod q\}|}{q}T^{n}f,

where the limit can be taken in any suitable sense (in norm, weakly, or pointwise).
For instance, when
q=3
, we get

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}T^{n^{2}}f=\frac{f+2Tf}{3},

so in particular the limit is different from
\int f\;d\mu=\frac{f+Tf+T^{2}f}{3}
. The issue here is the
local obstructions
, i.e. the fact that integer polynomials are generally not equidistributed in residue classes. On the other hand, if
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
is
totally ergodic
(i.e.
T,T^{2},T^{3},\ldots
are all ergodic), then

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}T^{n^{2}}f-\int f\;d\mu\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0.

Describing the limit in a general system therefore requires us to merge the two extreme cases of periodic and totally ergodic systems. In particular, when the system admits complicated periodic factors, then any explicit formula for the limit would likely be too unsavoury to serve any practical purpose. Proposition
1.5
below will describe a more compact and ready-to-apply way of describing the limit, even if a less explicit one.

1.2.2.
Describing the limit via a characteristic factor or a seminorm

Faced with the difficulties inherent in explicit descriptions of limits,
we usually resort to describing the limit implicitly, by identifying a factor or seminorm that controls a given average.

Definition 1.4

(Factor and seminorm control)
.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system,
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
be sequences, and
1\leq j\leq\ell
be an index. We say that a factor
{\mathcal{Y}}_{j}\subseteq{\mathcal{X}}
or a seminorm
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{j}
on
L^{\infty}(\mu)

controls the sequences on the system at index
j
if for arbitrary
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
,

(1)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|A_{N}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0\quad\textrm{whenever}\quad\E(f_{j}|{\mathcal{Y}}_{j})=0\quad\textrm{or}\quad\left\|f_{j}\right\|_{j}=0.

If the same factor/seminorm can be chosen for any
j
, we simply say that it
controls the sequences on the system
,
controls the average
, or
controls the tuple
(T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)},\ldots,T_{\ell}^{a_{\ell}(n)})_{n}
.

The factor
{\mathcal{Y}}_{j}
is also known as
(partial) characteristic factor (at index
j
)
. The name comes from the work of Furstenberg and Weiss
[
75
]
, but the underlying idea already arises in the foundational work of Furstenberg
[
72
]
.

The factor and seminorm will in general depend on the index
j
, particularly while dealing with several different transformations. In the context of Example
1.3
, such an implicit description is provided by the
rational Kronecker factor

(2)

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T):=\bigvee_{r\in{\mathbb{N}}}{\mathcal{I}}(T^{r})=\langle E\in{\mathcal{X}}\colon T^{-r}E=E\;\textrm{for some}\;r\in{\mathbb{N}}\rangle,

i.e. the smallest factor of
{\mathcal{X}}
containing all its periodic factors.

Proposition 1.5

(Rational Kronecker factor controls single polynomial averages
[
73
, Lemma 3.14]
)
.

Let
a\in{\mathbb{Z}}[t]
be nonconstant,
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
be a system, and
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
. Then

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}T^{a(n)}f\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0\quad\textrm{whenever}\quad\E(f|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T))=0.

Proposition
1.5
formalizes the idea that the only obstructions in understanding single ergodic averages along integer polynomials come from periodic systems.

The control of the limit by a seminorm as in (
1
) is a purely analytic statement, usually achieved by chiefly analytic means. However, we are typically only interested in those seminorms
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{j}
that admit a dynamical interpretation, i.e. to which we can associate a factor
{\mathcal{Y}}_{j}
satisfying

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{j}=0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\E(f|{\mathcal{Y}}_{j})=0.

In many cases, the factors of interest admit a useful structural description, i.e. they are either (inverse limits of) algebraically structured systems (as in Example
1.6
below) or joins of simple and explicit factors of the system (see Example
1.8
). The control of an average by a factor plus the structure theory of the factor then gives us a dynamical description of the limit. The smaller the characteristic factor, the more precise our knowledge of the limit. Two quintessential instances of this procedure are presented below; for the definition of the seminorms and factors arising in both examples, see Appendix
B
.

Example 1.6

(Host-Kra theory for Furstenberg averages)
.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
be a system. In their seminal work
[
89
]
, Host and Kra showed that for every
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
there exists a constant
C_{\ell}>0
and a seminorm
\lvert\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!\rvert_{\ell,T}
on
L^{\infty}(\mu)
(called
degree-
\ell
Host-Kra seminorm
) such that for all 1-bounded functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have

(3)

\displaystyle\limsup_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T^{n}f_{1}\cdots T^{\ell n}f_{\ell}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq C_{\ell}\lvert\!|\!|f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{\ell,T}.

They then constructed a factor
{\mathcal{Z}}_{\ell-1}(T)\subseteq{\mathcal{X}}
such that

(4)

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{\ell,T}=0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\E(f|{\mathcal{Z}}_{\ell-1}(T))=0.

Splitting each
f_{j}
into
\E(f_{j}|{\mathcal{Z}}_{\ell-1}(T))
and its orthogonal complement, and using the seminorm control (
3
) together with the factor property (
4
) to annihilate the contribution of the orthogonal complement, they showed that it suffices to study the
L^{2}(\mu)
-limit of

(5)

\displaystyle\E_{n\in[N]}T^{n}f_{1}\cdots T^{\ell n}f_{\ell}

for
{\mathcal{Z}}_{\ell-1}(T)
-measurable functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}
. For ergodic systems, they then proved the following structure theorem.

Theorem 1.7

(Host-Kra structure theorem)
.

Let
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
be an ergodic system. Then
(X,{\mathcal{Z}}_{\ell},\mu,T)
is isomorphic to an inverse limit of
\ell
-step nilsystems.

Hence, passing to the ergodic components of
\mu
if needed and using everything above, Host and Kra reduced the study of the
L^{2}(\mu)
convergence of (
5
) to the analogous problem for
(\ell-1)
-step nilsystems. The (everywhere) convergence on nilsystems then follows from the work of Leibman
[
120
]
, with the limit described by Ziegler
[
148
, Theorem 1.2]
.

Example 1.8

(Host’s magic extensions)
.

Building on earlier work of Austin
[
2
]
, Host
[
87
]
extended bits of the Host-Kra theory to (the
L^{2}(\mu)
limits of) the averages

(6)

\displaystyle\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{n}f_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{n}f_{\ell},

coming from an arbitrary system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
(where, recalling our standing assumption,
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
are invertible and commute with each other). Specifically, he constructed a
box seminorm

\lvert\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!\rvert_{T_{\ell},T_{\ell}T_{1}^{-1},\ldots,T_{\ell}T_{\ell-1}^{-1}}
on
L^{\infty}(\mu)
and a
box factor

{\mathcal{Z}}(T_{\ell},T_{\ell}T_{1}^{-1},\ldots,T_{\ell}T_{\ell-1}^{-1})\subseteq{\mathcal{X}}

such that

(7)

\displaystyle\limsup_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{n}f_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{n}f_{\ell}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq\lvert\!|\!|f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{T_{\ell},T_{\ell}T_{1}^{-1},\ldots,T_{\ell}T_{\ell-1}^{-1}}

for all
1
-bounded functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, and moreover

(8)

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{T_{\ell},T_{\ell}T_{1}^{-1},\ldots,T_{\ell}T_{\ell-1}^{-1}}=0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\E(f|{\mathcal{Z}}(T_{\ell},T_{\ell}T_{1}^{-1},\ldots,T_{\ell}T_{\ell-1}^{-1}))=0.

Combining (
7
) and (
8
), he reduced the study of (
6
) to the case when
f_{\ell}
is measurable with respect to
{\mathcal{Z}}(T_{\ell},T_{\ell}T_{1}^{-1},\ldots,T_{\ell}T_{\ell-1}^{-1})
.
1
1

1

For Furstenberg averages (
5
), i.e. when
T_{j}=T^{j}
for some fixed transformation, we have
\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{T_{\ell},T_{\ell}T_{1}^{-1},\ldots,T_{\ell}T_{\ell-1}^{-1}}\asymp\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{\ell,T}
and
{\mathcal{Z}}(T_{\ell},T_{\ell}T_{1}^{-1},\ldots,T_{\ell}T_{\ell-1}^{-1})={\mathcal{Z}}_{\ell-1}(T)
by combining (
66
), (
67
), and (
69
). Hence box seminorms and factors extend the Host-Kra seminorms and factors to the setting of commuting transformations.

It turns out that in contrast to Host-Kra factors, box factors do not seem to admit a nice structural description inside the system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
itself. However, the system admits an extension
\pi:(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{\mathcal{X}},\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{T}_{\ell})\to(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
(which Host called
magic extension
) on which

(9)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Z}}(\widetilde{T}_{\ell},\widetilde{T}_{\ell}\widetilde{T}_{1}^{-1},\ldots,\widetilde{T}_{\ell}\widetilde{T}_{\ell-1}^{-1})={\mathcal{I}}(\widetilde{T}_{\ell})\vee{\mathcal{I}}(\widetilde{T}_{\ell}\widetilde{T}_{\ell-1})\vee\cdots\vee{\mathcal{I}}(\widetilde{T}_{\ell}\widetilde{T}_{1}^{-1}).

This means that every function
\widetilde{f}
measurable with respect to (
9
) can be approximated by linear combinations of functions
g_{0}g_{1}\cdots g_{\ell-1}
, where each
g_{j}
is measurable with respect to
{\mathcal{I}}(\widetilde{T}_{\ell}\widetilde{T}_{j}^{-1})
(where
\widetilde{T}_{0}:=\textrm{Id}_{\widetilde{X}}
).
In particular,

(10)

\displaystyle\widetilde{T}_{\ell}(g_{0}g_{1}\cdots g_{\ell-1})=g_{0}\cdot\widetilde{T}_{1}g_{1}\cdots\widetilde{T}_{\ell-1}g_{\ell-1},

and so
\widetilde{T}_{\ell}
disappears when acting on functions measurable with regards to (
9
).
Hence, lifting the functions
f_{j}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
to functions
\widetilde{f}_{j}=f_{j}\circ\pi\in L^{\infty}(\widetilde{\mu})
, using the properties (
7
), (
8
), and (
9
), approximating
\widetilde{f}_{\ell}
by a linear combination of functions
g_{0}g_{1}\cdots g_{\ell-1}
as above, and using the identity (
10
), the study of
L^{2}(\mu)
convergence of (
6
) can be reduced to the one of

\displaystyle\E_{n\in[N]}\widetilde{T}_{1}^{n}(\widetilde{f}_{1}\cdot g_{1})\cdots\widetilde{T}_{\ell-1}^{n}(\widetilde{f}_{\ell-1}\cdot g_{\ell-1}).

Inducting on
\ell
in this fashion, we can infer the norm convergence of (
6
) from the mean ergodic theorem.

The idea of finding a structured extension on which a multiple ergodic average admits a simpler structural description dates back to Furstenberg and Weiss
[
75
]
. For the average (
6
), Austin
[
2
]
first constructed a structured extension (which he called
pleasant extension
) satisfying the property (
9
) using Furstenberg self-joinings rather than box seminorms. In subsequent works (e.g.
[
3
,
4
]
), he constructed further extensions of a similar flavor to study other multiple ergodic averages. Host’s construction came later, but it was more explicit, and his machinery of box seminorms more robust, playing a crucial role in the developments summarized in this survey.

1.2.3.
Describing a limit by comparison

In certain cases, we may want to describe a limit that we a priori know very little about by comparing it with a limit of which we have fairly good understanding. A good example of this strategy is provided by the following result due to Frantzikinakis
[
53
]
, a special case of Theorem
6.4
.

Theorem 1.9

(Identity for arithmetic progressions along fractional powers)
.

Let
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
be a system. Then for any
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have

(11)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T^{{\lfloor n^{3/2}\rfloor}}f_{1}\cdots T^{\ell{\lfloor n^{3/2}\rfloor}}f_{\ell}-\E_{n\in[N]}T^{n}f_{1}\cdots T^{\ell n}f_{\ell}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0.

Since we know via Example
1.6
that the average on the right of (
11
) is controlled by the seminorm
\lvert\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!\rvert_{\ell,T}
and the factor
{\mathcal{Z}}_{\ell-1}(T)
, the same holds for the average on the left.

1.3.
Joint ergodicity: what is it, and why is it useful?

The preceding sections may leave the reader with the disheartening impression that we can explicitly identify the limit of multiple ergodic averages only in exceedingly rare cases. Fortunately, there exists a pretty vast class of averages that admit a very simple, explicit description of the limit. This brings us finally to the concept of joint ergodicity.

Definition 1.10

(Joint ergodicity)
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
be sequences and
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system. We say that

(i)

a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are
jointly ergodic
for
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
if

(12)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}T_{j}^{{a_{j}(n)}}f_{j}-\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}\int f_{j}\,d\mu\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0

holds for all
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
.

(ii)

a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are
jointly ergodic
if they are jointly ergodic for every system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
with
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
ergodic.
2
2

2

This is a stronger assumption than the ergodicity of the joint action of
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
. For instance, the joint action of
T_{1}(x,y)=(x+\sqrt{2},y)
and
T_{2}(x,y)=(x,y+\sqrt{3})
on
X={\mathbb{T}}^{2}
ergodic (in that no set of Lebesgue measure in
(0,1)
is invariant under both
T_{1},T_{2}
), but neither
T_{1}
nor
T_{2}
is individually ergodic.
For
\ell=1
, we simply say that
a_{1}
is
ergodic
.

When
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are jointly ergodic for
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
, we also say that the tuple
(T_{1}^{{a_{1}(n)}},\ldots,T_{\ell}^{{a_{\ell}(n)}})_{n}
is
jointly ergodic
for
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
. If
(T_{1}^{{a(n)}},\ldots,T_{\ell}^{{a(n)}})_{n}
is
jointly ergodic
for
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
, then we also say that
a
is jointly ergodic for
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
. In the case
\ell=1
, we simply say that
a
is
ergodic
for
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
.

A lot of sequences under consideration in this survey will be real-valued (e.g.
n^{3/2}
or
n\log n
), therefore we need some rounding function to turn them into integer sequences. To simplify the discussion, we adopt the following convention.

Convention 1
.

Let
{\mathcal{P}}
be a property defined for sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
. We say that sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
satisfy the property
{\mathcal{P}}
if their integer parts do.

For most of the results, the choice of the floor function versus other rounding functions (e.g. ceiling function, nearest integer) does not matter, and we will remark explicitly when it does.

Joint ergodicity was first studied by Berend and Bergelson in the 1980s
[
6
,
7
]
, who fully characterized the joint ergodicity of the averages (
6
).

Theorem 1.11

(Characterization of joint ergodicity for
(T_{1}^{n},\ldots,T_{\ell}^{n})_{n}

[
6
, Theorem 3.1]
)
.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system. Then
(T_{1}^{n},\ldots,T_{\ell}^{n})_{n}
is jointly ergodic for
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
if and only if the following two conditions are satisfied:

(i)

(Product ergodicity condition)
T_{1}\times\cdots\times T_{\ell}
is ergodic for
(X^{\ell},{\mathcal{X}}^{\otimes\ell},\mu^{\ell})
;

(ii)

(Difference ergodicity condition)
T_{i}T_{j}^{-1}
is ergodic for
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
for all distinct
1\leq i,j\leq\ell
.

One should see joint ergodicity as the best-case scenario, as it provides the nicest possible limiting formula for a multiple ergodic average. A clear necessary condition for joint ergodicity is that
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
are all ergodic (
not
to be confused with the ergodicity of their joint action). When the transformations are not ergodic, then the natural generalization of joint ergodicity is given by the concept below.

Definition 1.12

(Invariant factor control
3
3

3

In
[
67
]
, being controlled by the invariant factor was called
weak joint ergodicity
. However, given that this notion holds under more general assumptions on the system (no ergodicity required), it is contentious whether one should think of it as a weak or strong variant of joint ergodicity.
)
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
be sequences and
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system. We say that

(i)

a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are
controlled by the invariant factor
for
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
if

(13)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}T_{j}^{{a_{j}(n)}}f_{j}-\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}\E(f_{j}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{j}))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0

holds for all
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
.

(ii)

a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are
controlled by the invariant factor
if they are controlled by the invariant factor for every system.

The utility of this latter concept to combinatorics is best evidenced by the following result; it asserts that sequences controlled by the invariant factor satisfy a strong form of multiple recurrence. In what follows,
\overline{d}
denotes the positive upper density.

Proposition 1.13

(Lower bounds for multiple recurrence)
.

Suppose that the sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
are controlled by the invariant factor. Then the following hold:

(i)

For every system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
and every
E\in{\mathcal{X}}
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}\mu(E\cap T_{1}^{-{a_{1}(n)}}E\cap\cdots\cap T_{\ell}^{-{a_{\ell}(n)}}E)\geq\mu(E)^{\ell+1}.

In particular, for every
\varepsilon>0
, the set

(14)

\displaystyle\{n\in{\mathbb{N}}\colon\mu(E\cap T_{1}^{-{a_{1}(n)}}E\cap\cdots\cap T_{\ell}^{-{a_{\ell}(n)}}E)\geq\mu(E)^{\ell+1}-\varepsilon\}

has positive upper density.

(ii)

For any
A\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}}^{k}

and vectors
{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{v}}_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{k}
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}\overline{d}(A\cap(A-{\mathbf{v}}_{1}{a_{1}(n)})\cap\cdots\cap(A-{\mathbf{v}}_{\ell}{a_{\ell}(n)}))\geq\overline{d}(A)^{\ell+1}.

In particular, for every
\varepsilon>0
, the set

(15)

\displaystyle\{n\in{\mathbb{N}}\colon\overline{d}(A\cap(A-{\mathbf{v}}_{1}{a_{1}(n)})\cap\cdots\cap(A-{\mathbf{v}}_{\ell}{a_{\ell}(n)}))\geq\overline{d}(A)^{\ell+1}-\varepsilon\}

has positive upper density.

If furthermore the formulas (
12
) and (
13
) hold for any Følner sequence on
{\mathbb{N}}
in place of
([N])_{N\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, then we can conclude that the sets (
14
) and (
15
) are syndetic.

The elements of the sets (
14
) or (
15
) are often called
popular common differences
(although the term does not have a precise definition, given that it depends on
\varepsilon
).

Proof.

We start with the first claim. Fix a system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
. The joint ergodicity of the sequences implies that

\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}\mu(E\cap T_{1}^{-{a_{1}(n)}}E\cap\cdots\cap T_{\ell}^{-{a_{\ell}(n)}}E)\\
=\int 1_{E}\cdot\E(1_{E}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{1}))\cdots\E(1_{E}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{\ell}))\;d\mu.

By
[
37
, Lemma 1.6]
, a consequence of the Hölder inequality, the right hand side is bounded from below by
\mu(E)^{\ell+1}
, implying the first result.

Now let

\displaystyle E_{n}

\displaystyle:=E\cap T_{1}^{-{a_{1}(n)}}E\cap\cdots\cap T_{\ell}^{-{a_{\ell}(n)}}E

\displaystyle B_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle:=\{n\in{\mathbb{N}}\colon\mu(E\cap T_{1}^{-{a_{1}(n)}}E\cap\cdots\cap T_{\ell}^{-{a_{\ell}(n)}}E)\geq\mu(E)^{\ell+1}-\varepsilon\}.

Then

\displaystyle\mu(E)^{\ell+1}

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}\mu(E_{n})

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}\mu(E_{n})1_{B_{\varepsilon}}(n)+\limsup_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}\mu(E_{n})1_{{\mathbb{N}}\backslash B_{\varepsilon}}(n)

\displaystyle\leq\overline{d}(B_{\varepsilon})+\mu(E)^{\ell+1}-\varepsilon;

hence
\overline{d}(B_{\varepsilon})\geq\varepsilon
, implying the second result. The consequence for the subsets of
{\mathbb{Z}}^{k}
follows from Furstenberg’s correspondence principle. The deduction of the syndeticity of the sets (
14
) and (
15
) under the stronger assumption on (
12
) and (
13
) follows by modifying the arguments above in a straightforward fashion.
∎

For certain sequences such as integer polynomials, the control by invariant factor is too much to hope for. Indeed, Example
1.3
shows that the single ergodic average along
n^{2}
is not controlled by the invariant factor due to local obstructions. In this and similar cases, we need to replace the invariant factor by the rational Kronecker factor, defined in (
2
).

Definition 1.14

(Rational Kronecker factor control)
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
be sequences and
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system. We say that

(i)

a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are
controlled by the rational Kronecker factor
for
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
if for all
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
,

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}T_{j}^{{a_{j}(n)}}f_{j}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0

holds whenever
\E(f_{j}|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T_{j}))=0
for some
1\leq j\leq\ell
.

(ii)

a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are
controlled by the rational Kronecker factor
if they are controlled by the rational Kronecker factor for every system.

Rational Kronecker factor control typically suffices for popular common differences as long as our sequences satisfy good divisibility properties:

Definition 1.15

(Good divisibility properties)
.

Sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
satisfy
good divisibility properties
if for all
r\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the set

\displaystyle\{n\in{\mathbb{N}}\colon r|a_{1}(n),\ldots,a_{\ell}(n)\}

has positive upper density.

See e.g.
[
65
, Theorem 1.3, Corollary 1.7]
,
[
69
, Corollaries 2.11 and 2.12]
, and
[
68
, Theorem 3.4]
for popular common difference corollaries for integer polynomials and other sequences with good divisibility properties.

1.4.
What do we study when we study joint ergodicity?

We hope to have convinced the reader by now that joint ergodicity and its variants are a worthwhile object of examination: not only do they provide clear limiting formulas, but they also lead to strong forms of recurrence and offer valuable insight into analogous problems in additive combinatorics. But what are the actual questions that we examine when we study joint ergodicity? At least three classes of problems fall under the umbrella of joint ergodicity, and we shall discuss them in turn in the subsequent chapters:

(i)

(Joint ergodicity criteria) Identify simple sufficient and necessary criteria for joint ergodicity, invariant factor control, and rational Kronecker factor control.

(ii)

(Joint ergodicity for large classes of sequences and systems) Identify large classes of sequences for which we have joint ergodicity under mild assumptions on the system.

(iii)

(Joint ergodicity classification problem) Characterize sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
with the following property: the sequences are jointly ergodic for a system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},...,T_{\ell})
if and only if they simultaneously satisfy (an appropriate generalization of) the difference and product ergodicity conditions for the system.

We start this survey with the presentation in Section
2
of two approaches, which we call Old and New Joint Ergodicity Strategies, to prove joint ergodicity of multiple ergodic averages. In doing so, we lay out the joint ergodicity criteria (Theorem
2.8
), proved by Frantzikinakis
[
59
]
as well as Frantzikinakis and the author
[
69
]
, that have been one of two main engines behind recent advances on the topic. We then present in Section
3
the main joint ergodicity results for integer polynomials, Hardy sequences, and other families of iterates under the assumptions of ergodicity, total ergodicity, and weak mixing. Section
4
discusses the so-called
joint ergodicity classification problem
, i.e. the question of identifying those families of sequences for which joint ergodicity on an arbitrary system is equivalent to the ergodicity of certain actions on this system. Subsequently, we move on to outline the proofs of two major technical breakthroughs that enabled recent joint ergodicity developments: the degree lowering argument and new methods for obtaining Host-Kra seminorm control. This is done in Section
5
.

Since joint ergodicity is only one possible scenario for what the limit of a multiple ergodic average might be, the survey would be incomplete without the broader picture of what happens in the absence of joint ergodicity. This is the content of Section
6
. Likewise, the importance of assuming the commutativity of transformations becomes more pronounced when juxtaposed with its absence; this is what we discuss in Section
7
. The proper part of the survey concludes with Section
8
, a brief enumeration of other relevant topics that we do not discuss at length due to space constraints. Lastly, the appendices at the end summarize the notation used throughout, the basics of Hardy fields, and the rudiments of the Host-Kra theory.

The area is still full of open problems, and we strive to present them consistently throughout the survey. For more open questions on multiple ergodic averages, we advise the reader to consult the excellent survey of Frantzikinakis
[
56
]
(as well as his website, which lists up-to-date progress on those problems) and an older survey of Bergelson
[
13
]
. A gentle introduction to the study of multiple ergodic averages can be found in surveys of Kra
[
109
]
and Host
[
86
]
.
For a thorough summary of nilsystems, Host-Kra theory, and their applications, the textbook of Host and Kra remains the ultimate reference
[
90
]
. Lastly, recent breakthroughs on joint ergodicity would not be possible without fruitful interchange of ideas with additive combinatorics. The state of the art in the latter field is brilliantly summarized in the recent survey of Peluse
[
131
]
.

1.5.
Acknowledgments

This survey benefited greatly from the collective wisdom and generosity of the ergodic theory community. I am particularly grateful to Andreas Koutsogiannis, Konstantinos Tsinas, and Nikos Frantzikinakis for thorough and thoughtful feedback on the earlier drafts of this survey. I would also like to thank Bryna Kra, Wenbo Sun, Sebastián Donoso, Joel Moreira, Florian Richter, and Vitaly Bergelson for suggestions, corrections, and clarifications.

2.
Joint ergodicity criteria

Joint ergodicity is a pleasant and useful thing to have - but how to obtain it? A turning point in the study of joint ergodicity (and related properties from Definitions
1.12
and
1.14
) came in 2021-2022, with the advent of new joint ergodicity criteria summarized in Theorem
2.8
below as well as novel ways of obtaining seminorm control for ergodic averages. This section aims to introduce these new joint ergodicity criteria and juxtapose them with earlier methods.

2.1.
Key definitions

We begin with several definitions that will play a prominent role in the forthcoming discussion. Before perusing the material below, we invite the reader to familiarize themselves with the background material in Appendix
B
on the Host-Kra theory, nonergodic eigenfunctions, and nilsystems.

Definition 2.1

(Seminorm control)
.

We say that sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\colon{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
:

(i)

admit degree-
s
Host-Kra seminorm control for the system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
if for all
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have

(16)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{a_{\ell}(n)}f_{\ell}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0

whenever
\lvert\!|\!|f_{j}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{j}}=0
for some
1\leq j\leq\ell
;

(ii)

admit Host-Kra seminorm control for the system

(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
if they admit degree-
s
control for some
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
;

(iii)

admit (degree-
s
) Host-Kra seminorm control
if they admit it for every system.

In fact, our applications require something weaker than (degree-
s
) Host-Kra seminorm control in the sense given by Definition
2.1
: it suffices to assume that (
16
) holds as long as
\lvert\!|\!|f_{m}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{m}}=0
whenever
f_{m+1},\ldots,f_{\ell}
are
nonergodic eigenfunctions
of
T_{m+1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
respectively (for all choices of
1\leq m\leq\ell
). This weaker property is stated in Definition
5.2
when we outline the proof of Theorem
2.9
.

In existing works (e.g.
[
59
,
69
]
), sequences admitting Host-Kra seminorm control were also called
good for seminorm estimates/control
.

Definition 2.2

(Equidistribution on nilsystems)
.

We say that sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\colon{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}

equidistribute on the nilsystem
(Y,{\mathcal{Y}},m,S_{1},\ldots,S_{\ell})
if for
m
-a.e.
y\in Y
, we have

\displaystyle\overline{\left\{(S_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}y,\ldots,S_{\ell}^{a_{\ell}(n)}y)\colon n\in{\mathbb{N}}\right\}}=\overline{\left\{(S_{1}^{n_{1}}y,\ldots,S_{\ell}^{n_{\ell}}y)\colon n_{1},\ldots,n_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{N}}\right\}};

in particular, the right-hand side equals
Y^{\ell}
whenever
S_{1},\ldots,S_{\ell}
are all ergodic.

Definition 2.3

(Equidistribution and irrational equidistribution)
.

We say that sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\colon{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
are

(i)

good for equidistribution for the system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
if for all nonergodic eigenfunctions
\chi_{1}\in{\mathcal{E}}(T_{1}),\ldots,\chi_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{E}}(T_{\ell})
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}T_{j}^{a_{j}(n)}\chi_{j}-\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}\E(\chi_{j}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{j}))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0;

(ii)

good for equidistribution
if they are good for equidistribution for every system;

(iii)

good for irrational equidistribution for the system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
if for all nonergodic eigenfunctions
\chi_{1}\in{\mathcal{E}}(T_{1}),\ldots,\chi_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{E}}(T_{\ell})
, we have

(17)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}T_{j}^{a_{j}(n)}\chi_{j}-\E_{n\in[N]}\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}T_{j}^{a_{j}(n)}\E(\chi_{j}|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T_{j}))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0;

(iv)

good for irrational equidistribution
if they are good for irrational equidistribution for every system.

The necessity to deal with nonergodic eigenfunctions rather than the classical ones comes from the fact that nonergodic eigenfunctions span
{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}
-systems for nonergodic transformations (see Appendix
B.3
).

A recurrent theme in this survey is an interplay between
local
vs.
global
assumptions and results: those that hold for a particular system vs. those holding for every system from some large class (ergodic, single-transformation, etc.). In many instances, the global assumptions/results take simpler form than the local ones. As an example, we restate below the two global criteria from Definition
2.3
in terms of exponential sums; for the rather straightforward proofs, see
[
69
, Lemma 2.4]
.

Lemma 2.4

(Equivalent formulation of global equidistribution properties)
.

The sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\colon{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
are:

(i)

good for equidistribution if and only if

\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}\,e(a_{1}(n)\beta_{1}+\cdots+a_{\ell}(n)\beta_{\ell})=0

for
\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{\ell}\in[0,1)
not all zero;

(ii)

good for irrational equidistribution if and only if

\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}\,e(a_{1}(n)\beta_{1}+\cdots+a_{\ell}(n)\beta_{\ell})=0

for
\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{\ell}\in[0,1)
not all rational.

Example 2.5
.

For noninteger
0<b_{1}<\cdots<b_{\ell}
, sequences
n^{b_{1}},\ldots,n^{b_{\ell}}
are good for equidistribution, whereas any affinely independent polynomials in
{\mathbb{Z}}[t]
(e.g.
n,n^{2},\ldots,n^{\ell}
) are good for irrational equidistribution.

Likewise, for systems in which all transformations are ergodic, the two local criteria in Definition
2.3
can be restated in terms of eigenvalues.

Lemma 2.6

(Equidistribution properties for ergodic systems)
.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system with
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
ergodic. The sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\colon{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
are:

(i)

good for equidistribution for the system if and only if

\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}\,e(a_{1}(n)\beta_{1}+\cdots+a_{\ell}(n)\beta_{\ell})=0

for all
\beta_{1}\in\Spec(T_{1}),\ldots,\beta_{\ell}\in\Spec(T_{\ell})
, not all of which are zero;

(ii)

good for irrational equidistribution if and only if

\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}\,e(a_{1}(n)\beta_{1}+\cdots+a_{\ell}(n)\beta_{\ell})=0

for all
\beta_{1}\in\Spec(T_{1}),\ldots,\beta_{\ell}\in\Spec(T_{\ell})
, not all of which are rational.

When
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
are nonergodic, the property of being good for equidistribution cannot be reduced to the exponential sums in Lemma
2.6
, as demonstrated by the following example.

Example 2.7
.

Let
X={\mathbb{T}}^{2}
be endowed with the Borel
\sigma
-algebra and Lebesgue measure, and let

\displaystyle T_{1}(x,y):=(x,y+x)\quad\textrm{and}\quad T_{2}(x,y)=(x,y+x+\beta)

for some
\beta\in{\mathbb{T}}
.
It is easy to verify that
T_{1},T_{2}
have trivial spectrum, and so the exponential sums conditions from Lemma
2.6
are satisfied for trivial reasons by any sequences
a_{1},a_{2}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
.
Yet
T_{1},T_{2}
both generate (nonergodic)
{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}
-systems, and every function
\chi(x,y)=e(kx+ly)
(for
k,l\in{\mathbb{Z}}
) is a nonergodic eigenfunction of both transformations. Hence to verify the good for equidistribution property for
a_{1},a_{2}
and
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},T_{2})
, we need to check whether

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}e((ka_{1}(n)+la_{2}(n))x+la_{2}(n)\beta)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0

for all
k,l\in{\mathbb{Z}}
not both 0. This fails even when
a_{1}(n)=a_{2}(n)=n
,
\beta=\frac{1}{2}
, and
k=-l\in 2{\mathbb{Z}}
.

In the single-transformation case, we make the following convention.

Convention 2
.

We say that any property defined for
\ell
transformations
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
holds for a system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
if it holds with
T_{1}=\cdots=T_{\ell}=T
.

2.2.
Old Joint Ergodicity Strategy

With the definitions out of the way, we are ready to present the main strategies to prove joint ergodicity.
Suppose that we want to establish the joint ergodicity of sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
for a system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
. Up to the 2021-2022 breakthroughs, we would have proceeded as follows.

Old Joint Ergodicity Strategy:

(i)

(Host-Kra seminorm control) Show that the sequences admit Host-Kra seminorm control for the system.

(ii)

(Reduction to nilsystems) Use the Host-Kra structure theorem (Theorem
1.7
) to reduce the problem to nilsystems, i.e. derive the identity (
12
) for all nilsystems
(Y,{\mathcal{Y}},m,S_{1},\ldots,S_{\ell})
that arise as factors of
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
.

(iii)

(Equidistribution on nilsystems) Show that the sequences equidistribute on all nilsystems as above.

In particular, if the transformations
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
are totally ergodic, as is the case in some applications (see e.g. Theorem
3.8
), the same holds for the nilrotations
S_{1},\ldots,S_{\ell}
.

The first step in the Old Joint Ergodicity Strategy is primarily analytic and usually involves some variant of the PET induction scheme of Bergelson
[
12
]
. The details are presented in Section
5.2.1
. Here, we just remark that it consists of applying the van der Corput inequality, change of variables, and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality to the average under consideration many times until reaching an average in which all terms are linear in
n
. Each application reduces the average to one of lower “complexity” (in some suitable sense), and the careful measure of complexity ensures that one needs only finitely many steps to reach a linear average. At the end, we invoke the seminorm estimates for linear averages of Host-Kra (
3
) or Host (
7
), themselves products of several applications of the van der Corput and Cauchy-Schwarz inequalities.

The second step in the Old Joint Ergodicity Strategy is a straightforward application of Theorem
1.7
jointly with an
L^{2}(\mu)
approximation argument and the martingale convergence theorem.
4
4

4

If
{\mathcal{Y}}=\bigvee_{j=1}^{\infty}{\mathcal{Y}}_{j}
for some increasing sequence of
\sigma
-algebras
{\mathcal{Y}}_{1}\subseteq{\mathcal{Y}}_{2}\subseteq\cdots
, then

\lim\limits_{j\to\infty}\left\|\E(f|{\mathcal{Y}}_{j})-\E(f|{\mathcal{Y}})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0.

The third step is considerably more challenging: obtaining a required equidistribution result on nilsystems involves a lot of nasty computations that restrict the classes of sequences tractable using this method (see e.g. the discussion below Theorem
3.33
).

2.3.
New Joint Ergodicity Strategy

The hurdles faced by the seekers of joint ergodicity simplified dramatically in 2021-2022, with the advent of new joint ergodicity criteria.

Theorem 2.8

(Joint ergodicity criteria
[
59
, Theorem 1.1]
,
[
69
, Theorem 2.5]
)
.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system with
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
ergodic. The sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\colon{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
are jointly ergodic for the system if and only if the following conditions hold:

(i)

the sequences admit Host-Kra seminorm control for the system;

(ii)

the sequences are good for equidistribution for the system.

In other words, Theorem
2.8
combined with Lemma
2.6
reduces the arduous step (iii) of the Old Joint Ergodicity Strategy to a much simpler problem involving exponential sums.

Without assuming ergodicity, Theorem
2.8
takes the following stronger form.

Theorem 2.9

(Criteria for invariant factor control
[
69
, Theorem 2.5]
)
.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system. The sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\colon{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
are controlled by the invariant factor for the system if and only if the following conditions hold:

(i)

the sequences admit Host-Kra seminorm control for the system;

(ii)

the sequences are good for equidistribution for the system.

It is clear that being controlled by the invariant factor implies both Host-Kra seminorm control and being good for equidistribution. Indeed, since
{\mathcal{I}}(T_{j})={\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(T_{j})
, the lowest-degree factor in the Host-Kra hierarchy, we have via (
69
) that being controlled by the invariant factor is equivalent to degree-1 Host-Kra seminorm control. Furthermore, by taking the functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}
in (
12
) to be nonergodic eigenfunctions, we immediately infer being good for equidistribution from joint ergodicity. The other direction is the deep and nontrivial one; we will sketch its proof in Section
5.1
.

If the end goal is to obtain control by the rational Kronecker factor, we can use the following variant of the aforementioned results.

Theorem 2.10

(Criteria for rational Kronecker factor control
[
59
, Theorem 1.6]
,
[
69
, Theorem 2.6]
)
.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system. The sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\colon{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
are controlled by the rational Kronecker factor for the system if and only if the following conditions hold:

(i)

the sequences admit Host-Kra seminorm control for the system;

(ii)

the sequences are good for irrational equidistribution for the system;

(iii)

the equation (
17
) holds when
f_{j}
is an eigenfunction of
T_{j}
with rational eigenvalue for all but one value
1\leq j\leq\ell
.
5
5

5

This last condition, absent in Theorems
2.8
and
2.9
, is needed in the base case of the proof of the difficult direction of this result. In reality, it is easily satisfied for sequences of interest, e.g. for all sequences good for irrational equidistribution
[
69
, Lemma 5.3]
.

Once again, rational Kronecker factor control easily implies the other two conditions while the converse direction is difficult.

The previous three theorems yield the following global corollaries.

Corollary 2.11

(Global joint ergodicity criteria
[
69
, Theorem 2.1]
)
.

The sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\colon{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
are jointly ergodic if and only if the following conditions hold:

(i)

the sequences admit Host-Kra seminorm control for all systems
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
with
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
ergodic;

(ii)

the sequences are good for equidistribution.

Corollary 2.12

(Criteria for global invariant/rational Kronecker factor control
[
69
, Theorem 2.2]
)
.

The sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\colon{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
are controlled by the invariant/rational Kronecker factor respectively if and only if the following conditions hold:

(i)

the sequences admit Host-Kra seminorm control;

(ii)

the sequences are good for equidistribution/irrational equidistribution respectively.

The proofs of all of the results above rely on the
degree lowering
method, an extremely versatile analytic argument developed by Peluse
[
129
]
and later refined by Peluse-Prendiville
[
132
]
to obtain quantitative versions of the polynomial Szemerédi theorem in the finitary universe. Frantzikinakis then realized its potential value in ergodic theory. Translating the arguments from
[
132
]
into the language of dynamics, he proved the variants of Theorem
2.8
-Corollary
2.12
in the case of a single transformation
T_{1}=\cdots=T_{\ell}

[
59
]
. The aforementioned results have then been extended to the case of
{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}
-systems by Frantzikinakis and the author
[
69
]
. The argument for the single-transformation case uses the ergodic decomposition to reduce matters to the ergodic system. This is no longer possible for several commuting transformations, as there is no variant of ergodic decomposition that would make them simultaneously ergodic.
6
6

6

Consider
X={\mathbb{T}}^{2}
,
T_{1}(x,y)=(x+\sqrt{2},y)
,
T_{2}(x,y)=(x,y+\sqrt{3})
. Then the ergodic decompositions of the Lebesgue measure on
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
with respect to
T_{1},T_{2}
are different.

The lack of ergodicity was the main challenge in extending the results from
[
59
]
to
[
69
]
, and it necessitated the development of several technical results of independent interest.

With these criteria in mind, the surest path towards proving joint ergodicity/invariant factor control/rational Kronecker factor control of sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
for a system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
takes the following form nowadays.

New Joint Ergodicity Strategy:

(i)

(Host-Kra seminorm control) Show that the sequences admit Host-Kra seminorm control for the system.

(ii)

(Reduction to an equidistribution problem) Use the relevant one of Theorems
2.8
,
2.9
, or
2.10
to reduce to an equidistribution problem.

(iii)

(Equidistribution on nilsystems) Show that the sequences are good for (irrational) equidistribution for the system.

The crux of the matter is then to obtain Host-Kra seminorm control over the averages of interest, as the relevant equidistribution results are often either known or easy to derive from existing ones. This problem is already rather challenging, and up to 2022, Host-Kra seminorm control was only known in two families of examples:

(i)

in the single-transformation case, assuming some form of “essential distinctness” of sequences;

(ii)

in the commuting case, assuming that sequences have sufficiently “distinct growth”.

The second big breakthrough, in addition to the joint ergodicity criteria, was the development of new tools for Host-Kra seminorm estimates. These new techniques are presented at length in Section
5.2
, and the specific results on Host-Kra seminorm control proved this way are discussed all over the survey.

Given the difficulties inherent in establishing Host-Kra seminorm control, it is tempting to ask whether the seminorm control conditions can be removed altogether.

Problem 1

(Simple global joint ergodicity criterion
[
59
, Problem 1]
)
.

Prove or disprove: sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
are jointly ergodic if and only if they are good for equidistribution.

The version of Problem
1
for a fixed system admits a negative answer even for
\ell=1
, as demonstrated by the example below.

Example 2.13

(Failure of simple local joint ergodicity criterion)
.

Consider the sequence
a(n)={\lfloor\log_{2}n\rfloor}
. As shown in
[
44
]
, this sequence is ergodic on
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
if and only if the system is conjugate to a 1-point system, or equivalently
L^{\infty}(\mu)
consists of constants. Indeed, if

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T^{{\lfloor\log_{2}n\rfloor}}f-\int f\;d\mu\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0

then also

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[2^{N},2^{N+1})}T^{{\lfloor\log_{2}n\rfloor}}f-\int f\;d\mu\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0.

Since
{\lfloor\log_{2}n\rfloor}=N
for all
n\in[2^{N},2^{N+1})
, the latter convergence is equivalent to

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|T^{N}f-\int f\;d\mu\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0.

Measure invariance then implies that
\left\|f-\int f\;d\mu\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0
, which can only happen if
f
is constant. This shows that ergodicity of
a
imposes the claimed restriction on the system, and the converse direction is trivial. At the same time,
a
is good for equidistribution for any weakly mixing systems as those do not have any nontrivial eigenvalues. It follows that
a
is good for equidistribution but not ergodic on weakly mixing systems that are not conjugate to a 1-point system.

Lastly, we record an important corollary of Theorem
2.8
for nilsystems.

Corollary 2.14

(Joint ergodicity criteria for nilsystems
[
59
, Corollary 1.4]
)
.

The sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\colon{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
are jointly ergodic for all nilsystems
(Y,{\mathcal{Y}},\nu,S_{1},\ldots,S_{\ell})
with
S_{1},\ldots,S_{\ell}
ergodic if and only if they are good for equidistribution.

The key point about nilsystems is that an
s
-step nilsystem is a
{\mathcal{Z}}_{s}
-system, meaning that
{\mathcal{Z}}_{s}
equals the entire Borel
\sigma
-algebra. Therefore the Host-Kra seminorm of degree
s+1
is a norm, and any family of sequences admits Host-Kra seminorm control on such a system for trivial reasons.

Importantly, joint ergodicity is about
norm
convergence, while being good for equidistribution is about
pointwise
convergence on
{\mathbb{T}}
. The next problem, mentioned in passing below
[
59
, Corollary 1.4]
, inquires whether Corollary
2.14
can be strengthened to a purely pointwise joint ergodicity condition for nilsystems.

Problem 2

(Pointwise joint ergodicity criteria for nilsystems)
.

Suppose that the sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\colon{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
are good for equidistribution.
Is it true that for every nilsystem
(Y,{\mathcal{Y}},\nu,S_{1},\ldots,S_{\ell})
with
S_{1},\ldots,S_{\ell}

ergodic, the sequence

\displaystyle(S_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}y,\ldots,S_{\ell}^{a_{\ell}(n)}y)_{n}

is equidistributed on
Y^{\ell}
for
\mu
-a.e.
y\in Y
?

3.
Joint ergodicity for large classes of sequences and systems

This section is dedicated to joint ergodicity results and open problems for specific families of iterates: polynomials, Hardy sequences, sequences involving primes, generalized polynomials, tempered functions, and functions of superpolynomial growth or with oscillations. Most results and questions presented in this section can be categorized into one of the two following heuristics.

Heuristic 3.1

(Heuristic for joint ergodicity for pairwise independent sequences)
.

Any reasonable “pairwise independent” sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
are jointly ergodic for any system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
with
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
weakly mixing.

Heuristic 3.2

(Heuristic for joint ergodicity for independent sequences)
.

Any reasonable “independent” sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
are jointly ergodic for any system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu
,
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
with
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
(totally) ergodic.

We shall not offer much justification on which sequences we deem reasonable other than the utilitarian one: these are the well-behaved sequences for which we can prove nice results. At the very least, they should be amenable to usual analytic tools employed to study distribution mod 1, ergodicity, and single recurrence. They should also have a stable limiting behavior: ideally converge or go to infinity, but neither too slowly nor too fast.

By contrast, we put considerable effort in this section to pin down the notions of “independence” and “pairwise independence”.
Their precise meaning depends on the context; for instance, it takes a different shape for integer polynomials vs. Hardy sequences.
Before we fill these two notions of independence with substance, let us ponder on the origin of the conditions in Heuristics
3.1
and
3.2
. In the light of Theorem
2.8
, joint ergodicity is equivalent to Host-Kra seminorm control and good equidistribution properties. Weakly mixing transformations admit no nonergodic eigenfunctions, so for such systems all sequences are trivially good for equidistribution. Even more pointedly, the Host-Kra seminorms trivialize for a weakly mixing system
T
in that
\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T}=\mathopen{}\left|\int f\;d\mu\mathclose{}\right|
for all
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
, in which case Host-Kra seminorm control is tantamount to joint ergodicity. Therefore, an appropriate notion of “pairwise independence” should capture the requirements for Host-Kra seminorm control.
This point is important enough to formalize it in a separate heuristic.

Heuristic 3.3

(Heuristic for seminorm control for pairwise independent sequences)
.

Any reasonable “pairwise independent” sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
admit Host-Kra seminorm control.

If our sequences satisfy the stronger conditions for “independence” rather than “pairwise independence”, then we still expect them to admit Host-Kra seminorm control according to Heuristic
3.3
. Additionally, the needed notion of “independence” should be strong enough to imply being good for (rational) equidistribution, making the sequences good for equidistribution for any (totally) ergodic system.

3.1.
Integer polynomials

3.1.1.
Single transformation

The first result that can be viewed as a joint ergodicity statement is the following result of Furstenberg, which appeared in his foundational work on multiple ergodic averages.

Theorem 3.4

(
[
72
]
)
.

For every
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the sequences
n,2n,\ldots,\ell n
are jointly ergodic for any weakly mixing system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
.

A decade later, Bergelson extended Theorem
3.4
to all
essentially distinct
polynomials
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[t]
, i.e. those for which
a_{j},a_{i}-a_{j}
are nonconstant for every distinct
1\leq i,j\leq\ell
.

Theorem 3.5

(
[
12
]
)
.

Any essentially distinct polynomials
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[t]
are jointly ergodic for any weakly mixing system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
.

Theorem
3.5
exemplifies Heuristic
3.1
, showing that in the single-transformation case, the right notion of “pairwise independence” of integer polynomials is essential distinctness. The paper
[
12
]
was written almost two decades before the arrival of Host-Kra seminorms, and so these notions could not have possibly been used in the proof. However, the proof introduces the PET induction scheme that we use to establish Host-Kra seminorm control nowadays. Indeed, we have the following equivalence.

Theorem 3.6

(Essential distinctness vs. Host-Kra seminorm control)
.

The polynomials
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[t]
are essentially distinct if and only if they admit Host-Kra seminorm control for any system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
.

Example 3.7

(Necessity of essential distinctness)
.

To see the necessity of essential distinctness for Host-Kra seminorm control, consider the average

\displaystyle\E_{n\in[N]}T^{n}f_{1}\cdot T^{n+1}f_{2};

suppose also without loss of generality that
T
is ergodic.
By the mean ergodic theorem, it converges in norm to
\int f_{1}\cdot Tf_{2}\;d\mu
. Taking
f_{1}=Tf
,
f_{2}=\overline{f}
for some
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
we see that the limit is
\int|f|^{2}\;d\mu
. Hence the average converges to 0 if and only if
f=0
, which is a much stronger condition than
\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T}=0
for some
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

Just like Theorem
3.5
instantiates Heuristic
3.1
, the following result of Frantzikinakis and Kra epitomizes Heuristic
3.2
. We call
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[t]

affinely independent
if for any
c_{1},\ldots,c_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, the polynomial
c_{1}a_{1}+\cdots+c_{\ell}a_{\ell}
is constant unless
c_{1}=\cdots=c_{\ell}=0
.

Theorem 3.8

(
[
64
, Theorem 1.1]
)
.

Any affinely independent polynomials
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[t]
are jointly ergodic for any totally ergodic system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
.

For
n,n^{2}
, Theorem
3.8
has previously been proved by Furstenberg and Weiss
[
75
]
.
Theorem
3.8
demonstrates, for instance, that for a totally ergodic
T
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T^{n}f_{1}\cdots T^{n^{\ell}}f_{\ell}-\int f_{1}\;d\mu\cdots\int f_{\ell}\;d\mu\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0.

It thus shows that affine independence is the right notion of independence for integer polynomials.

Example 3.9

(Necessity of affine independence)
.

To see why affine independence is needed in Theorem
3.8
, take any
a\in{\mathbb{Z}}[t]
(or, indeed, any sequence
a:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
). Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
be a group rotation by some element
\beta\in X
. Then
a(n),2a(n)
are not jointly ergodic for
T
. Indeed, by taking
f_{1}(x)=\chi(2x)
,
f_{2}(x)=\chi(-x)
for any nontrivial character (i.e. continuous group homomorphism)
\chi:X\to\mathbb{S}^{1}
, we see that

\displaystyle f_{1}(T^{a(n)}x)\cdot f_{2}(T^{a(n)}x)=\chi(2(x+\beta a(n)))\cdot\chi(-(x+\beta\cdot 2a(n)))=\chi(x)

for every
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
and
x\in X
.
In particular,
\chi(x)
is the pointwise limit of the corresponding multiple ergodic average, and it is very much not equal to
\int f_{1}\;d\mu\cdot\int f_{2}\;d\mu=0
. This example shows how the absence of affine independence prevents joint ergodicity on any system with a nontrivial Kronecker factor.

Total ergodicity is needed to prevent local obstructions; see Example
1.3
. Without this assumption, Frantzikinakis and Kra obtain the following more general result.

Theorem 3.10

(
[
65
, Theorem 1.1]
)
.

Let polynomials
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[t]
be affinely independent. Then they are controlled by the rational Kronecker factor for every system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
.

The proof of Theorems
3.8
and
3.10
is a quintessential example of the Old Joint Ergodicity Strategy. Reducing to nilsystems via Theorem
3.6
and the Host-Kra structure theorem, the authors spend the bulk of their work establishing the following equidistribution result on nilsystems from which Theorem
3.8
follows.

Theorem 3.11

(Equidistribution of affinely independent polynomials on totally ergodic nilsystems
[
64
, Theorem 1.2]
)
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[t]
be affinely independent and
(Y,{\mathcal{Y}},m,S)
be a totally ergodic nilsystem. Then for
m
-a.e.
y\in Y
, we have

\displaystyle\overline{\{(S^{a_{1}(n)}y,\ldots S^{a_{\ell}(n)}y)\colon n\in{\mathbb{N}}\}}=Y^{\ell}.

3.1.2.
Commuting transformations

Up to a few years ago, very little was known on the structure of multiple ergodic averages of commuting transformations along polynomials except for their norm convergence
[
143
]
. One of the few results in this direction was the following joint ergodicity result
of Chu, Frantzikinakis, and Host.

Theorem 3.12

(
[
38
, Theorem 1.2]
)
.

Any nonconstant polynomials
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[t]
of distinct degrees admit Host-Kra seminorm control. In particular, they are jointly ergodic for any system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
with
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
weakly mixing.

The restriction to distinct-degree polynomials comes from the fact that this is the only class of polynomials for which multiple ergodic averages of commuting transformations could be controlled by Host-Kra seminorms using purely the PET induction scheme. Indeed, the proof of Theorem
3.12
breaks even for the average along
n^{2},n^{2}+n
. Furthermore, difficulties inherent in studying equidistribution on nonergodic nilsystems prevented Chu, Frantzikinakis, and Host from deducing joint ergodicity for totally ergodic transformations.
Further progress on these questions was stalled until the work of Frantzikinakis and the author, in which the ensuing results have been proved.

Theorem 3.13

(
[
69
, Theorem 2.9]
)
.

Any affinely independent polynomials
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[t]
are controlled by the rational Kronecker factor. In particular, they are jointly ergodic for any system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
with
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
totally ergodic.

Example 3.14
.

The simplest case covered by Theorem
3.13
that remained out of reach of previous methods was that for any system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},T_{2})
with
T_{1},T_{2}
totally ergodic, the sequences
n^{2},n^{2}+n
are jointly ergodic, i.e.

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{n^{2}}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{n^{2}+n}f_{2}-\int f_{1}\;d\mu\cdot\int f_{2}\;d\mu\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0

for any
f_{1},f_{2}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
. This was not even known under the much stronger assumption that
T_{1},T_{2}
are weakly mixing.

Theorem
3.13
concretizes Heuristic
3.2
for integer polynomials and commuting transformations. It shows that the correct notion of “independence” for integer polynomials is “affine independence”, just like it was the case for a single transformation. This stands in contrast to “pairwise independence”: while for a single transformation this notion took the form of essential distinctness, for commuting transformations we need our polynomials
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[t]
to be
pairwise independent
, in the sense that for all distinct
1\leq i,j\leq\ell
and
c_{i},c_{j}\in{\mathbb{Q}}
, the polynomial
c_{i}a_{i}+c_{j}a_{j}
is nonconstant unless
c_{i}=c_{j}=0
.

Theorem 3.15

(
[
69
, Theorem 2.8 and Corollary 2.9]
)
.

Any pairwise independent polynomials
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[t]
:

(i)

admit Host-Kra seminorm control;

(ii)

are jointly ergodic for any system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
with
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
weakly mixing.

The new joint ergodicity criteria (and specifically Theorem
2.10
) play a crucial role in deriving Theorem
3.13
from Theorem
3.15
(
i
), as they circumvent the study of distribution of polynomial orbits on nonergodic nilsystems. The second ingredient of at least equal importance are the new techniques developed to get the seminorm control in
3.15
(
i
), and which are discussed at length in Section
5.2
.

3.2.
Hardy sequences

Integer polynomials form arguably the most natural class of sparse sequences that arise in the study of multiple ergodic averages. Yet they are but a small class of a much broader family of
Hardy sequences of polynomial growth
that include sequences like

\displaystyle\sqrt{2}n^{2}+\sqrt{3}n,n^{3/2},n\log n,\exp(\sqrt{\log n}),1/n.

For precise definitions of Hardy sequences, we refer the reader to Appendix
C
. Here, we just recall that by
{\mathcal{H}}
we denote the collection of all Hardy functions of polynomial growth lying in some fixed Hardy field that contains
{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{E}}
, all logarithmico-exponential functions, and enjoys a bunch of other nice properties (it is closed under compositions, compositional inverses, and variable shifts).
7
7

7

Particular results stated in this survey may use only some of these properties, and hence they may hold for more general Hardy fields. However, since we prioritize brevity and ease of exposition over generality, we leave it to the interested reader to consult the relevant literature for the weakest possible condition on
{\mathcal{H}}
required in the particular context.

The study of Hardy sequences in ergodic theory has a long history, dating back to work of Boshernitzan
[
33
]
. What makes Hardy sequences reasonably easy to work with are their analytic properties. As long as a function
a\in{\mathcal{H}}
grows faster than
\log
, its derivative
a^{\prime}(t)
grows like
a(t)/t
(up to a multiplicative factor).
As a consequence, we can Taylor expand
a
on short intervals as polynomials with well-controlled error terms and explicitly given coefficients.

Example 3.16

(Taylor expansion of Hardy sequences on short intervals)
.

Let
a(t)=t^{3/2}
. If we choose a function
L\in{\mathcal{H}}
satisfying
N^{1/4}\prec L(N)\prec N^{1/2}
, then we can expand
a(t)
on the interval
(N,N+L(N)]
as

\displaystyle(N+n)^{3/2}=N^{3/2}+\frac{3}{2}N^{1/2}n+\frac{3}{8N^{1/2}}n^{2}-\frac{1}{16\xi_{N,n}^{3/2}}n^{3}

for every
n\in[L(N)]
and some
\xi_{N,n}\in(N,N+L(N)]
. The admissible range for
L
was chosen so that the error term asymptotically vanish while the leading quadratic term does not, in the sense that

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\max_{n\in[L(N)]}\frac{1}{16\xi_{N,n}^{3/2}}n^{3}=0\quad\textrm{and}\quad\lim_{N\to\infty}\max_{n\in[L(N)]}\frac{3}{8N^{1/2}}n^{2}=\infty.

Hence on every interval
(N,N+L(N)]
,
a
behaves like a quadratic polynomial. If instead we choose
L
in the range
N^{1-3/(2d)}\prec L(N)\prec N^{1-3/(2(d+1))}
, then the Taylor approximant for
a
will be a degree-
d
polynomial.

The fact that on different short intervals we get different approximating polynomials (what some works
[
56
,
71
,
106
]
call
variable polynomials
) means that we often need to work with finite averages
\E_{n\in[N]}
, only taking the limit
N\to\infty
towards the very end of the argument. This necessitates more quantitative arguments than one would use for polynomials.

The reduction of Hardy sequences to polynomials on short intervals means that any progress on multiple ergodic averages along polynomials is likely to open new paths in the study of Hardy sequences. Not surprisingly, the results presented in this section historically followed analogous developments for integer polynomials.

3.2.1.
Classification of Hardy sequences

Before stating joint ergodicity results for Hardy sequences, we want to provide some intuition for various conditions that we will see later. A good starting point that motivates forthcoming definitions is the equidistribution result of Boshernitzan.

Theorem 3.17

(Boshernitzan’s equidistribution result
[
33
, Theorem 1.3]
)
.

Let
a\in\mathcal{H}
. The sequence
(a(n))_{n}
is equidistributed
\mod~\!1
if and only if for every
p\in{\mathbb{Q}}[t]
, we have

(18)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{|a(t)-p(t)|}{\log t}=\infty.

If
a
satisfies (
18
), we say that it
stays away from rational polynomials
.

In what follows, we will work with the following families of Hardy sequences. We caution the reader that existing research works may name them differently.

Definition 3.18

(Independence properties of Hardy sequences)
.

We call
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{H}}
:

(i)

essentially distinct
if for all distinct
1\leq i,j\leq\ell
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{|a_{j}(t)|}{\log t}=\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{|a_{i}(t)-a_{j}(t)|}{\log t}=\infty;

(ii)

pairwise independent
if for all distinct
1\leq i,j\leq\ell
and
c_{i},c_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}
not both zero, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{|c_{i}a_{i}(t)+c_{j}a_{j}(t)|}{\log t}=\infty;

(iii)

strongly independent
if every nontrivial linear combination of
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
stays logarithmically away from rational polynomials, i.e. for all
c_{1},\ldots,c_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{R}}
not all zero and for all
p\in{\mathbb{Q}}[t]
, we have

(19)

\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{|c_{1}a_{1}(t)+\cdots+c_{\ell}a_{\ell}(t)-p(t)|}{\log t}=\infty;

(iv)

strongly irrationally independent
if (
19
) holds for all
c_{1},\ldots,c_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{R}}
not all rational and for all
p\in{\mathbb{Q}}[t]
.

The notions of essential distinctness and pairwise independence generalize the notions of essential distinctness and pairwise affine independence for integer polynomials. By contrast, strong independence and strong irrational independence will play a similar role as affine independence for integer polynomials, even though neither of the former two extends the latter.

Like it was the case for integer polynomials, essential distinctness and pairwise independence will be preconditions for Host-Kra seminorm control (and hence for joint ergodicity in weakly mixing systems); strong independence will be necessary for invariant factor control; and strong irrational independence will enable rational Kronecker factor control.

Clearly, strong independence implies strong irrational independence, which in turn implies pairwise independence. The following examples demonstrate the failure of the converse directions:

Example 3.19
.

The sequences
t^{\sqrt{2}},\;t^{\sqrt{2}}+t^{1/2}
are strongly independent; same with
t^{3/2},\;t^{3/2}+t^{1/2}
or any other linearly independent
fractional polynomials

a_{j}(t)=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{d}\alpha_{ji}t^{b_{i}}
for
noninteger
positive real powers
b_{1},\ldots,b_{d}
.

Example 3.20
.

The sequences
t^{\sqrt{2}},\;t^{\sqrt{2}}+t
are strongly irrationally independent but not strongly independent. More generally, linearly independent fractional polynomials
a_{j}(t)=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{d}\alpha_{ji}t^{b_{i}}
for (possibly integer) positive reals
b_{1},\ldots,b_{d}
and rational coefficients
\alpha_{ji}
will be strongly irrationally independent, but not necessarily strongly independent.

Example 3.21
.

Linearly independent real polynomials are pairwise independent, but they need not even be strongly irrationally independent (consider e.g.
\sqrt{2}t^{2},\;\sqrt{2}t^{2}+\sqrt{3}t
).

3.2.2.
Single transformation

The systematic study of joint ergodicity of Hardy sequences was initiated by Frantzikinakis with the following result.

Theorem 3.22

(
[
53
, Theorem 2.6]
)
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{E}}
satisfy the following two properties:

(i)

a_{1}\prec\cdots\prec a_{\ell}
;

(ii)

for every
1\leq j\leq\ell
there exists
k_{j}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
and
0<\varepsilon_{j}<1
such that
t^{k_{j}+\varepsilon_{j}}\prec a_{j}(t)\prec t^{k_{j}+1}
;

Then they are controlled by the invariant factor for every system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
. In particular, they are jointly ergodic for every ergodic system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
.

We recall Convention
1
that when we speak of joint ergodicity, invariant factor control, etc. for real-valued sequences, then all these properties refer to the integer parts of these sequences.

In particular, Theorem
3.22
implies that the corresponding multiple ergodic average converges in norm. In general, for “nonpolynomial” Hardy sequences, identifying the limit is the surest way of showing norm convergence, as we do not know how to prove it by other means.

Theorem
3.22
thus covers those Hardy sequences that are sufficiently nonpolynomial. The (partially) complimentary case of real polynomials was covered by Karageorgos and Koutsogiannis.

Theorem 3.23

(
[
97
, Theorem 2.1]
)
.

Any strongly independent real polynomials
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}

\in{\mathbb{R}}[t]
are controlled by the invariant factor for every system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
. In particular, they are jointly ergodic for every ergodic system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
.

Further joint ergodicity results for Hardy sequences and a single transformation have been proved by Bergelson, Moreira, and Richter
[
27
]
as well as Frantzikinakis
[
59
]
; the second of these works was the first in the study of Hardy sequences that relied on the New Joint Ergodicity Strategy. Finally, the problem for a single transformation was effectively resolved by Tsinas
[
142
]
with the following two theorems.

Theorem 3.24

(
[
142
, Theorem 1.2]
)
.

Any strongly independent
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{H}}
are controlled by the invariant factor for every system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
. In particular, they are jointly ergodic for every ergodic system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
.

Theorem
3.24
realizes Heuristic
3.2
for Hardy sequences of polynomial growth and single transformation, just like the result below realizes Heuristic
3.1
.

Theorem 3.25

(
[
142
, Theorem 1.3 and Corollary 1.4]
)
.

Any essentially distinct
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{H}}
:

(i)

admit Host-Kra seminorm control;

(ii)

are jointly ergodic for any system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
with
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
weakly mixing.

3.2.3.
Commuting transformations

Before the breakthroughs in the last few years, joint ergodicity of Hardy sequences for commuting transformations was only known for sequences of sufficiently distinct growth, in analogy with the result of Chu-Frantzikinakis-Host for polynomials (Theorem
3.12
).

Theorem 3.26

(
[
53
, Theorem 2.2]
)
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{H}}
satisfy the following two properties:

(i)

a_{1}\prec\cdots\prec a_{\ell}
;

(ii)

for every
1\leq j\leq\ell
there exists
k_{j}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
such that
t^{k_{j}}\log t\prec a_{j}(t)\prec t^{k_{j}+1}
;

Then they are controlled by the invariant factor. In particular, they are jointly ergodic for all systems
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
with
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
ergodic.

An important special case of Theorem
3.26
is that for any noninteger
0<b_{1}<\cdots<b_{\ell}
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{{\lfloor n^{b_{1}}\rfloor}}f_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{{\lfloor n^{b_{\ell}}\rfloor}}f_{\ell}-\E(f_{1}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{1}))\cdots\E(f_{\ell}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{\ell}))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0.

Theorem 3.27

(
[
104
, Corollary 1.7]
)
.

Any nonconstant polynomials
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{R}}[t]
of distinct degrees:

(i)

admit Host-Kra seminorm control;

(ii)

are jointly ergodic for any system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
with
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
weakly mixing.

Like with Theorem
3.12
, the problem with extending Theorems
3.26
and
3.27
to sequences of the same growth was the absence of methods for establishing Host-Kra seminorm control. Following the arrival of such techniques for integer polynomials (discussed in Section
5.2
