), similar tools were developed for Hardy sequences. Their upshot are the following three results of Donoso, Koutsogiannis, Sun, Tsinas, and the author, verifying Heuristics
3.2
,
3.3
, and
3.1
for Hardy sequences.

Theorem 3.28

(
[
45
, Theorem 1.4]
)
.

Any strongly independent Hardy sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{H}}
are controlled by the invariant factor. In particular, they are jointly ergodic for all systems
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
with
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
ergodic.

As a special case, Theorem
3.28
gives

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{{\lfloor n^{\sqrt{2}}\rfloor}}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{{\lfloor n^{\sqrt{2}}+n^{1/2}\rfloor}}f_{2}-\E(f_{1}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{1}))\cdot\E(f_{2}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{2}))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0,

a result not known before even for weakly mixing
T_{1},T_{2}
. More generally, it yields joint ergodicity for arbitrary linearly independent fractional polynomials with noninteger powers.

Theorem 3.29

(
[
45
, Theorems 1.3 and 1.16]
)
.

Any pairwise independent Hardy sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{H}}
:

(i)

admit Host-Kra seminorm control;

(ii)

are jointly ergodic for any system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
with
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
weakly mixing.

It is worth emphasizing that the class of pairwise independent Hardy sequences contains the class of pairwise independent integer polynomials. Therefore Theorem
3.29
generalizes Theorem
3.15
.

For strongly irrationally independent Hardy sequences, Theorem
3.28
admits the following variant.

Theorem 3.30

(
[
45
, Theorem 1.6]
)
.

Any strongly irrationally independent Hardy sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{H}}
are controlled by the rational Kronecker factor. In particular, they are jointly ergodic for all systems
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
with
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
totally ergodic.

3.2.4.
Weighted averages

One assumption underlying all the results on Hardy sequences so far is that the sequences must grow faster than
\log
. That is because logarithm is bad both for equidistribution and for mean ergodic theorem. Indeed,

\displaystyle\E_{n\in[N]}e(\alpha\log n)=\E_{n\in[N]}n^{2\pi i\alpha}\sim\frac{N^{2\pi i\alpha}}{1+2\pi i\alpha}=\frac{e(\alpha\log N)}{1+2\pi i\alpha},

from which it is clear that this sequence diverges. Likewise, the average

\displaystyle\E_{n\in[N]}T^{{\left\lfloor\log n\right\rfloor}}f

does not converge in general since for infinitely many
N
, the function
{\left\lfloor\log n\right\rfloor}
equals
{\left\lfloor\log N\right\rfloor}
on intervals
[N,(1+c)N]
for some small
c>0
. These issues can be overcome by studying
weighted averages
. Indeed, if we replace Cesàro average by the logarithmic average, then

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left|\frac{1}{\log N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\frac{e(\alpha\log n)}{n}\mathclose{}\right|=O\mathopen{}\left(\frac{1}{|\alpha|\log N}\mathclose{}\right),

which goes to 0 unless
\alpha=0
. Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{\log N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\frac{1}{n}T^{{\lfloor\log n\rfloor}}f=\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}T^{n}f=\E(f|{\mathcal{I}}(T))

in
L^{2}(\mu)
, i.e. the logarithmic ergodic average with the iterate
{\lfloor\log n\rfloor}
satisfies the conclusion of the mean ergodic theorem. One can see this as a “discrete substitution”; indeed, if we replace a discrete transformation
T
by a flow
R
and averaging by integrating, then the usual substitution rule gives us

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\log N}\int_{0}^{N}\frac{1}{t}f(R^{\log t}x)\;dt=\frac{1}{\log N}\int_{0}^{\log N}f(R^{u}x)\;du,

which is a continuous variant of
\E_{n\in[{\lfloor\log N\rfloor}]}T^{n}f
.

In
[
27
]
, Bergelson, Moreira, and Richter studied a weighted version of multiple ergodic averages along Hardy sequences. Their setup is as follows.
Given a Hardy function
W\in{\mathcal{H}}
satisfying
1\prec W(t)\ll t
, set
w(n):=W(n+1)-W(n)
, and denote

\displaystyle\E_{n\in[N]}^{W}A(n):=\frac{1}{W(n)}\sum_{n=1}^{N}w(n)A(n).

If
W(n)=n
, then
w(n)=1
and we recover the Cesàro average. By contrast, if
W(N)=\log N
, then
w(n)=(1+o(1))\frac{1}{n}
, and so logarithmic averages morally fall into this framework. We can naturally define a variant of joint ergodicity for
W
-weighted averages.

Definition 3.31
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
be sequences and
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system. We say that

(i)

a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are
W
-jointly ergodic
for the system if

(20)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E^{W}_{n\in[N]}w(n)\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}T_{j}^{{a_{j}(n)}}f_{j}-\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}\int f_{j}\,d\mu\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0

holds for all
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
;

(ii)

a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are
controlled by the invariant factor for
W
-averages
for the system if

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E^{W}_{n\in[N]}w(n)\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}T_{j}^{{a_{j}(n)}}f_{j}-\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}\E(f_{j}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{j}))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0

holds for all
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
.

(iii)

a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}

admit Host-Kra seminorm control for
W
-averages
for the system if there exists
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that for all
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
,

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E^{W}_{n\in[N]}w(n)\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}T_{j}^{{a_{j}(n)}}f_{j}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0

holds whenever
\lvert\!|\!|f_{j}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{j}}=0
for some
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

Definition 3.32
.

A sequence
a\in{\mathcal{H}}
is
W
-good
if one of the following holds:

(i)

either
|a(t)-p(t)|\ll 1
for some
p\in{\mathbb{R}}[t]
;

(ii)

or
|a(t)-p(t)|\gg\log W(t)
for all
p\in{\mathbb{R}}[t]
.

Bergelson, Moreira, and Richter proved the following joint ergodicity result for weighted averages.

Theorem 3.33

(
[
27
, Theorem B(ii)]
)
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{H}}
, and suppose that any nontrivial linear combination
b
of elements in

(21)

\displaystyle\{a_{j}^{(k)}:\;1\leq j\leq\ell,\;k\geq 0\}

(where
a^{(k)}
is the
k
-th derivative of
a
) is
W
-good and satisfies

(22)

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}|b(t)-p(t)|=\infty\quad\textrm{for\; every}\quad p\in{\mathbb{Q}}[t].

Then
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are
W
-jointly ergodic for every ergodic system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
.

The proof of Theorem
3.33
in
[
27
]
follows the Old Joint Ergodicity Strategy and rests on two major ingredients: seminorm estimates and an equidistribution-on-nilsystems result of Richter
[
134
]
. It is the latter result that requires all
b
’s of the form (
21
) to be
W
-good and satisfy (
22
). The work
[
27
]
is one of the last papers that deduces joint ergodicity using Old Joint Ergodicity Strategy and employs rather formidable manipulations on nilsystems.

Notably, the joint ergodicity results for Hardy sequences from Sections
3.2.2
and
3.2.3
that use New Joint Ergodicity Strategy make less restrictive assumptions on the sequences. It should therefore be possible to weaken the assumptions in Theorem
3.33
. Additionally, in the light of the results from Section
3.2.3
for commuting transformations, it would be welcome to merge the results for weighted averages of a single transformation with those of unweighted averages of commuting transformations. We propose several problems in this direction.

Problem 3
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{H}}
be
W
-pairwise independent
in that for all distinct
1\leq i,j\leq\ell
and
c_{i},c_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}
not both zero, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{|c_{i}a_{i}(t)+c_{j}a_{j}(t)|}{\log W(t)}=\infty;

Show that
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
admit Host-Kra seminorm control for
W
-averages for any system.

Problem 4
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{H}}
be
W
-strongly independent
in that for all
c_{1},\ldots,c_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{R}}
not all zero and for all
p\in{\mathbb{Q}}[t]
, we have

\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{|c_{1}a_{1}(t)+\cdots+c_{\ell}a_{\ell}(t)-p(t)|}{\log W(t)}=\infty;

Show that
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are controlled by the invariant factor for
W
-averages for any system.

For the sake of Problem
4
, we observe that the joint ergodicity criteria can be rather trivially extend to weighted averages once the definitions are appropriately modified. We refer the reader to
[
69
, Section 2.7]
for a sample of such results.

3.3.
Primes

A lot of multiple recurrence, convergence, and joint ergodicity results can be refined by allowing the averaging parameter
n
to run over primes
{\mathbb{P}}=\{p_{1}<p_{2}<\cdots\}
or shifted primes
{\mathbb{P}}-1
.
8
8

8

In the ergodic literature, the name
shifted primes
almost always refers to the sets
{\mathbb{P}}-1
or
{\mathbb{P}}+1
since these are the only shifts of primes that have
good divisibility property
, i.e. their intersection with
q{\mathbb{Z}}
for every
q\in{\mathbb{N}}
has positive relative density. In particular, these are the only prime shifts that are sets of recurrence.

The underlying idea is to replace the average along primes by the Cesàro average weighed by the von Mangoldt function; indeed, from the prime number theorem, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]\cap{\mathbb{P}}}A(n)-\E_{n\in[N]}\Lambda(n)A(n)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0

for any uniformly bounded
A:{\mathbb{N}}\to L^{\infty}(\mu)
. We then want to use the Gowers uniformity of
\Lambda-1
to compare the latter with
\lim\limits_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}A(n)
. In reality,
\Lambda-1
is not Gowers uniform; we instead need to work with appropriately modified von Mangoldt functions

\Lambda_{w,b}(n):=\frac{\phi(W)}{W}\Lambda(Wn+b),

where
w,b\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
W:=\prod\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\in{\mathbb{P}}\colon p\leq w\end{subarray}}p
is the
w
-th primorial, and
\phi
is the Euler totient function. This modification takes care of the irregularities of distribution of primes modulo small primes. It is then a deep result of Green-Tao
[
81
,
82
,
83
]
that in the limit
w\to\infty
, the functions
\Lambda_{w,b}-1
have vanishing Gowers norms of any degree. These ideas originating in additive combinatorics have been transplanted to ergodic theory by Frantzikinakis-Host-Kra
[
60
,
61
]
, Wooley-Ziegler
[
145
]
, Bergelson-Leibman-Ziegler
[
25
]
, Koutsogiannis
[
103
]
, and Koutsogiannis-Karageorgos
[
97
]
to prove the polynomial Szemerédi theorem along shifted primes.

The study of averages along primes then breaks down into two steps:

(i)

the uniformity estimate

(23)

\displaystyle\lim_{w\to\infty}\lim_{N\to\infty}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in[W]\colon\\
\gcd(b,W)=1\end{subarray}}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}(\Lambda_{w,b}(n)-1)A(Wn+b)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0;

(ii)

the examination of

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}A(Wn+b)

for
w,b\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

There are two renditions of this general strategy, depending on the purpose and properties of the sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
:

(i)

if the sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are good for irrational equidistribution and have good divisibility property (or satisfy some variant of these two properties), and we look for new multiple recurrence results, then we study the averages with
n
restricted to
{\mathbb{P}}-1
(or
{\mathbb{P}}+1
) since the restrictions of
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
to this set typically inherit the claimed properties;

(ii)

in the other cases, e.g.:

(a)

if the sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are good for equidistribution;

(b)

or if the sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are good for irrational equidistribution and we are interested in norm convergence or limiting identities,

then we study the averages with
n
restricted to
{\mathbb{P}}
; however, the same argument normally works for any shift
{\mathbb{P}}+c
.

We now present joint ergodicity results for sequences evaluated at primes.

3.3.1.
Sequences along primes that are good for equidistribution

Using the Old Joint Ergodicity Strategy, Karageorgos and Koutsogiannis proved the extension of their Theorem
3.23
along primes.

Theorem 3.34

(
[
97
, Theorem 2.14]
)
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{R}}[t]
be strongly independent real polynomials.
Then the sequences
a_{1}(p_{n}),\ldots,a_{\ell}(p_{n})
are controlled by the invariant factor for any system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
. In particular, they are jointly ergodic for any ergodic system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
.

On the other hand, one of the first applications of the New Joint Ergodicity Strategy was the following result of Frantzikinakis for fractional polynomials along primes.

Theorem 3.35

(
[
58
, Theorem 1.1]
)
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
be linearly independent fractional polynomials
a_{j}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\alpha_{ji}t^{b_{i}}
with
\alpha_{ji}\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
b_{i}
’s positive nonintegers. Then the sequences
a_{1}(p_{n}),\ldots,a_{\ell}(p_{n})
are controlled by the invariant factor for any system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
. In particular, they are jointly ergodic for any ergodic system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
.

As an example, Theorem
3.35
yields the identity

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]\cap{\mathbb{P}}}T^{{\lfloor n^{b_{1}}\rfloor}}f_{1}\cdot T^{{\lfloor n^{b_{2}}\rfloor}}f_{2}-\int f_{1}\;d\mu\cdot\int f_{2}\;d\mu\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0

for any noninteger
0<b_{1}<b_{2}
whenever
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
is ergodic and
f_{1},f_{2}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
.

The same paper inquired about extending Theorem
3.35
to general strongly independent Hardy sequences. This problem was resolved by Koutsogiannis and Tsinas
[
108
]
for the single transformation and by Donoso, Koutsogiannis, Sun, Tsinas, and the author
[
45
]
for commuting transformations.

Theorem 3.36

(
[
108
, Theorem 1.4]
)
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{H}}
be strongly independent Hardy sequences. Then the sequences
a_{1}(p_{n}),\ldots,a_{\ell}(p_{n})
are controlled by the invariant factor for any system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
. In particular, they are jointly ergodic for any ergodic system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
.

Theorem 3.37

(
[
45
, Theorem 1.12(i)]
)
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{H}}
be strongly independent Hardy sequences. Then the sequences
a_{1}(p_{n}),\ldots,a_{\ell}(p_{n})
are controlled by the invariant factor. In particular, they are jointly ergodic for all systems
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
with
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
ergodic.

For completeness, we note that the work of Koutsogiannis and Tsinas also proves the extension of Theorem
3.26
along primes
[
108
, Theorem 1.5]
, a special case of Theorem
3.37
.

While Theorems
3.36
and
3.37
extend Theorems
3.24
and
3.28
to averages along primes, the following result does the same with Theorem
3.29
.

Theorem 3.38

(
[
45
, Theorem 1.12(ii)]
)
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{H}}
be pairwise independent Hardy sequences. Then the sequences
a_{1}(p_{n}),\ldots,a_{\ell}(p_{n})
:

(i)

admit Host-Kra seminorm control;

(ii)

are jointly ergodic for any system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
with
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
weakly mixing.

All the aforementioned results follow from a beautiful transference principle of Koutsogiannis and Tsinas. In what follows, we use the following definition, examples of which are
t+\sqrt{2},\;t^{2}+1/t
.

Definition 3.39

(Almost rational polynomials)
.

We call
a\in{\mathcal{H}}
an
almost rational polynomial
if
\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}|a(t)-p(t)|=0
for some
p\in{\mathbb{Q}}[t]+{\mathbb{R}}
.

Theorem 3.40

(Transference principle for Hardy sequences along primes
[
108
, Theorem 1.2]
)
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{H}}
be Hardy sequences, each one of which either stays logarithmically away from rational polynomials or is an almost rational polynomial.
Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system and
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
. Suppose that for any
w,b\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the averages

\displaystyle\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{a_{1}(Wn+b)}f_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{a_{\ell}(Wn+b)}f_{\ell}

converge to the same limit
F
in
L^{2}(\mu)
(where
W=\prod\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\in{\mathbb{P}}\colon p\leq w\end{subarray}}p
as before). Then

\displaystyle\E_{n\in[N]\cap{\mathbb{P}}}T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{a_{\ell}(n)}f_{\ell}

also converges to
F
in
L^{2}(\mu)
.

We note that in the statement of
[
108
, Theorem 1.2]
, the convergence of the averages along
Wn+b
to
F
is required for all
W\in{\mathbb{N}}
, not just primorials (products of consecutive primes). However, the inspection of the proof shows that it suffices for if to hold for primorials.

Furthermore,
[
108
, Theorem 1.2]
holds for more general averages in which each term
T_{j}^{a_{j}(n)}f_{j}
is replaced by a product
T_{1}^{a_{j1}(n)}\cdots T_{\ell}^{a_{j\ell}(n)}f_{j}
.

3.3.2.
Integer polynomials along primes

A transference principle in the spirit of Theorem
3.40
also gives extensions of Theorems
3.13
and
3.15
to averages of integer polynomials along primes.

Theorem 3.41

(
[
69
, Theorem 2.13]
)
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[t]
be affinely independent polynomials. Then the sequences
a_{1}(p_{n}),\ldots,a_{\ell}(p_{n})

are controlled by the rational Kronecker factor. In particular, they are jointly ergodic for any system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
with
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
totally ergodic.

Theorem 3.42
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[t]
be pairwise independent polynomials. Then the sequences
a_{1}(p_{n}),\ldots,a_{\ell}(p_{n})
:

(i)

admit Host-Kra seminorm control;

(ii)

are jointly ergodic for any system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
with
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
weakly mixing.

The second of these results is not explicitly stated in
[
69
]
, but following the remark below
[
69
, Theorem 2.13]
, it can be deduced in a straightforward way from Theorem
3.15
and
3.40
.

3.4.
Generalized polynomials

Another natural class of sequences of polynomial growth that has received significant attention in dynamics are generalized polynomials.

Definition 3.43
.

A function
a:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is a
generalized polynomial
if it can be formed by finitely many applications of the symbols
+,-,\cdot,{\left\lfloor\cdot\right\rfloor}
(and hence also the fractional part
\{\cdot\}
) to constants and the identity function
b(t)=t
.

Examples of generalized polynomials include

\displaystyle t,\;\sqrt{2}t^{2}+\sqrt{3}t,\;\pi t{\lfloor\sqrt{5}t\rfloor},\;e{\lfloor\sqrt{5}t^{6}+{\lfloor\sqrt{7}t\rfloor}\rfloor}{\lfloor\sqrt{6}t\rfloor},\;\sqrt{6}t\{\sqrt{2}t^{2}\}.

On the surface, generalized polynomials look similar to polynomials, but they may differ considerably. For instance, there exist nonconstant generalized polynomials that are bounded (e.g.
\left\{t/2\right\}
).

One reason behind the interest in generalized polynomials is that they arise naturally in higher order Fourier analysis. For instance, the function
n\mapsto e(\sqrt{2}n{\left\lfloor\sqrt{3}n\right\rfloor})
can be realized as a 2-step nilsequence on the Heisenberg nilmanifold. See e.g. the paper of Bergelson and Leibman
[
21
]
for more results concerning the connection between these two classes.

In contrast to polynomials and Hardy sequences, the structure of multiple ergodic averages along generalized polynomials is relatively unexplored. We therefore pose a number of problems to breathe life into this line of research. For simplicity, we state these problems for a single transformation; however, every single one of them can be extended to commuting transformations.
The first problem asks about the norm convergence of such averages.

Problem 5

(Norm convergence for generalized polynomials
[
56
, Problem 14]
)
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
be generalized polynomials,
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
be a system, and
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
. Does the average

\displaystyle\E_{n\in[N]}T^{{\lfloor a_{1}(n)\rfloor}}f_{1}\cdots T^{{\lfloor a_{\ell}(n)\rfloor}}f_{\ell}

converge in
L^{2}(\mu)
?

While the case
\ell=1
of Problem
5
follows from the spectral theorem and the characterization of bounded generalized polynomials from
[
21
]
, the problem remains even open for
\ell=2
and
T
weakly mixing systems.

The next problem aims at finding the correct notion of pairwise independence for generalized polynomials.

Problem 6

(Seminorm control for generalized polynomials)
.

Characterize those generalized polynomials that admit Host-Kra seminorm control for every system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
.

The next two problems ask for the right notions of independence and strong independence for generalized polynomials. Ideally, the requested characterization would take the form of a condition on nontrivial linear combinations of the sequences.

Problem 7

(Joint ergodicity for strongly independent generalized polynomials)
.

Characterize those generalized polynomials that are jointly ergodic for every ergodic system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
.

Problem 8

(Joint ergodicity for independent generalized polynomials)
.

Characterize those generalized polynomials that are controlled by the rational Kronecker factor for every system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
.

3.5.
Generalized Hardy sequences

Just like generalized polynomials are formed by adding
{\lfloor\cdot\rfloor}
to three operations defining polynomials, we can consider a generalization of Hardy sequences that involves sequences like

\displaystyle{\lfloor n^{b}\rfloor}^{2},\;{\lfloor n\log n\rfloor}^{3/2},\;\exp({\lfloor\log n\rfloor}).

We refrain from formally defining such a class; we will however refer to it informally as
generalized Hardy sequences
. Sequences like
{\lfloor n^{b}\rfloor}^{2}
naturally appear in the intermediate steps of PET arguments for Hardy sequences
[
45
,
142
]
; their equidistribution as well as (single) recurrence and convergence properties were studied by Frantzikinakis
[
52
]
. Several problems on such sequences have been posed by Tsinas
[
142
]
but have not received much attention so far. We restate them here, adding several of our own. We invite the reader to consult
[
142
, Section 1.5]
for more discussion.

Problem 9

(Norm convergence for generalized Hardy sequences
[
142
, Conjecture 1]
)
.

Let
0<b_{1}<\cdots<b_{\ell}
,
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
be a system, and
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
. Does the average

\displaystyle\E_{n\in[N]}T^{{\lfloor n^{b_{1}}\rfloor}^{2}}f_{1}\cdots T^{{\lfloor n^{b_{\ell}}\rfloor}^{2}}f_{\ell}

converge in
L^{2}(\mu)
?

Problem 10

(Seminorm control for generalized Hardy sequences)
.

Let
0<b_{1}<\cdots<b_{\ell}
. Do the sequences
{\lfloor n^{b_{1}}\rfloor}^{2},\cdots,{\lfloor n^{b_{\ell}}\rfloor}^{2}
admit Host-Kra seminorm control?

Problem 11

(Joint ergodicity for generalized Hardy sequences and totally ergodic systems
[
142
, Conjecture 2]
)
.

Let
0<b_{1}<\cdots<b_{\ell}
. Are the sequences
{\lfloor n^{b_{1}}\rfloor}^{2},\cdots{\lfloor n^{b_{\ell}}\rfloor}^{2}
jointly ergodic for any totally ergodic system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
?

Problem 12

(Joint ergodicity for generalized Hardy sequences and ergodic systems)
.

Let
0<b_{1}<\cdots<b_{\ell}
be not in
\frac{1}{2}{\mathbb{Z}}
. Are the sequences
{\lfloor n^{b_{1}}\rfloor}^{2},\cdots{\lfloor n^{b_{\ell}}\rfloor}^{2}
jointly ergodic for any ergodic system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
?

Problem 13

(Multiple recurrence for generalized Hardy sequences
[
142
, Conjecture 3]
)
.

Let
0<b_{1}<\cdots<b_{\ell}
,
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
be a system, and
E\in{\mathcal{X}}
with
\mu(E)>0
. Does there exist
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
for which

\displaystyle\mu(E\cap T^{-{\lfloor n^{b_{1}}\rfloor}^{2}}E\cap\cdots\cap T^{-{\lfloor n^{b_{\ell}}\rfloor}^{2}}E)>0?

3.6.
Tempered functions

Another class of functions of polynomial growth studied in ergodic theory is the family of tempered functions.

Definition 3.44

(Tempered functions)
.

Let
d\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
. A function
a:[t_{0},\infty)\to{\mathbb{R}}
is called
tempered
of
degree
d
if it satisfies the following conditions:

(i)

a
is
(d+1)
-times continuously differentiable;

(ii)

a^{(d+1)}(t)
tends monotonically to 0 as
t\to\infty
;

(iii)

\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}t|a^{(d+1)}(t)|=\infty
.

A tempered function of degree
0
is called
Fejér
.

Just like with Hardy functions, we identify two tempered functions if they eventually agree; hence when we speak of tempered functions, we really mean
germs
of functions.

Despite superficial similarity and nontrivial overlap, tempered functions differ from Hardy functions in several significant ways.
Tempered functions need not be smooth, nor do their ratios converge in general. No polynomial is tempered. Tempered functions are not closed under arithmetic operations or differentiation (for instance,
t^{3/2}+t,\;t^{3/2}
are both tempered, but their difference is not).
To avoid various problematic cases, we typically restrict to special classes of tempered functions, such as the class
{\mathcal{T}}
defined below:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}

\displaystyle:=\left\{a\in C^{\infty}\colon\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{ta^{(i+1)}(t)}{a^{(i)}(t)}\in{\mathbb{R}}\;\textrm{for all}\;i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}\right\};

\displaystyle{\mathcal{T}}_{i}

\displaystyle:=\left\{a\in{\mathcal{R}}\colon\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{ta^{\prime}(t)}{a(t)}\in(i,i+1]\;\textrm{and}\;\lim_{t\to\infty}a^{(i+1)}(t)=0\right\};

\displaystyle{\mathcal{T}}

\displaystyle:=\bigcup_{i=0}^{\infty}{\mathcal{T}}_{i}.

By
[
17
]
, a function
a\in{\mathcal{T}}_{i}
has degree
i
and satisfies the growth condition
t^{i}\log t\prec a(t)\prec t^{i+1}
; hence elements of
{\mathcal{T}}
are nonpolynomial in a quantitatively precise sense.

Examples of functions in
{\mathcal{T}}
include
t^{b}(\log t)^{c}
for
b\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\backslash{\mathbb{N}}
, as well as functions with sufficiently well-controlled oscillation, as in the example below.

Example 3.45
.

Let
g(t)=t^{b}(2+\cos\sqrt{\log t})
for
b\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\backslash{\mathbb{N}}
. Then

g^{(i)}(t)=(1+o(1))c_{b,i}t^{b-i}(2+\cos\sqrt{\log t})

for
c_{b,i}:=b(b-1)\cdots(b-(i-1))
, which for every
i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
is nonzero by the assumption
b\notin{\mathbb{N}}
.
Since the oscillatory factor is bounded by 1, we deduce that this function is in
{\mathcal{T}}_{i}
for
i={\left\lfloor b\right\rfloor}={\left\lceil b\right\rceil}-1
.

Joint ergodicity for multiple ergodic averages has been studied by Bergelson and Håland-Knutson
[
17
]
, and later by Koutsogiannis
[
105
]
. They proved a number of joint ergodicity results by imposing various conditions on the tempered functions, some under weakly mixing and some under ergodic assumptions. We will list here samples of their results, directing the reader to both papers for more general statements.

The first of these results can be seen as an extension of Furstenberg’s joint ergodicity result for weakly mixing systems (Theorem
3.4
).

Theorem 3.46

(
[
17
, Corollary 5.14(i)]
)
.

Let
a
be a tempered function and
0<c_{1}<\cdots<c_{\ell}
be real numbers. Then the sequences
c_{1}a(n),\cdots,c_{\ell}a(n)
are jointly ergodic for any weakly mixing system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
.

The second one concerns tempered functions of distinct growth.

Theorem 3.47

(
[
105
, Proposition 2.5, Corollary 2.6, Corollary 2.7]
)
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{T}}
be tempered functions with the growth conditions
a_{1}\prec\cdots\prec a_{\ell}
.

(i)

Then
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
admit Host-Kra seminorm control. In particular, they are jointly ergodic for any system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
with
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
weakly mixing.

(ii)

Suppose that any nontrivial linear combination of
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
is in
{\mathcal{T}}
. Then
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are controlled by the invariant factor. In particular, they are jointly ergodic for any system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
with
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
ergodic.

The extra assumption in the second case is needed to invoke equidistribution results for tempered functions. As the example below shows, the result may fail without it, and hence the fact that
{\mathcal{T}}
is not closed under taking linear combinations (as opposed to
{\mathcal{H}}
) has real consequences.

Example 3.48
.

Let

\displaystyle a_{1}(t)=t^{3/2}(2+\cos\sqrt{\log t})+2t\quad\textrm{and}\quad a_{2}(t)=t^{3/2}(2+\cos\sqrt{\log t}).

While they are both tempered of degree 1, their linear combination
a_{1}(t)-a_{2}(t)=2t
is not tempered.
Hence they do not satisfy the assumptions of Theorem
3.47
(
ii
), nor, as we see shortly, its conclusion. Indeed, on taking
T_{1}=T_{2}
to be the map
x\mapsto x+1
on
{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}
and letting
f_{1}(x)=\overline{f_{2}}(x)=(-1)^{x}
, we see that

\displaystyle f_{1}(T_{1}^{{\lfloor a_{1}(n)\rfloor}}x)\cdot f_{2}(T_{2}^{{\lfloor a_{2}(n)\rfloor}}x)=1

holds for all
x\in{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and hence the pointwise limit of the Cesàro average is 1.
By contrast,
\int f_{1}\;d\mu=\int f_{2}\;d\mu=0
; hence
a_{1},a_{2}
are not jointly ergodic for this system.

For a single transformation, and whenever
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
have distinct positive degrees, the first part of Theorem
3.47
was covered by
[
17
, Corollary 5.14(iii)]

Theorem 3.49

(
[
105
, Theorem 2.4]
,
[
17
, Theorem 7.3]
)
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{T}}
be Fejér functions with the growth conditions
a_{1}\prec\cdots\prec a_{\ell}
. Suppose moreover that the ratios
a^{\prime}_{j}(t)/a_{j+1}^{\prime}(t)
are eventually monotone for every
1\leq j<\ell
. Then
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are controlled by the invariant factor. In particular, they are jointly ergodic for any system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
with
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
ergodic.

The conclusion for
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
ergodic was first obtained by Bergelson and Håland-Knutson; Koutsogiannis then removed the ergodicity assumption.

In the light of recent progress on polynomials and Hardy sequences outline in previous sections, it is natural to inquire whether the assumptions of different growth in the results above can be weakened to some variants of independence and pairwise independence.

Problem 14
.

Find a reasonable notion of “pairwise independence” on
{\mathcal{T}}
so that all pairwise independent functions
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{T}}
admit Host-Kra seminorm control (and hence are jointly ergodic for every system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
with
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
weakly mixing).

Problem 15
.

Find a reasonable notion of “independence” on
{\mathcal{T}}
so that all independent functions
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{T}}
are controlled by the invariant factor (and hence are jointly ergodic for every system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
with
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
ergodic).

3.7.
Sequences of superpolynomial growth and with oscillations

The scope of methods employed to deliver Host-Kra seminorm estimates is constrained by the growth of the sequence: existing techniques cannot deal with
sequences growing faster than polynomials. Differentiating a polynomial finitely many times brings it to a linear one. Much for the same reasons, finitely many steps of the PET induction scheme (see Section
5.2.1
) applied to an average along “nice” sequences of polynomial growth (polynomials, Hardy sequences, tempered functions, etc.) bring it down to an average with linear iterates that can be controlled by Host-Kra or box seminorms as in Examples
1.6
and
1.8
. While lacunary sequences tend to be bad for ergodic-theoretic purposes (for instance, the sequence
(2^{n}\alpha)_{n}
is not dense for a set of
\alpha\in{\mathbb{T}}
of full Hausdorff dimension, as shown by Erdös and Taylor
[
50
]
9
9

9

For example, take any
\alpha
with no consecutive 1’s in its binary expansion. It is easy to see that
2^{n}\alpha\in[0,3/4)
, as the binary expansion of any
x\in[3/4,1)
starts with two 1’s.
), there is a grey zone of sequences that grow superpolynomially but subexponentially, like
n^{(\log n)^{b}}
(for small
b>0
) or
n^{\log\log n}
. In contrast to lacunary sequences, some of these intermediate ones are known to be equidistributed
[
98
]
.

A similar issue arises in the study of sequences with oscillations, like
n\sin n
. While known to be equidistributed
[
8
,
9
,
10
]
, they are not amenable to current techniques for seminorm estimates because their successive derivatives do not exhibit any decay.

Combining known equidistribution results for such sequences with Corollary
2.14
, Frantzikinakis deduced the following results on ergodic nilsystems (he states them in the single-transformation case, but the exact same reasoning works in the commuting case).

Theorem 3.50

(
[
59
, Theorem 1.10]
)
.

The following families of sequences are jointly ergodic for any nilsystem
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
with
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
ergodic:

(i)

n^{(\log n)^{b_{1}}},\ldots,n^{(\log n)^{b_{\ell}}}
for any
0<b_{1}<\cdots<b_{\ell}<1/2
;

(ii)

a_{1}(n)\sin n,\ldots,a_{\ell}(n)\sin n
for affinely independent
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[t]
;

(iii)

n^{k}\sin(n),n^{k}\sin(2n)\ldots,n^{k}\sin(\ell n)
for any
k,\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

The next problem inquires whether the restriction to nilsystems in Theorem
3.50
can be dropped; in other words, whether the abovementioned families of sequences are amenable to the Host-Kra machinery.

Problem 16

(
[
59
, Problem 2]
)
.

Do the families in Theorem
3.50
admit Host-Kra seminorm control (even in the single-transformation case)?

4.
Joint ergodicity classification problem

The previous section listed rather general classes of sequences and systems for which we have joint ergodicity; hence it dealt with
sufficient
conditions for this phenomenon. This section concentrates on equivalent characterization of joint ergodicity, i.e. sufficient
and
necessary conditions.

One such set of necessary and sufficient conditions is provided by Theorem
2.8
. Vaguely speaking, one can view the Host-Kra seminorm control and equidistribution conditions as dealing with the weakly mixing and spectral behaviors respectively. This section focuses on two different conditions, which relate joint ergodicity to ergodicity of simpler actions. Before we state them, we record one definition used profusely throughout this section.

Definition 4.1

(Strongly and weakly ergodic actions)
.

A sequence of measure preserving-transformations
(T_{n})_{n}
on
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
is
strongly/weakly ergodic
if

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}T_{n}f=\int f\;d\mu

for any
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
in the strong/weak sense. If we do not specify the mode of convergence, then “ergodicity” stands for “strong ergodicity”.

Here are the conditions that we shall work with.

Definition 4.2

(Ergodicity conditions)
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\colon{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
be sequences and
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system. For this system, the sequences satisfy:

(i)

the
product ergodicity condition
if
(T_{1}^{{a_{1}(n)}}\times\cdots\times T_{\ell}^{{a_{\ell}(n)}})_{n}
is ergodic for the product system
(X^{\ell},{\mathcal{X}}^{\otimes\ell},\mu^{\ell})
;

(ii)

the
difference ergodicity condition
if
(T_{i}^{{a_{i}(n)}}T_{j}^{-{a_{j}(n)}})_{n}
is ergodic for
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
for all distinct
1\leq i,j\leq\ell
.

Many results defined in this section concern the case when all the iterates are the same. While discussing such results
we apply the following convention.

Convention 3
.

We say that a property defined for sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
holds for a sequence
a:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
if it holds with
a=a_{1}=\cdots=a_{\ell}
.

For instance, we often say
that
a
satisfies the product/difference ergodicity condition or is jointly ergodic for a system if the relevant property is satisfied for
a=a_{1}=\cdots=a_{\ell}
. If our sequences are real-valued, then we say that they satisfy the product/difference ergodicity condition if their integer parts do, in accordance with Convention
1
.

Theorem
1.11
, the foundational joint ergodicity result of Berend and Bergelson, can then be restated as follows.

Theorem 4.3
.

The sequence
a(n)=n
is jointly ergodic for a system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
if and only if it satisfies the product and difference ergodicity conditions for this system.

It is instructive to see how the product and difference ergodicity conditions interact with the conditions present in Theorem
2.8
. To this end, we sketch a modern-day proof of Theorem
4.3
that uses Theorem
2.8
as a black box.

Proof.

We start by relating the product ergodicity condition to being good for equidistribution. It is classical (see e.g.
[
90
, Chapter 4, Theorem 16]
) that the space of
T_{1}\times\cdots\times T_{\ell}
-invariant functions
is spanned by functions

\displaystyle g_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes g_{\ell},

where each
g_{j}
is an eigenfunction of
T_{j}
with eigenvalue
\beta_{j}
subject to the constraint
\beta_{1}+\cdots+\beta_{\ell}=0
. Now, the function
g_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes g_{\ell}
is constant if and only if
\beta_{1}=\cdots=\beta_{\ell}=0
, from which it follows that
T_{1}\times\cdots\times T_{\ell}
is ergodic if and only if
a(n)=n
is good for equidistribution for the system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
.

We similarly relate Host-Kra seminorm control to the difference ergodicity condition.
Recall the seminorm estimate (
7
), which we restate here for convenience:

\displaystyle\limsup_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{n}f_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{n}f_{\ell}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq\lvert\!|\!|f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{T_{\ell},T_{\ell}T_{1}^{-1},\ldots,T_{\ell}T_{\ell-1}^{-1}}.

On one hand, if
T_{i}T_{j}^{-1}
are ergodic, then we can bound the right-hand side by
\lvert\!|\!|f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{\ell,T_{\ell}}
, yielding Host-Kra seminorm control (note that the choice of the index
\ell
here is arbitrary). Hence the product and difference ergodicity conditions together imply joint ergodicity by Theorem
2.8
. On the other hand, if
T_{i}T_{j}^{-1}
is not ergodic for some
i\neq j
, take a nonconstant
T_{i}T_{j}^{-1}
-invariant
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
.
If we set
f_{i}=f
,
f_{j}=\overline{f}
, then
T_{i}^{n}f_{i}\cdot T_{j}^{n}f_{j}=T_{i}^{n}|f|^{2}
. Hence

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathopen{}\left|\E_{n\in[N]}\int T_{i}^{n}f\cdot T_{j}^{n}f_{j}\;d\mu\mathclose{}\right|=\int|f|^{2}\;d\mu.

If however
a(n)=n
is jointly ergodic, then

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathopen{}\left|\E_{n\in[N]}\int T_{i}^{n}f\cdot T_{j}^{n}f_{j}\;d\mu\mathclose{}\right|=\mathopen{}\left|\int f\;d\mu\mathclose{}\right|^{2}.

Since
f
is assumed to be nonconstant, this is strictly smaller than
\int|f|^{2}\;d\mu
, yielding a contradiction to joint ergodicity.
∎

The insights presented in the proof above hold more generally: the product ergodicity condition is related to being good for equidistribution while the difference ergodicity condition can be used to upgrade box seminorm control to Host-Kra seminorm control; the latter is vastly preferable because we generally have no good structure theorems for box seminorms. For more discussion on the relationships between these various notions, consult
[
45
, Section 12]
.

A problem that has driven a lot of joint ergodicity research is to understand the extent to which the product and difference ergodicity conditions characterize joint ergodicity in general.

Problem 17

(Joint ergodicity classification problem,
[
46
, Problem 1]
)
.

Classify the sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
for which the following equivalence holds for any system:
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are jointly ergodic for a system if and only if they satisfy the product and difference ergodicity conditions for this system.

Most of the work in establishing Problem
17
for some class of iterates goes into showing that product and difference ergodicity conditions are sufficient for joint ergodicity; hence it makes sense to think of this direction as “difficult”.

The remainder of this section will present known positive (and negative) results on this problem, as well as further open questions.

4.1.
Integer polynomials

For integer polynomials, Problem
17
has been resolved by Frantzikinakis and the author.

Theorem 4.4

(
[
67
]
)
.

Problem
17
has an affirmative answer for integer polynomials. That is, integer polynomials are jointly ergodic for a system if and only if they satisfy the product and difference ergodicity conditions for this system.

Theorem
4.4
was proved after a number of partial results:

(i)

Donoso, Koutsogiannis, and Sun resolved Problem
17
for a single integer polynomial, i.e. when
a_{1}=\cdots=a_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[t]

[
46
]
;

(ii)

Frantzikinakis and the author
[
69
]
answered Problem
17
in the complementary case of pairwise independent integer polynomials.

These earlier works
were important because they introduced new techniques for deriving seminorm estimates presented in Section
5.2
.

For polynomials in
{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}[t]
, the following more general problem remains open. For a system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
and
b\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}
, we denote
T^{\mathbf{b}}=T_{1}^{b_{1}}\cdots T_{\ell}^{b_{\ell}}
.

Problem 18

(Joint ergodicity classification problem for polynomials over
{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}

[
46
, Conjecture 1.5]
,
[
43
, Conjecture 1.3]
)
.

Let
\mathbf{a}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}[t]
be polynomials and
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu
,
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system. Prove or disprove:
(T^{\mathbf{a}_{1}(n)},\ldots,T_{\ell}^{\mathbf{a}_{\ell}(n)})_{n}
is jointly ergodic on
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
if and only if the following hold:

(i)

(T^{{\mathbf{a}_{1}(n)}}\times\cdots\times T^{{\mathbf{a}_{\ell}(n)}})_{n}
is ergodic for the product system
(X^{\ell},{\mathcal{X}}^{\otimes\ell},\mu^{\ell})
;

(ii)

(T^{{\mathbf{a}_{i}(n)}}T_{j}^{-\mathbf{a}_{j}(n)})_{n}
is ergodic for
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
for all distinct
1\leq i,j\leq\ell
.

Theorem
4.4
corresponds to the case when
\mathbf{a}_{j}={\mathbf{e}}_{j}a_{j}
for some
a_{j}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[t]
. Several cases towards Problem
18
not covered by Theorem
4.4
have been addressed by Donoso, Koutsogiannis, Ferré-Moragues, and Sun
[
43
]
.

4.2.
Hardy sequences

For Hardy sequences, Problem
17
admits a positive answer in the following two special cases, resolved respectively by Donoso, Koutsogiannis, and Sun as well as Donoso, Koutsogiannis, Sun, Tsinas, and the author.

Theorem 4.5

(
[
47
, Theorem 1.1]
)
.

Let
a\in{\mathcal{H}}
, and suppose that
a

stays logarithmically away from real polynomials
in that
a-p\succ\log
for any
p\in{\mathbb{R}}[t]
. Then Problem
17
has an affirmative answer for
a
.

Theorem
4.5
thus covers
a(t)
being any of
t^{3/2},t\log t,t+(\log t)^{2}
, but neither of
\sqrt{2}t^{2},t^{2}+\log t
. In
[
47
, Theorem 6.1]
, the authors also extend it to
a\in{\mathcal{T}}
.

Theorem 4.6

(
[
45
, Theorem 1.9]
)
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{H}}
be pairwise independent. Then Problem
17
has an affirmative answer for
a
.

For general Hardy iterates of polynomial growth, Problem
17
turns out to be more intricate than anticipated. In
[
44
]
, Donoso, Koutsogiannis, Sun, Tsinas, and the author resolve affirmatively the “difficult” direction of Problem
17
, establishing the following.

Theorem 4.7

(
[
44
, Theorem 1.5]
)
.

Suppose that
a_{1},\dots,a_{\ell}\in\mathcal{H}
satisfy the difference and product ergodicity conditions for a system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
.
Then they are jointly ergodic for the system.

The same work establishes the following partial converse. Recall Definition
3.39
for the definition of almost rational polynomials.

Theorem 4.8

(
[
44
, Theorem 1.7]
)
.

Suppose that
a_{1},\dots,a_{\ell}\in\mathcal{H}
are jointly ergodic for a system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},

\ldots,T_{\ell})
. Then the following holds:

(i)

The sequences satisfy the product ergodicity condition on the system.

(ii)

Suppose additionally that for every
i\neq j
,
c_{i},c_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}
, the function
c_{i}a_{i}-c_{j}a_{j}
is either an almost rational polynomial or stays logarithmically away from
{\mathbb{Q}}[x]
. Then the sequences satisfy the difference ergodicity condition on the system.

Combining Theorem
4.7
and
4.8
gives an affirmative answer to Problem
17
for a large class of Hardy sequences, which includes all fractional polynomials (with any nonnegative exponents, including positive integer ones).

Corollary 4.9

(
[
44
, Corollary 1.8]
)
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{H}}
be Hardy sequences with the property that for every
i\neq j
and
c_{i},c_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}
the function
c_{i}a_{i}-c_{j}a_{j}
is either an almost rational polynomial or it stays logarithmically away from rational polynomials.
Then the sequences are jointly ergodic for a system if and only if they satisfy the product and difference ergodicity conditions for the system.

Unexpectedly, Problem
17
has a negative answer for Hardy sequences not covered by Corollary
4.9
.

Theorem 4.10

(
[
44
, Theorem 1.10]
)
.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
be a system. Then
(T^{n},T^{n+{\left\lfloor\log_{2}n\right\rfloor}})_{n}
is jointly ergodic if and only if
T
is mixing. By contrast,
(T^{{\left\lfloor\log_{2}n\right\rfloor}})_{n}
is ergodic if and only the system is conjugate to a one-point system. Consequently, if
T
is mixing but not conjugate to a one-point system, then
(T^{n},T^{n+{\left\lfloor\log_{2}n\right\rfloor}})_{n}
is jointly ergodic even though
(T^{{\left\lfloor\log_{2}n\right\rfloor}})_{n}
is not ergodic.

The issue that Theorem
4.10
exploits is that joint ergodicity for a given system need not imply the difference ergodicity condition for this system because the ergodic average along
(T_{i}^{a_{i}(n)}T_{j}^{-a_{j}(n)})_{n}
may not converge in norm. However, it is always the case that the joint ergodicity of
(T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)},\ldots,T_{\ell}^{a_{\ell}(n)})_{n}
implies that

(24)

\displaystyle\E_{n\in[N]}T_{i}^{a_{i}(n)}T_{j}^{-a_{j}(n)}f

converges weakly to
\int f\;d\mu
for any
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
since

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}\int g\cdot T_{i}^{a_{i}(n)}T_{j}^{-a_{j}(n)}f\;d\mu

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}\int T_{j}^{a_{j}(n)}g\cdot T_{i}^{a_{i}(n)}f\;d\mu

\displaystyle=\int g\;d\mu\cdot\int f\;d\mu

by joint ergodicity. In the light of that,
[
44
]
proposes two variants of Problem
17
that rely on the weak convergence version of the notions used in Problem
17
.

Problem 19

(
[
44
, Question 8.3]
)
.

Does Problem
17
admit affirmative answer for all
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{H}}
if we define the difference ergodicity condition in terms of weak rather than strong ergodicity?

Problem 20

(
[
44
, Question 8.4]
)
.

Does Problem
17
admit affirmative answer for all
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{H}}
if joint ergodicity as well as product and difference ergodicity conditions are all defined in terms of weak rather than strong limits?

Problem
19
can be very difficult; a positive answer would give that 2-mixing implies a property resembling 3-mixing: see
[
44
, Proposition 8.5]
.

Another possible way to fix Problem
17
for Hardy sequences would be to consider weighted averaging schemes as in Section
3.2.4
.

Problem 21

(
[
44
, Conjecture 8.8]
)
.

Fix
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{H}}
. Can we always find
1\prec W(t)\ll t
such that Problem
17
admits affirmative answer for
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
if we define joint ergodicity as well as product and difference ergodicity conditions using
W
-averaging schemes?

We invite the reader interested in possible fixes to Problem
17
to consult
[
44
, Section 8]
, which provides an ample discussion of three questions listed above.

4.3.
Generalized polynomials

Problem
17
can be posed for other classes of sequences. In this survey, we restrict our attention to one more such class: generalized polynomials, defined in Section
3.4

Problem 22
.

Resolve Problem
17
for generalized polynomials.

The reason we discuss this particular class is that the following special case of Problem
22
has been resolved by Bergelson, Leibman, and Son.

Theorem 4.11

(
[
24
, Theorem 0.4]
)
.

Problem
17
admits the affirmative answer for generalized linear functions.

Generalized linear functions
are precisely what one thinks: they are functions constructed from applying finitely many times the operations of addition, scalar multiplication, and taking integer parts to linear functions
\alpha t+\beta
. Examples include

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}{\left\lfloor\alpha_{2}t+\alpha_{3}\right\rfloor}+\alpha_{4}t+\alpha_{5},\;\left\{\alpha_{1}t\right\}+{\left\lfloor\alpha_{2}t\right\rfloor},\;\alpha_{1}{\left\lfloor\alpha_{2}{\left\lfloor\alpha_{3}t+\alpha_{4}\right\rfloor}+\alpha_{5}\right\rfloor}+\alpha_{6}

for
\alpha_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}
. We do emphasize, however, that generalized linear functions are a very particular subclass of generalized polynomials, and it is not clear how much of the intuition behind Theorem
4.11
extends to arbitrary generalized polynomials.

5.
Technical breakthroughs

This section aims to highlight the main ideas behind two major technical breakthroughs that lie at the foundation of recent joint ergodicity advances:

(i)

the
degree lowering
argument used to prove the difficult direction in Theorem
2.8
;

(ii)

the new techniques to prove Host-Kra seminorm estimates for multiple ergodic averages of commuting transformations, which involve a refined version of the
PET induction scheme
, (qualitative and quantitative)
concatenation
arguments for box seminorms and factors, and the
seminorm smoothing
technique that upgrades box seminorm control of an average to one by Host-Kra seminorms.

5.1.
Degree lowering

In this subsection, we will outline the proof of the difficult direction of Theorem
2.9
via degree lowering. Given its complexity, it is impossible to outline the argument by giving equal importance to its every aspect. In this exposition, we focus on the complex induction scheme that underpins the argument and the idea of “transferring seminorm control” lying at its base. At the same time, we try to sweep under the carpet various (non-trivial and highly ingenious) technical maneuvers such as dual-difference interchange and removing low-complexity functions.
For an alternative take on degree lowering, which gives comparatively more space to technical tricks and less to induction in the more concrete case of the Furstenberg-Weiss average

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}T^{n}f_{1}\cdot T^{n^{2}}f_{2},

we recommend the exposition of Frantzikinakis
[
59
, Section 2]
.

Theorem
2.9
is a local result, in the sense that it holds for fixed integer sequences and system. Therefore, for the entirety of this section, we fix sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\colon{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
and a system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
. We thus restate the statement whose proof we are about to sketch.

Theorem 5.1

(The difficult direction of Theorem
2.9
)
.

Suppose that the sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
admit Host-Kra seminorm control and are good for equidistribution for the system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
. Then they are controlled by the invariant factor for the system, i.e.

(25)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}T_{j}^{{a_{j}(n)}}f_{j}-\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}\E(f_{j}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{j}))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0

holds for all
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
.

5.1.1.
Induction scheme

The proof of Theorem
2.9
follows a double induction argument. In the outer layer of induction, we induct on the parameter
0\leq m\leq\ell
: it is the complexity of the average, measuring the number of functions in the average that are not of a “simple” form. The inner induction scheme, relegated to the next subsection, deals with successively lowering the degree of the Host-Kra seminorm that controls our average.

The inductive property that we derive in the outer induction scheme is as follows.

Definition 5.2
.

Let
0\leq m\leq\ell
. We say that Property
(P_{m})
holds if the identity (
25
) holds whenever
f_{m+1},\ldots,f_{\ell}
are nonergodic eigenfunctions of
T_{m+1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
respectively.

By definition, Property
(P_{0})
corresponds to the case when all functions are nonergodic eigenfunctions. It is tantamount to the property of being good for equidistribution, and so it holds by assumption. This fixes the base case. Theorem
5.1
is equivalent to property
(P_{\ell})
.

In the outer inductive step, we thus need to consider averages in which some of the functions are nonergodic eigenfunctions, and hence they are much simpler than arbitrary functions in
L^{\infty}(\mu)
. Therefore, the inductive step will use the following Host-Kra seminorm control assumption. It is strictly weaker than the assumption stated in Theorem
2.9
and
5.1
; but it is all that we need (see the discussion below Definition
2.1
).

Definition 5.3

(Weak Host-Kra seminorm control)
.

We say that
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}

admit weak (Host-Kra) seminorm control
for the system if there exists
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that for all
1\leq m\leq\ell
and
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
with
f_{m+1},\ldots,f_{\ell}
being nonergodic eigenfunctions of
T_{m+1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
respectively, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}T_{j}^{{a_{j}(n)}}f_{j}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0

whenever
\lvert\!|\!|f_{m}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{m}}=0
.

Note that the order of sequences matters in Definition
5.3
. In applications, we order them according to the increasing “degree” (whatever that means in a particular context), so that
a_{j}
has no greater “degree” than
a_{j+1}
.

Example 5.4
.

Weak seminorm control is easier to verify than full Host-Kra seminorm control because nonergodic eigenfunctions quickly disappear in the analytic arguments used to establish seminorm control. To illustrate this point, we
consider the average

\displaystyle\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{n}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{n^{2}}f_{2}.

If
f_{2}
is a nonergodic eigenfunction of
T_{2}
, then two applications of the van der Corput inequality (Lemma
A.1
) followed by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality eliminate
T_{2}^{n^{2}}f_{2}
and allow us to control its
L^{2}(\mu)
limit by
\lvert\!|\!|f_{1}|\!|\!\rvert_{3,T_{1}}
. By contrast, without any assumption of
f_{2}
, we need to first use the PET induction scheme to control the average by
\lvert\!|\!|f_{2}|\!|\!\rvert_{3,T_{2}}
(see Example
5.14
for the details), then use the nonergodic form of the Host-Kra structure theorem to replace
f_{2}
by a function
f_{2}^{\prime}
such that
(f_{2}^{\prime}(T_{2}^{n}x))_{n}
is a 2-step nilsequence pointwise a.e., and then use either multiple applications of the van der Corput lemma or equidistribution results on nilsystems to deduce
\lvert\!|\!|f_{1}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{1}}
control for some
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

5.1.2.
Inductive step: reduction to the degree lowering property

We now present the outer inductive step. For the simplicity of exposition, we assume
\ell=2
, and we shall derive Property
(P_{2})
from Property
(P_{1})
. This case captures all the difficulties of the general case while allowing for a much cleaner presentation.
Then Property
(P_{1})
, which we shall invoke inductively, reduces to the pleasant statement that

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{a_{1}(n)}\chi_{2}-\E(f_{1}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{1}))\cdot\E(\chi_{2}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{2}))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0

whenever
\chi_{2}
is a nonergodic eigenfunction of
T_{2}
.

In the outer inductive step, the goal is to show invariant factor control for averages

(26)

\displaystyle\limsup_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{2}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

The proof consists of the following substeps, each of which is about obtaining sharper seminorm control of the average (
26
):

(i)

(Weak seminorm control) control the average (
26
) by
\lvert\!|\!|f_{2}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{2}}
for some
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
;

(ii)

(Transferring control to weakly structured function) control the average (
26
) by
\lvert\!|\!|F_{2}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{2}}
for a
weakly structured
function
F_{2}
defined below;

(iii)

(Degree lowering) control the average (
26
) by
\lvert\!|\!|F_{2}|\!|\!\rvert_{1,T_{2}}
;

(iv)

(Transferring optimal control
10
10

10

In deriving Property
(P_{m+1})
from
(P_{m})
for general
m,\ell
, at this step we would transfer control to
f_{j}
for any
j\neq m+1
.
to
f_{1}
) control the average (
26
) by
\lvert\!|\!|f_{1}|\!|\!\rvert_{1,T_{1}}
;

(v)

(Extending optimal control to
f_{2}
) control the average (
26
) by
\lvert\!|\!|f_{2}|\!|\!\rvert_{1,T_{2}}
.

As this roadmap suggests, the derivation of
(P_{2})
from
(P_{1})
will involve a lot of back-and-forth between the seminorms of different functions. This is an essential feature of both the degree lowering and seminorm smoothing arguments.

Before we define the weakly structured function
F_{2}
, we make one standing assumption for the argument in this section:

We assume that for all
f_{1},f_{2}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, the average

\displaystyle\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{2}

converges in
L^{2}(\mu)
.

This assumption is not needed; the degree lowering arguments from
[
59
,
69
]
circumvent it by using limsups in inequalities and subsequential weak limit in the definition of
F_{2}
below. However, this assumption allows us to simplify the formula for the weakly structured function
F_{2}
, thus facilitating the exposition.

Weak seminorm control, the first step of the roadmap above, is part of the assumptions. We therefore move on to the second step, i.e. to transferring the control from
f_{2}
to
F_{2}
. The starting point for this step is the lemma below, which also gives the definition of
F_{2}
.

Lemma 5.5

(Stashing: replacing
f_{2}
by a weakly structured function)
.

Let
f_{1},f_{2}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
be 1-bounded.
Then

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{2}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{4}\leq\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{a_{1}(n)}F_{2}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)},

where
11
11

11

Without assuming
L^{2}(\mu)
convergence, we define
F_{2}
as follows. We first take a sequence
(N_{k})
along which the averages
g_{k}:=\E_{n\in[N_{k}]}T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{2}
converge weakly. Then we define
F_{2}
as
weak
limit

\displaystyle F_{2}=\lim_{k\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N_{k}]}T_{2}^{-a_{2}(n)}\overline{g_{k}}\cdot T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}T_{2}^{-a_{2}(n)}\overline{f_{1}}.

This turns out to be sufficient for the applications.

(27)

\displaystyle F_{2}:=\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}T_{2}^{-a_{2}(n)}\overline{f_{0}}\cdot T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}T_{2}^{-a_{2}(n)}\overline{f_{1}}

for

\displaystyle f_{0}:=\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}\overline{f_{1}}\cdot T_{2}^{a_{1}(n)}\overline{f_{2}}.

Proof.

Expanding the square of the
L^{2}(\mu)
norm and composing the resulting integral with
T_{2}^{-a_{2}(n)}
gives

\displaystyle 0<\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{2}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}\int f_{0}\cdot T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{2}\;d\mu

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}\int T_{2}^{-a_{2}(n)}{f_{0}}\cdot T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}T_{2}^{-a_{2}(n)}{f_{1}}\cdot f_{2}\;d\mu

\displaystyle=\int\overline{F_{2}}\cdot f_{2}\;d\mu.

Applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, expanding the inner product, and composing back with
T_{2}^{a_{2}(n)}
, we get

\displaystyle 0<\left\|F_{2}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int\overline{F_{2}}\cdot F_{2}\;d\mu

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}\int T_{2}^{-a_{2}(n)}{f_{0}}\cdot T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}T_{2}^{-a_{2}(n)}{f_{1}}\cdot F_{2}\;d\mu

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}\int f_{0}\cdot T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}{f_{1}}\cdot T_{2}^{a_{2}(n)}F_{2}\;d\mu.

The claim follows from one more application of the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality.
∎

The Cauchy-Schwarz gymnastics performed in the proof of Lemma
5.5
is known in additive combinatorics as
stashing
, a name due to Manners
[
124
]
.

As a consequence of Proposition
5.5
and the weak seminorm control assumption, we get the following auxiliary seminorm control, which completes the second step of the roadmap.

Corollary 5.6

(Seminorm control in terms of weakly structured function)
.

There exists
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that the following holds. Let
f_{1},f_{2}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
. If

(28)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{2}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0,

then
\lvert\!|\!|F_{2}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{2}}>0
.

Note that Corollary
5.6
is equivalent to saying that
\lvert\!|\!|F_{2}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{2}}
controls (
26
).
This contrapositive reformulation turns out to be more handy for degree lowering.

Everything so far has been basic preparatory maneuvers; the essence of the argument lies in the result below.

Proposition 5.7

(Degree lowering)
.

Assume that Property
(P_{1})
holds. If
\lvert\!|\!|F_{2}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{2}}>0
for some
s\geq 2
, then also
\lvert\!|\!|F_{2}|\!|\!\rvert_{s-1,T_{2}}>0
.

The proof of Proposition
5.7
will be sketched in the following subsection. Before, let us see how it can be used to derive property
(P_{2})
from
(P_{1})
, and hence complete the proof of Theorem
5.1
.

Iterating Proposition
5.7

s-1
times forms the inner induction cycle in the double induction scheme that underpins the proof of Theorem
5.1
. It results in the following statement.

Corollary 5.8

(Iterated degree lowering)
.

Assume that Property
(P_{1})
holds. If
\lvert\!|\!|F_{2}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{2}}>0
for some
s\geq 2
, then also
\lvert\!|\!|F_{2}|\!|\!\rvert_{1,T_{2}}>0
.

Combining Corollary
5.8
with Corollary
5.6
, we get the following corollary, which completes the third step in the roadmap.

Corollary 5.9

(Optimal seminorm control in terms of weakly structured function)
.

Assume that Property
(P_{1})
holds. Let
f_{1},f_{2}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
. If (
28
) holds, then
\lvert\!|\!|F_{2}|\!|\!\rvert_{1,T_{2}}>0
.

We thus control the original average (
26
) by the smallest possible Host-Kra seminorm of
F_{2}
. However,
F_{2}
does not appear in the original average, and so this seminorm control is only useful as an intermediate step in controlling (
26
) by degree-1 seminorms of
f_{1},f_{2}
. The remaining two steps of the roadmap are precisely about transferring the optimal seminorm control from
F_{2}
to
f_{1},f_{2}
. First, we do that for
f_{1}
, which is the fourth step of the roadmap.

Proposition 5.10
.

Assume that Property
(P_{1})
holds. Let
f_{1},f_{2}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
.
If (
28
) holds, then
\lvert\!|\!|f_{1}|\!|\!\rvert_{1,T_{1}}>0
.

Proof.

Suppose that (
28
) holds. Then
\lvert\!|\!|F_{2}|\!|\!\rvert_{1,T_{1}}>0
by Corollary
5.9
. Using the formula (
68
) for the degree-1 seminorm as well as the definition (
27
) of
F_{2}
, we deduce that

\lvert\!|\!|F_{2}|\!|\!\rvert_{1,T_{1}}^{2}=\int\overline{F_{2}}\cdot\E(F_{2}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{2}))\;d\mu\\
=\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}\int T_{2}^{-a_{2}(n)}{f_{0}}\cdot T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}T_{2}^{-a_{2}(n)}{f_{1}}\cdot\E(F_{2}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{2}))\;d\mu>0.

Composing the integral with
T_{2}^{a_{2}(n)}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}\int f_{0}\cdot T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}{f_{1}}\cdot T_{2}^{a_{2}(n)}\E(F_{2}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{2}))\;d\mu>0.

Crucially, the function
\E(F_{2}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{2}))
is
T_{2}
-invariant, and so

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}\int(f_{0}\cdot\E(F_{2}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{2})))\cdot T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}{f_{1}}\;d\mu>0.

From the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality we infer that

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}{f_{1}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0.

This new average is now amenable to Property
(P_{1})
(with 1, clearly a nonergodic eigenfunction of
T_{2}
, being the function at the index
2
). By inductively invoking
(P_{1})
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f_{1}|\!|\!\rvert_{1,T_{1}}=\left\|\E(f_{1}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{1}))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0.

∎

Lastly, we establish optimal seminorm control of (
26
) in terms of
f_{2}
. This is the last step in the roadmap, and it completes the proof of Theorem
5.1
modulo the proof of Proposition
5.7
in the next section.

Proposition 5.11
.

Assume that Property
(P_{1})
holds. Let
f_{1},f_{2}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
. If (
28
) holds, then
\lvert\!|\!|f_{2}|\!|\!\rvert_{1,T_{2}}>0
.

Proof.

Suppose that (
28
) holds. We split
f_{1}=\E(f_{1}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{1}))+g_{1}
.
The error term
g_{1}
has vanishing seminorm
\lvert\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!\rvert_{1,T_{1}}
, contributing 0 to (
28
) by the contrapositive of Proposition
5.10
. From this and (
28
) we deduce that

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{{a_{1}(n)}}\E(f_{1}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{1}))\cdot T_{2}^{a_{2}(n)}f_{2}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0,

and hence

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{2}^{a_{2}(n)}f_{2}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0

by the
T_{1}
-invariance of
\E(f_{1}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{1}))
and the Hölder inequality. The conclusion
\lvert\!|\!|f_{2}|\!|\!\rvert_{1,T_{2}}>0
then follows by a mini-rendition of the degree lowering argument that can be found in the proof of
[
59
, Theorem 1.1]
.
∎

5.1.3.
Outline of the proof of Proposition
5.7

We conclude the proof of Theorem
5.1
with the proof of Proposition
5.7
, the deepest and most interesting step in the entire argument. The proofs presented in Section
5.1.2
used rather basic maneuvers: simple applications of the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, measure invariance, and the induction hypothesis; if there were any complications, they came from setting up the induction. By contrast, the proof of Proposition
5.7
involves several formidably technical steps.

We will prove two instances of Proposition
5.7
, corresponding to
s=2,3
. The first of these cases avoids much of the technicalities present in the general case, yet it shows why the degree reduction happens and where Property
(P_{1})
is invoked. The second of these cases will be considerably more technical, but the notation will not be as prohibitive as in the general case. We list these two cases as separate statements and prove them one by one.

The proof that we present below differs from the argument of Frantzikinakis and the author from
[
69
]
; rather, it is based on a later argument of the author in the discrete setting
[
114
]
. The proof in
[
69
]
covers Theorem
2.10
, which is more tricky than Theorem
2.9
and therefore requires a more subtle treatment, one that the relatively simpler argument below does not provide.

Proposition 5.12

(Degree lowering, baby case I)
.

Assume that Property
(P_{1})
holds. If
\lvert\!|\!|F_{2}|\!|\!\rvert_{2,T_{2}}>0
, then also
\lvert\!|\!|F_{2}|\!|\!\rvert_{1,T_{2}}>0
.

For the reader who has not seen the degree lowering argument before, we advise to assume on the first read that
T_{2}
is ergodic. Under this assumption, we can simply take
U
to be all of
X
and
\chi_{2}
to be an eigenfunction of
T_{2}
in the proof that follows. Furthermore, (
33
) and Property
(P_{1})
will imply that
\chi_{2}
is constant.

Proof.

By the inverse theorem for the degree-2 Host-Kra seminorm, there exists a nonergodic eigenfunction
\chi_{2}
of
T_{2}
such that

(29)

\displaystyle\int F_{2}\cdot\overline{\chi_{2}}\;d\mu>0\quad\textrm{and}\quad\E(F_{2}\cdot\overline{\chi_{2}}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{2}))\geq 0

(if
T_{2}
is ergodic, these two properties are the same).
The essence of the proof lies in showing that this can only happen if
\chi_{2}
has special structure, and specifically it is
T_{2}
-invariant on a positive-measure set (if
T_{2}
is ergodic, then
\chi_{2}
has to be constant). Once we establish this property, it will be straightforward to show that
F_{2}
indeed has positive degree-1 Host-Kra seminorm.

The inequalities above imply that there exists
\delta>0
and a
T_{2}
-invariant set
U\subseteq X
such that

(30)

\displaystyle\E(F_{2}\cdot\overline{\chi_{2}}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{2}))(x)\geq\delta\quad\textrm{for all}\quad x\in U;

in other words,
F_{2}\cdot\overline{\chi_{2}}
is bounded away from 0 on a positive proportion of the ergodic components of
\mu
with respect to
T_{2}
(if
T_{2}
is ergodic, then
U=X
, and the conclusion of being bounded away from 0 is immediate from (
29
)).
Hence

\displaystyle\int F_{2}\cdot\overline{\chi_{2}}\cdot 1_{U}\;d\mu=\int\E(F_{2}\cdot\overline{\chi_{2}}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{2}))\cdot 1_{U}\;d\mu>0.

On expanding the definition of
F_{2}
, we have

(31)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}\int T_{2}^{-a_{2}(n)}\overline{f_{0}}\cdot T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}T_{2}^{-a_{2}(n)}\overline{f_{1}}\cdot\overline{\chi_{2}}\cdot 1_{U}\;d\mu>0.

Composing with
T_{2}^{a_{2}(n)}
and taking the complex conjugate, we deduce that

(32)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}\int{f_{0}}\cdot T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{a_{2}(n)}(\chi_{2}\cdot 1_{U})\;d\mu>0.

The Cauchy-Schwarz inequality then gives

(33)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{a_{2}(n)}(\chi_{2}\cdot 1_{U})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0.

Since
\chi_{2}\cdot 1_{U}
is a nonergodic eigenfunction, this average is amenable to the application of Property
(P_{1})
. This is the key step where we reduce the complexity of the original average in a manner that allows us to invoke the induction hypothesis.
Indeed, Property
(P_{1})
combined with (
33
) implies that

\displaystyle\left\|\E(\chi_{2}\cdot 1_{U}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{2}))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0,

and so we can find a
T_{2}
-invariant subset
U^{\prime}\subseteq U
with
\mu(U^{\prime})>0
such that

\mathopen{}\left|\E(\chi_{2}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{2}))(x)\mathclose{}\right|>0

for all
x\in U^{\prime}
. Now, if
\lambda_{2}
is the nonergodic eigenvalue of
\chi_{2}
, then from the pointwise ergodic theorem and the fact that
|\lambda_{2}(x)|\in\{0,1\}
we deduce that

(34)

\begin{split}\E(\chi_{2}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{2}))(x)&=\chi_{2}(x)\cdot\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}\lambda(x)^{n}\\
&=\chi_{2}(x)\cdot 1_{\lambda(x)=1}=\chi_{2}(x)\cdot 1_{\chi_{2}(x)=\chi_{2}(T_{2}x)}\end{split}

for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in U^{\prime}
. Removing the exceptional null set from
U^{\prime}
, we conclude that
\chi_{2}
is
T_{2}
-invariant on
U^{\prime}
.

We have thus established that on a positive-measure set,
\chi_{2}
is
T_{2}
-invariant. Hence
\chi_{2}
possesses an extra structure that it a priori would not need to have. This conclusion plays a key part in degree lowering, and we shall see now how exactly it is applied.
Combining the lower bound (
30
), the inclusion
U^{\prime}\subseteq U
, and the fact that
U^{\prime}
is
T
-invariant and has positive measure, we deduce that

\displaystyle\int F_{2}\cdot\overline{\chi_{2}}\cdot 1_{U^{\prime}}\;d\mu=\int\E(F_{2}\cdot\overline{\chi_{2}}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{2}))\cdot 1_{U^{\prime}}\;d\mu>0.

The function
\overline{\chi_{2}}\cdot 1_{U^{\prime}}
is
T_{2}
-invariant, which allows us to replace
F_{2}
by
\E(F_{2}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{2}))
in the first expression above. The Cauchy-Schwarz inequality then gives

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|F_{2}|\!|\!\rvert_{1,T_{2}}=\left\|\E(F_{2}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{2}))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0,

as claimed

Note that if
T_{2}
is ergodic, then the argument following (
33
) radically simplifies. It follows rather immediately from (
33
) and the invocation of Property
(P_{1})
that
\chi_{2}
is constant. On plugging this back to (
29
), we immediately reach the conclusion that
\lvert\!|\!|F_{2}|\!|\!\rvert_{1,T_{2}}=\mathopen{}\left|\int F_{2}\;d\mu\mathclose{}\right|>0
.
∎

Here is another perspective on the maneuvers carried out in the proof of Proposition
5.12
. We start with the average (
26
) with arbitrary
f_{2}
. After the first stashing, we replace it by the auxiliary function
F_{2}
. This function is “weakly structured” in the sense that it encapsulates the behavior of the original average. In the proof of Proposition
5.12
, we reduce to the average (
26
) in which the place of
f_{2}
is taken by the nonergodic eigenfunction
\chi_{2}\cdot 1_{U}
(see (
32
)), which is “properly structured”. In other words, we progressively replace
f_{2}
by a more and more structured function. To this average we then apply Property
(P_{1})
, the induction hypothesis.

We now state the second baby case of degree lowering, which introduces further technicalities compared to Proposition
5.13
.

Proposition 5.13

(Degree lowering, baby case II)
.

Assume that Property
(P_{1})
holds. If
\lvert\!|\!|F_{2}|\!|\!\rvert_{3,T_{2}}>0
, then also
\lvert\!|\!|F_{2}|\!|\!\rvert_{2,T_{2}}>0
.

Proof.

First, we observe that the inverse theorem for degree-3 Host-Kra seminorm involves much more complicated objects than nonergodic eigenfunctions. To land in the situation where Property
(P_{1})
is applicable, we apply the inductive formula for Host-Kra seminorms. It gives

\displaystyle\lim_{H\to\infty}\E_{h\in[H]}\lvert\!|\!|\Delta_{h{\mathbf{e}}_{2}}F_{2}|\!|\!\rvert_{2,T_{2}}^{4}>0,

where
\Delta_{h{\mathbf{e}}_{2}}f:=f\cdot T_{2}^{h}\overline{f}
.
In particular, there exist
\delta>0
and a set
\Lambda\subseteq{\mathbb{N}}
of positive lower density such that

(35)

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|\Delta_{h{\mathbf{e}}_{2}}F_{2}|\!|\!\rvert_{2,T_{2}}^{4}\geq\delta\quad\textrm{for all}\quad h\in\Lambda.

By the inverse theorem for the degree-2 Host-Kra seminorm, for every
h\in\Lambda
there exists an eigenfunction
\chi_{2,h}
of
T_{2}
such that

\displaystyle\int\Delta_{h{\mathbf{e}}_{2}}F_{2}\cdot{\chi_{2,h}}\;d\mu\geq\delta\quad\textrm{and}\quad\E(\Delta_{h{\mathbf{e}}_{2}}F_{2}\cdot{\chi_{2,h}}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{2}))\geq 0.

We will show once again that the nonergodic eigenfunctions
\chi_{2,h}
admit some special structure, and specifically that on positive-measure sets, they take the form
\chi\cdot\lambda_{h}
for some nonergodic eigenfunctions
\chi
of
T_{2}
and
T_{2}
-invariant
\lambda_{h}
’s. (If
T_{2}
is ergodic, then simply
\chi_{2,h}=\chi
for
h\in\Lambda
.)

By the popularity principle, for every
h\in\Lambda
we can find a
T_{2}
-invariant set
U_{h}
with
\mu(U_{h})\geq\delta/2
such that

(36)

\displaystyle\E(\Delta_{h{\mathbf{e}}_{2}}F_{2}\cdot{\chi_{2,h}}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{2}))(x)\geq\delta/2\quad\textrm{for all}\quad x\in U_{h}.

Hence

(37)

\displaystyle\liminf_{H\to\infty}\E_{h\in[H]}1_{\Lambda}(h)\int\Delta_{h{\mathbf{e}}_{2}}F_{2}\cdot{\chi_{2,h}}\cdot 1_{U_{h}}\;d\mu>0.

The key complication in this case compared to the one covered by Proposition
5.12
is the presence of the multiplicative derivative
\Delta_{h{\mathbf{e}}_{2}}F_{2}
in place of
F_{2}
. We want to move the differentiation “inside” the definition of
F_{2}
. This step, not present in Proposition
5.12
, is called
dual-difference interchange
, and we carry it now.

The rule of thumb in ergodic theory is that if we do not know what to do, it does not hurt to try applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. This is indeed what we will do. First, however, we expand the definition of
T_{2}^{h}F_{2}
(but not of the second
F_{2}
), obtaining

\displaystyle\liminf_{H\to\infty}\E_{h\in[H]}1_{\Lambda}(h)\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}\int F_{2}\cdot T_{2}^{h}\mathopen{}\left(T_{2}^{-a_{2}(n)}{f_{0}}\cdot T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}T_{2}^{-a_{2}(n)}{f_{1}}\mathclose{}\right)\cdot{\chi_{2,h}}\cdot 1_{U_{h}}\;d\mu>0.

Moving the (finite) average over
h
inside the integral and applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we get

\displaystyle\liminf_{H\to\infty}\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}\left\|\E_{h\in[H]}1_{\Lambda}(h)\cdot T_{2}^{h}\mathopen{}\left(T_{2}^{-a_{2}(n)}{f_{0}}\cdot T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}T_{2}^{-a_{2}(n)}{f_{1}}\mathclose{}\right)\cdot{\chi_{2,h}}\cdot 1_{U_{h}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}>0.

We then expand the square to get the nasty inequality

\liminf_{H\to\infty}\limsup_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}\E_{h,h^{\prime}\in[H]}1_{\Lambda^{2}}(h,h^{\prime})\\
\int T_{2}^{h}\mathopen{}\left(T_{2}^{-a_{2}(n)}{f_{0}}\cdot T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}T_{2}^{-a_{2}(n)}{f_{1}}\mathclose{}\right)\cdot T_{2}^{h^{\prime}}\mathopen{}\left(T_{2}^{-a_{2}(n)}\overline{f_{0}}\cdot T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}T_{2}^{-a_{2}(n)}\overline{f_{1}}\mathclose{}\right)\\
\chi_{2,h}\cdot\overline{\chi_{2,h^{\prime}}}\cdot 1_{U_{h}}\cdot 1_{U_{h^{\prime}}}\;d\mu>0.

Because we started with a degree-3 Host-Kra seminorm, the dual-difference interchange required only one application of the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality to perform dual-difference interchange. If we started with the degree-
s
seminorm,
s-2
applications would be needed. Given how much notational mess one Cauchy-Schwarz has introduced, we leave it to the reader’s vivid imagination to envisage the nightmare that the general case of dual-difference interchange leads to.

Fortunately, we are able to make the notation more palatable by a few manipulations. Composing the integral with
T_{2}^{-h^{\prime}}
, defining

\displaystyle f_{j,h,h^{\prime}}:=\Delta_{(h-h^{\prime}){\mathbf{e}}_{2}}\overline{f_{j}},\quad\chi_{2,h,h^{\prime}}:=T_{2}^{-h^{\prime}}(\chi_{2,h}\cdot\overline{\chi_{2,h^{\prime}}}),\quad U_{h,h^{\prime}}:=U_{h}\cap U_{h^{\prime}},

and swapping the limsup over
N
with the average over
h,h^{\prime}
, we get

\liminf_{H\to\infty}\E_{h,h^{\prime}\in[H]}1_{\Lambda^{2}}(h,h^{\prime})\\
\limsup_{N\to\infty}\mathopen{}\left|\E_{n\in[N]}\int T_{2}^{-a_{2}(n)}f_{0,h,h^{\prime}}\cdot T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}T_{2}^{-a_{2}(n)}{f_{1,h,h^{\prime}}}\cdot\chi_{2,h,h^{\prime}}\cdot 1_{U_{h,h^{\prime}}}\;d\mu\mathclose{}\right|>0.

This expression looks quite like (
31
), and we proceed analogously to the proof of Proposition
5.12
. Composing the integral with
T_{2}^{a_{2}(n)}
and applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality gives us

\liminf_{H\to\infty}\E_{h,h^{\prime}\in[H]}1_{\Lambda^{2}}(h,h^{\prime})\limsup_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}{f_{1,h,h^{\prime}}}\cdot T_{2}^{a_{2}(n)}(\chi_{2,h,h^{\prime}}\cdot 1_{U_{h,h^{\prime}}})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0.

The functions
\chi_{2,h,h^{\prime}}\cdot 1_{U_{h,h^{\prime}}}
are nonergodic eigenfunctions of
T_{2}
, and so invoking Property
(P_{1})
much the same way as in the proof of Proposition
5.12
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\liminf_{H\to\infty}\E_{h,h^{\prime}\in[H]}1_{\Lambda^{2}}(h,h^{\prime})\left\|\E(\chi_{2,h,h^{\prime}}\cdot 1_{U_{h,h^{\prime}}}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{2}))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0.

By the pigeonhole principle, we can find a single
12
12

12

Technically, this
h^{\prime}
may vary with
H
, but we omit this technicality in order not to clutter the notation.

h^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
for which

\displaystyle\liminf_{H\to\infty}\E_{h\in[H]}1_{\Lambda}(h)\left\|\E(\chi_{2,h,h^{\prime}}\cdot 1_{U_{h,h^{\prime}}}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{2}))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0.

Just like in Proposition
5.12
, we can find
T_{2}
-invariant sets
U^{\prime}_{h}\subseteq U_{h,h^{\prime}}
for which

(38)

\displaystyle\liminf_{H\to\infty}\E_{h\in[H]}1_{\Lambda}(h)\mu(U^{\prime}_{h})>0,

and such that
\chi_{2,h,h^{\prime}}
is
T_{2}
-invariant on
U^{\prime}_{h}
. Given the formula for
\chi_{2,h,h^{\prime}}
, this means that
\chi_{2,h}=\chi\cdot\lambda_{h}
(where we set
\chi:=\chi_{2,h^{\prime}}
) on
U^{\prime}_{h}
for some
T_{2}
-invariant
\lambda_{h}
. Just like in Proposition
5.12
, we have uncovered some structure on the eigenfunctions
\chi_{2,h}
although this time the structure is in a sense “shared” across different
h
.

We now combine the lower bound (
36
) and (
38
) with the fact that
U^{\prime}_{h}\subseteq U_{h}
is
T_{2}
-invariant to infer that

\displaystyle\liminf_{H\to\infty}\E_{h\in[H]}1_{\Lambda}(h)\int\E(\Delta_{h{\mathbf{e}}_{2}}F_{2}\cdot{\chi_{2,h}}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{2}))\cdot 1_{U^{\prime}_{h}}\;d\mu>0.

Using the special structure of
\chi_{2,h}
on
U^{\prime}_{h}
and the
T_{2}
-invariance of
U^{\prime}_{h}
, we deduce that

(39)

\displaystyle\liminf_{H\to\infty}\E_{h\in[H]}1_{\Lambda}(h)\int\Delta_{h{\mathbf{e}}_{2}}F_{2}\cdot\chi\cdot\lambda_{h}\cdot 1_{U^{\prime}_{h}}\;d\mu>0.

We invite the reader to compare this expression with (
37
