), the starting point, to see how arbitrary
T_{2}
-eigenfunctions
\chi_{2,h}
have been replaced by the more structured ones.

We are almost at the end of the argument; all that remains is to remove the “low complexity” terms
1_{\Lambda}(h)\cdot\chi\cdot\lambda_{h}\cdot 1_{U^{\prime}_{h}}
from the integral. By applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality in
h
, we can remove
\chi
, getting

\displaystyle\liminf_{H\to\infty}\left\|\E_{h\in[H]}T_{2}^{h}\overline{F_{2}}\cdot 1_{\Lambda}(h)\cdot\lambda_{h}\cdot 1_{U^{\prime}_{h}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}>0.

Expanding the square and using the
T_{2}
-invariance of
1_{\Lambda}(h)\cdot\lambda_{h}\cdot 1_{U^{\prime}_{h}}
, we further infer from the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality that

\displaystyle\liminf_{H\to\infty}\E_{h,h^{\prime}\in[H]}\left\|\E(T_{2}^{h}F_{2}\cdot T_{2}^{h^{\prime}}\overline{F_{2}}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{2}))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0.

A simple exercise shows that this implies
\lvert\!|\!|F_{2}|\!|\!\rvert_{2,T_{2}}>0,
as claimed.

Note that if
T_{2}
is ergodic, then from (
38
), we simply get that
\chi_{2,h}=\chi
for the positive-lower-density set
h\in\Lambda
. Then the conclusion of (
39
) simplifies to

\displaystyle\liminf_{H\to\infty}\E_{h\in[H]}1_{\Lambda}(h)\int\Delta_{h{\mathbf{e}}_{2}}F_{2}\cdot\chi\;d\mu=\liminf_{H\to\infty}\int F_{2}\cdot\chi\cdot\E_{h\in[H]}T_{2}^{h}\overline{F_{2}}\cdot 1_{\Lambda}(h)\;d\mu>0.

The Cauchy-Schwarz inequality gives

\displaystyle\lim_{H\to\infty}\left\|\E_{h\in[H]}T_{2}^{h}\overline{F_{2}}\cdot 1_{\Lambda}(h)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0,

and by expanding the square and pulling the indicators out of the integral, it is easy to conclude that
\lvert\!|\!|F_{2}|\!|\!\rvert_{2,T_{2}}>0
.
∎

5.1.4.
Other degree lowering arguments

The argument presented above is one of four existing degree lowering arguments to prove Theorem
2.9
and its variants (Theorem
2.8
and
2.10
), in addition to those from
[
59
,
68
,
69
]
(see also
[
32
]
for a variant of the degree lowering from
[
59
]
carried out for more general ring actions). While they all differ slightly at the level of details, the overarching strategy in all of them is more or less the same. Specifically, all of them use the inverse theorem for the degree-2 Host-Kra seminorm to reduce the seminorm degree and aim at degree-1 control or its variant (rational Kronecker factor control). The degree lowering philosophy is, however, so robust that it can be used to reach other “optimal seminorm control” statements. Theorems
6.9
,
6.13
,
6.14
, and
6.20
are also proved via degree lowering arguments that use other inverse theorems as their starting points and therefore reach different conclusions.

5.2.
New techniques for seminorm control

The second big breakthrough that we now outline are new ways to prove Host-Kra seminorm control. Previously available technology relied on variants of the PET induction scheme of Bergelson
[
12
]
(see Example
5.14
), and it was only capable of delivering seminorm control in two cases of interest:

(i)

in the single-transformation case, for “essentially distinct” sequences;

(ii)

in the commuting case, for sequences of “distinct growth”.

Current machinery blends the PET induction scheme with concatenation and seminorm smoothing techniques, yielding three types of results:

(i)

Host-Kra seminorm estimates for “pairwise independent” sequences in the commuting case;

(ii)

Host-Kra seminorm estimates for general (i.e. potentially “pairwise dependent”) sequences in the commuting case under additional ergodicity assumptions on the system;

(iii)

box seminorm estimates in the commuting case.

In this survey, we will only present results of the first (Proposition
5.19
and Theorem
5.20
) and third type (Theorem
5.18
). For a result of the second type, we direct the reader to
[
67
, Theorem 1.1]
or
[
44
, Theorem 4.10]
.

5.2.1.
PET induction scheme: strengths and limitations

The one and only way of proving Host-Kra seminorm control before the advent of new techniques was the PET induction scheme. It relies on methodically applying the van der Corput and Cauchy-Schwarz inequalities to an average until we reach one with linear iterates that can be controlled via Host-Kra seminorms as in Example
1.6
.
The following example illustrates this procedure.

Example 5.14

(PET for
(T_{1}^{n},T_{2}^{n^{2}})_{n}
)
.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},T_{2})
be a system, and let
f_{1},f_{2}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
. Suppose that

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{n}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{n^{2}}f_{2}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0.

By the van der Corput inequality (Lemma
A.1
),

\displaystyle\limsup_{H_{1}\to\infty}\E_{h_{1}\in[H_{1}]}\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathopen{}\left|\E_{n\in[N]}\int T_{1}^{n}f_{1}\cdot T_{1}^{n+h_{1}}\overline{f}_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{n^{2}}f_{2}\cdot T_{2}^{(n+h_{1})^{2}}f_{2}\;d\mu\mathclose{}\right|>0.

Composing the average with
T_{1}^{-n}
, we get

\displaystyle\limsup_{H_{1}\to\infty}\E_{h_{1}\in[H_{1}]}\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathopen{}\left|\E_{n\in[N]}\int f_{1}\cdot T_{1}^{h_{1}}\overline{f}_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{n^{2}}T_{1}^{-n}f_{2}\cdot T_{2}^{(n+h_{1})^{2}}T_{1}^{-n}\overline{f}_{2}\;d\mu\mathclose{}\right|>0.

We then apply the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality to eliminate the terms independent of
n
, obtaining

\displaystyle\limsup_{H_{1}\to\infty}\E_{h_{1}\in[H_{1}]}\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{2}^{n^{2}}T_{1}^{-n}f_{2}\cdot T_{2}^{(n+h_{1})^{2}}T_{1}^{-n}\overline{f}_{2}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0.

This new average may look more complicated than the one we started with, not least because it involves two quadratic polynomials rather than one. However, a key observation is that both quadratic polynomials have the same leading coefficients. This relative simplicity of the new average compared to the original one is a baby example of the
complexity reduction
that underpins the PET induction scheme, and it will come in handy shortly.

Applying the van der Corput lemma once more, we deduce that

\limsup_{H_{2}\to\infty}\E_{h_{2}\in[H_{2}]}\limsup_{H_{1}\to\infty}\E_{h_{1}\in[H_{1}]}\\
\lim_{N\to\infty}\left|\E_{n\in[N]}\int T_{2}^{n^{2}}T_{1}^{-n}f_{2}\cdot T_{2}^{(n+h_{2})^{2}}T_{1}^{-(n+h_{2})}\overline{f}_{2}\right.\\
\left.\vphantom{\E_{n\in[N]}\int}T_{2}^{(n+h_{1})^{2}}T_{1}^{-n}\overline{f}_{2}\cdot T_{2}^{(n+h_{1}+h_{2})^{2}}T_{1}^{-(n+h_{2})}f_{2}\;d\mu\right|>0.

Because all four terms have the same leading coefficient in
n
, composing the integral with
T_{1}^{n}T_{2}^{-n^{2}}
allows us to reduce the average to one linear in
n
, giving

\limsup_{H_{2}\to\infty}\E_{h_{2}\in[H_{2}]}\limsup_{H_{1}\to\infty}\E_{h_{1}\in[H_{1}]}\lim_{N\to\infty}\\
\mathopen{}\left|\E_{n\in[N]}\int f_{2}\cdot T_{2}^{2h_{2}n+h_{2}^{2}}T_{1}^{-h_{2}}\overline{f}_{2}\cdot T_{2}^{2h_{1}n+h_{1}^{2}}\overline{f}_{2}\cdot T_{2}^{2(h_{1}+h_{2})n+(h_{1}+h_{2})^{2}}T_{1}^{-h_{2}}f_{2}\;d\mu\mathclose{}\right|>0.

Crucially, for all
h_{1},h_{2}\in{\mathbb{N}}
except the zero-density set
\{(h_{1},h_{2})\in{\mathbb{N}}\colon h_{1}=h_{2}\}
, the leading coefficients
2h_{1},2h_{2},2(h_{1}+h_{2})
are all different. Therefore an application of the bound (
7
) jointly with the scaling property (
66
) implies that
\lvert\!|\!|f_{2}|\!|\!\rvert_{3,T_{2}}>0
. The claimed seminorm control follows by contrapositive.

There are many variants of the argument above. For instance, by using a quantitative version of the van der Corput lemma, we can ensure that
H_{1},H_{2}
are the same; we can also let
H
grow slowly with
N
. By taking
H_{1},H_{2}
large but finite, we can also obtain a quantitative statement of the form

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{n}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{n^{2}}f_{2}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{O(1)}\ll\lvert\!|\!|f_{2}|\!|\!\rvert_{3,T_{2}}

under the assumptions of 1-boundedness on
f_{1},f_{2}
.

Unfortunately, the argument above fails to give Host-Kra seminorm control even for
(T_{1}^{n^{2}},T_{2}^{n^{2}+n})_{n}
. In this case, analogous manipulations only allow us to control the corresponding multiple ergodic average by an average of box seminorms.

Example 5.15

(PET for
(T_{1}^{n^{2}},T_{2}^{n^{2}+n})_{n}
)
.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},T_{2})
be a system, and let
f_{1},f_{2}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
be 1-bounded. Then

(40)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{n^{2}}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{n^{2}+n}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{O(1)}\ll\limsup_{H\to\infty}\E_{h_{1},h_{2},h_{3}\in[H]}\lvert\!|\!|f_{2}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{c}}_{1}(h),\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{7}(h)},

where the vectors
{\mathbf{c}}_{1}(h),\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{7}(h)
are given by

\displaystyle 2{\mathbf{e}}_{2}h_{1},2({\mathbf{e}}_{2}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1})h_{2},2({\mathbf{e}}_{2}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1})h_{3},

\displaystyle 2{\mathbf{e}}_{2}h_{1}+2({\mathbf{e}}_{2}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1})h_{2},2{\mathbf{e}}_{2}h_{1}+2({\mathbf{e}}_{2}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1})h_{3},2({\mathbf{e}}_{2}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1})(h_{2}+h_{3}),

\displaystyle 2{\mathbf{e}}_{2}h_{1}+({\mathbf{e}}_{2}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1})(h_{2}+h_{3}).

A priori, this is much worse than the conclusion of Example
5.14
since there is ostensibly no good reason why the beastly expression on the right-hand side of (
40
) would be of any use. Yet the explicit description of the vectors
{\mathbf{c}}_{1}(h),\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{7}(h)
offers certain hope.
Two crucial points that we shall exploit later is that these vectors are linear polynomials in
h
(for polynomials of higher degree than
n^{2},n^{2}+n
, they will be multilinear), and their coefficients are (multiples of) the leading coefficients of the polynomials
{\mathbf{e}}_{2}(n^{2}+n)
and
{\mathbf{e}}_{2}(n^{2}+n)-{\mathbf{e}}_{1}n^{2}
.

The computation performed in Example
5.15
can be found in
[
43
, Example 7]
(for an alternative presentation in the discrete setting, see also
[
114
, Example 2]
or
[
112
, Section 4.1]
). In
[
43
, Section 4]
, Donoso, Ferré-Moragues, Koutsogiannis, and Sun developed a general
coefficient tracking scheme
(later refined by Kravitz, Leng and the author
[
112
, Section 4]
; see also
[
45
, Section 7.1]
)) that allows us to upper bound

(41)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T^{\mathbf{a}_{1}(n)}f_{1}\cdots T^{\mathbf{a}_{\ell}(n)}f_{\ell}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}

for some
\ell
-tuples
\mathbf{a}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{\ell}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}
of polynomials, Hardy sequences, etc.
by an
average of box seminorms
of the form

\displaystyle\lim_{H\to\infty}\E_{h\in[H]^{s}}\lvert\!|\!|f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{c}}_{1}(h),\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{s}(h)},

where
{\mathbf{c}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{s}
are multilinear polynomials in
h
whose coefficients depend only on the ‘‘leading coefficients’’
13
13

13

This notion means precisely what one thinks it does for polynomials, but it is trickier to define for Hardy sequences. See
[
45
, Theorem 10.1]
and the discussion underneath for illustration.
of
\mathbf{a}_{\ell},\mathbf{a}_{\ell}-\mathbf{a}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{\ell}-\mathbf{a}_{\ell-1}
. The statements of such results, each wrapped in a formidable amount of formalism and terminology, can be found e.g. in
[
43
, Proposition 5.3]
,
[
112
, Proposition 4.9]
, and
[
45
, Proposition 7.7]
.

5.2.2.
Concatenation: control by a single box seminorm

The estimate obtained in Example
5.15
looks rather daunting. It turns out, however, that we can control the troublesome average of box seminorms on the right-hand side of (
40
) by a single box seminorm using a technique known as
concatenation
. Its end result is presented below.

Example 5.16

(Concatenation for
(T_{1}^{n^{2}},T_{2}^{n^{2}+n})_{n}
)
.

Let
{\mathbf{c}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{7}
be the polynomials showing up in (
40
). Then there exists
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that

(42)

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left(\limsup_{H\to\infty}\E_{h_{1},h_{2},h_{3}\in[H]}\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{c}}_{1}(h),\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{7}(h)}\mathclose{}\right)^{O(1)}\ll\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{e}}_{2}^{\times s},({\mathbf{e}}_{2}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1})^{\times s}}

(recall that
{\mathbf{e}}_{2}^{\times s}
denotes
s
copies of
{\mathbf{e}}_{2}
).

The idea behind the estimate (
42
) is to
merge
(“concatenate”) different directions
{\mathbf{c}}_{1}(h),\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{7}(h)
with each other in a way that allows us to recover the
principal directions

{\mathbf{e}}_{2},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1}
. The modern way to do this (performed e.g. in
[
45
,
112
,
114
]
) is to apply the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality many times to the left-hand side of (
42
) in a targeted way. A priori, this upper bounds the left-hand side of (
42
) by a much larger and complicated-looking average of box seminorms. If done correctly, however, the directions of the new seminorms are “equidistributed” as the parameters
h
vary. Using elementary counting arguments, we can then replace these equidistributed directions by
{\mathbf{e}}_{2},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1}
, or the group
\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{2},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1}\rangle
. We present a very simple case of this argument below, warning the reader that the general case involves a considerably more complicated induction.

Example 5.17

(Simple example of concatenation)
.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},T_{2})
be a system, and let
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
be 1-bounded.
We show that

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left(\limsup_{H\to\infty}\E_{h_{1},h_{2}\in[H]}\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{e}}_{1}h_{1}+{\mathbf{e}}_{2}h_{2}}^{2}\mathclose{}\right)^{2}\ll\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{1},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\rangle,\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{1},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\rangle},

and hence the right-hand side can be bounded by
\min\limits_{j=1,2}\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{e}}_{j},{\mathbf{e}}_{j}}
using (
66
).
Let

\displaystyle\delta

\displaystyle:=\limsup_{H\to\infty}\E_{h_{1},h_{2}\in[H]}\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{e}}_{1}h_{1}+{\mathbf{e}}_{2}h_{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\limsup_{H\to\infty}\E_{h_{1},h_{2}\in[H]}\lim_{M\to\infty}\E_{m\in[M]}\int f\cdot T_{1}^{h_{1}m}T_{2}^{h_{2}m}\overline{f}\;d\mu

\displaystyle=\limsup_{H\to\infty}\lim_{M\to\infty}\int f\cdot\E_{h_{1},h_{2}\in[H]}\E_{m\in[M]}T_{1}^{h_{1}m}T_{2}^{h_{2}m}\overline{f}\;d\mu.

By applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, expanding the square, and changing variables, we get

\displaystyle\delta^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{H\to\infty}\limsup_{M\to\infty}\int\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{11},h_{12},\\
h_{21},h_{22}\in[H]\end{subarray}}\E_{m_{1},m_{2}\in[M]}T_{1}^{h_{11}m_{1}}T_{2}^{h_{21}m_{1}}{f}\cdot T_{1}^{h_{12}m_{2}}T_{2}^{h_{22}m_{2}}\overline{f}\;d\mu

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{H\to\infty}\limsup_{M\to\infty}\int\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{11},h_{12},\\
h_{21},h_{22}\in[H]\end{subarray}}\E_{m_{1},m_{2}\in[M]}{f}\cdot T_{1}^{h_{12}m_{2}-h_{11}m_{1}}T_{2}^{h_{22}m_{2}-h_{21}m_{1}}\overline{f}\;d\mu.

We repeat this procedure once more to get

\delta^{4}\leq\limsup_{H\to\infty}\limsup_{M\to\infty}\\
\int\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{1j},h_{2j}\in[H]:\\
1\leq j\leq 4\end{subarray}}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{j}\in[M]:\\
1\leq j\leq 4\end{subarray}}{f}\cdot T_{1}^{h_{14}m_{4}-h_{13}m_{3}-h_{12}m_{2}+h_{11}m_{1}}T_{2}^{h_{24}m_{4}-h_{23}m_{3}-h_{22}m_{2}+h_{21}m_{1}}\overline{f}\;d\mu.

The key point is that as
h_{1j},h_{2j}
’s vary over
[H]
and
m_{j}
’s vary over
[M]
, then the polynomial

(43)

\displaystyle(h_{14}m_{4}-h_{13}m_{3}-h_{12}m_{2}+h_{11}m_{1},h_{24}m_{4}-h_{23}m_{3}-h_{22}m_{2}+h_{21}m_{1})

is “equidistributed” over its range
[\pm 2HM]^{2}
in the sense that each element
(n_{1},n_{2})\in[\pm 2HM]^{2}
admits at most
O(1)
times the expected number of representations of the form (
43
) (for large
H,M
). This bound, which we do not prove here, follows from a much more general estimate
[
112
, Proposition 7.5]
.
What happens is that two applications of the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality give us more variables to play with at the cost of losing a power of
\delta
, and the polynomials in more variables (like (
43
)) tend to be more uniformly distributed over their range than those in fewer variables (like the original polynomial
(h_{1}m,h_{2}m)
).

Plugging this equidistribution property into the last inequality, we get

\displaystyle\delta^{4}\ll\limsup_{H\to\infty}\limsup_{M\to\infty}\E_{n_{1},n_{2}\in[\pm 2HM]}\mathopen{}\left|\int f\cdot T_{1}^{n_{1}}T_{2}^{n_{2}}\overline{f}\;d\mu\mathclose{}\right|.

One more application of the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality gives

\displaystyle\delta^{8}\ll\limsup_{M\to\infty}\E_{n_{1},n_{2}\in[\pm M]}\mathopen{}\left|\int f\cdot T_{1}^{n_{1}}T_{2}^{n_{2}}\overline{f}\;d\mu\mathclose{}\right|^{2}=\lvert\!|\!|f\otimes\overline{f}|\!|\!\rvert_{\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{1},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\rangle}^{2}\leq\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{1},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\rangle,\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{1},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\rangle}^{4},

where we leave the last inequality as an exercise.

The original concatenation argument was developed by Tao and Ziegler
[
139
]
to study polynomial progressions in primes. It differed from the argument above in that it was about concatenating
factors
, not seminorms, and hence it was very qualitative in nature. This argument was later applied in the works of Donoso, Koutsogiannis, and Sun
[
46
,
47
]
as well as Donoso, Ferré-Moragues, Koutsogiannis, and Sun
[
43
]
to give
qualitative
comparison control averages of box seminorms
by a single box seminorm. In particular, in
[
43
, Example 8]
, they show the following qualitative version of (
40
):

(44)

\displaystyle\limsup_{H\to\infty}\E_{h_{1},h_{2},h_{3}\in[H]}\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{c}}_{1}(h),\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{7}(h)}=0\quad\textrm{whenever}\quad\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{e}}_{2}^{\times s},({\mathbf{e}}_{2}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1})^{\times s}}=0.

In a different direction, Peluse and Prendiville
[
132
]
and Peluse
[
130
]
developed the first
quantitative concatenation
argument in their study of single-dimensional polynomial progressions. A more flexible version of this argument was later developed by the author
[
114
]
in the context of multidimensional polynomial patterns over the finite fields, and later extended to the integer setting jointly with Kravitz and Leng
[
112
]
. The argument was subsequently translated to ergodic theory by Donoso, Koutsogiannis, Sun, Tsinas, and the author
[
45
]
. The exposition provided above is based on the argument stemming from
[
45
,
112
,
114
]
.

By combining the PET induction scheme from Section
5.2.1
with the concatenation arguments outlined above, we obtain the following rather general statement.

Theorem 5.18

(Box seminorm control for general polynomial averages)
.

Let
\mathbf{a}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}[t]
be essentially distinct. Then there exist
C>0
,
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and vectors
{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{v}}_{s}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}
belonging to the set of leading coefficients of
{\mathbf{e}}_{\ell},{\mathbf{e}}_{\ell}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{e}}_{\ell}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1}
, such that for all systems
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
and all 1-bounded functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T^{\mathbf{a}_{1}(n)}f_{1}\cdots T^{\mathbf{a}_{\ell}(n)}f_{\ell}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{C}\leq C\lvert\!|\!|f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{v}}_{s}}.

Theorem
5.18
is a special case of
[
45
, Proposition 8.6]
. Its discrete version can be found in
[
112
, Theorem 1.1]
, and its qualitative version was previously established in
[
43
, Theorem 2.5]
. Theorem
5.18
extends to (tuples of) Hardy sequences (see
[
45
, Theorem 10.1]
). However, this extension is much more challenging to state because the notion of leading coefficients for Hardy sequences is more tricky, and because we need a more robust notion of box seminorms (defined in
[
45
, Section 3]
) for tuples like
(T_{1}^{{\lfloor\sqrt{2}n^{2}\rfloor}},T_{2}^{{\lfloor\sqrt{3}n^{2}+n\rfloor}})_{n}
in which the leading coefficient
\sqrt{3}{\mathbf{e}}_{2}-\sqrt{2}{\mathbf{e}}_{1}
of the difference
{\lfloor\sqrt{3}n^{2}+n\rfloor}{\mathbf{e}}_{2}-{\lfloor\sqrt{2}n^{2}\rfloor}{\mathbf{e}}_{1}
has rationally independent entries.

5.2.3.
Seminorm smoothing

By combining the arguments from Examples
5.15
and
5.16
, we obtain the following inequality:

(45)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{n^{2}}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{n^{2}+n}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{O(1)}\ll\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{e}}_{2}^{\times s},({\mathbf{e}}_{2}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1})^{\times s}},

which looks much nicer than (
40
). Unfortunately, it is not very useful in itself, as we know no useful inverse theorems for the box seminorm
\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{e}}_{2}^{\times s},({\mathbf{e}}_{2}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1})^{\times s}}
for
s>1
. The last step, therefore, is to upgrade (
45
) to a proper Host-Kra seminorm estimate. This is done via a
seminorm smoothing
technique: it consists in “smoothing” the seminorm
\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{e}}_{2}^{\times s},({\mathbf{e}}_{2}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1})^{\times s}}
by replacing each vector
{\mathbf{e}}_{2}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1}
one-by-one by (possibly many) copies of the vector
{\mathbf{e}}_{2}
. In this particular example, this result takes the following form.

Proposition 5.19
.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},T_{2})
be a system and
f_{1},f_{2}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
be 1-bounded. Let
s_{1},s_{2}\in{\mathbb{N}}
. There exists
s_{1}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
so that if

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left(\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{n^{2}}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{n^{2}+n}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\mathclose{}\right)^{O(1)}\ll\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{e}}_{2}^{\times s_{1}},({\mathbf{e}}_{2}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1})^{\times s_{2}}},

then also

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left(\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{n^{2}}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{n^{2}+n}f_{2}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\mathclose{}\right)^{O(1)}\ll\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{e}}_{2}^{\times s^{\prime}_{1}},({\mathbf{e}}_{2}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1})^{\times(s_{2}-1)}},

Iterating Proposition
5.19
, we can get rid of all copies of
{\mathbf{e}}_{2}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1}
.

The proof of Proposition
5.19
uses a lot of tricks similar to degree lowering, but the underlying philosophy is different: we do not lower the overall degree of the seminorm, only the number of the problematic vectors
{\mathbf{e}}_{2}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1}
. We present the proof of Proposition
5.19
in the relatively simple case
(s_{1},s_{2})=(0,1)
, noting that the general case requires nasty maneuvers like dual-difference interchange present in the proof of Proposition
5.13
. For more exposition of seminorm smoothing, consult
[
69
, Section 8]
,
[
67
, Section 4]
, or
[
44
, Section 4.2]
.

Proof for
(s_{1},s_{2})=(0,1)
.

Let

(46)

\displaystyle\delta:=\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{n^{2}}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{n^{2}+n}f_{2}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

By Lemma
5.5
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{n^{2}}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{n^{2}+n}F_{2}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\geq\delta^{4},

where

\displaystyle F_{2}:=\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}T_{2}^{-n^{2}-n}\overline{f_{0}}\cdot T_{1}^{n^{2}}T_{2}^{-n^{2}-n}\overline{f_{1}}

for some 1-bounded
f_{0}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
.
Our assumption gives
\lvert\!|\!|F_{2}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{e}}_{2}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1}}\gg\delta^{O(1)}.
Using the inverse theorem for the degree-1 box seminorm (
71
), we have

\displaystyle\int F_{2}\cdot\overline{u_{2}}\;d\mu\gg\delta^{O(1)}

for
u_{2}=\E(\overline{F_{2}}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{2}T_{1}^{-1}))
.
Expanding the definition of
F_{2}
, taking complex conjugates, and composing the resulting integral with
T_{2}^{n^{2}+n}
, we get

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}\int f_{0}\cdot T_{1}^{n^{2}}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{n^{2}+n}u_{2}\;d\mu\gg\delta^{O(1)}.

Much like in degree lowering, we have replaced the arbitrary function
f_{2}
by a special function
u_{2}
. The
T_{2}T_{1}^{-1}
-invariance of
u_{2}
means that
T_{2}^{n^{2}+n}u_{2}=T_{1}^{n^{2}+n}u_{2}
, and hence

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}\int f_{0}\cdot T_{1}^{n^{2}}f_{1}\cdot T_{1}^{n^{2}+n}u_{2}\;d\mu\gg\delta^{O(1)}.

This is the essence of seminorm smoothing: to reduce the original average of commuting transformations to one involving a single transformation. Using known Host-Kra seminorm estimates for the averages in the single-transformation case, we deduce that
\lvert\!|\!|f_{1}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{1}}\gg\delta^{O(1)}
for some
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
. In other words, we have shown that
\lvert\!|\!|f_{1}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{1}}
controls the original average with polynomial bounds.

So far, we have passed from box seminorm control for
f_{2}
to Host-Kra seminorm control for
f_{1}
. In the next step, we will transfer seminorm control back to
f_{1}
. Because of this going back-and-forth between seminorms of
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
, we have called these two steps
ping
and
pong
in
[
69
]
.

We proceed to the
pong
step. Defining

\displaystyle F_{1}:=\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{-n^{2}}\overline{f_{0}}\cdot T_{2}^{n^{2}+n}T_{1}^{-n^{2}}\overline{f_{2}},

we deduce once again from Lemma
5.5
applied to the original average that

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{n^{2}}F_{1}\cdot T_{1}^{n^{2}+n}f_{2}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\geq\delta^{4}.

Applying the seminorm estimate from the
ping
step with
F_{1}
in place of
f_{1}
, we deduce that
\lvert\!|\!|F_{1}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{1}}\gg\delta^{O(1)}
. By the weak inverse theorem for the Host-Kra seminorms (
68
), we have

\displaystyle\int F_{1}\cdot{\mathcal{D}}\;d\mu\gg\delta^{O(1)},

where
{\mathcal{D}}={\mathcal{D}}_{s,T_{1}}(F_{1})
is the degree-
s
dual function of
F_{1}
. Expanding the definition of
F_{1}
and composing the resulting integral with
T_{1}^{n^{2}+n}
, we get

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}\int f_{0}\cdot T_{1}^{n^{2}}\overline{{\mathcal{D}}}\cdot T_{2}^{n^{2}+n}f_{2}\;d\mu\gg\delta^{O(1)}.

The point now is that the sequence
(T_{1}^{n}\overline{{\mathcal{D}}})_{n}
is a so-called
dual sequence
, and it behaves in many ways like a nilsequence. In particular, it was shown in
[
54
, Proposition 6.1]
that finitely many applications of the Cauchy-Schwarz and van der Corput inequalities completely eliminate this term from the average at the cost of duplicating
T_{2}^{n^{2}+n}f_{2}
each time the inequalities are applied. As an end result of this procedure, we get an expression like

\displaystyle\lim_{H\to\infty}\E_{h\in[H]^{s}}\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\prod_{\epsilon\in\{0,1\}^{s}}T_{2}^{(n+\epsilon\cdot h)^{2}+(n+\epsilon\cdot h)}f_{2}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\gg\delta^{O(1)}.

For a full-density set of
h
’s, this is an average of essentially distinct polynomials of a single transformation, and so seminorm estimates in the single-transformation case combined with a quantitative concatenation argument like the one outlined in Section
5.2.2
give the claimed conclusion
\lvert\!|\!|f_{2}|\!|\!\rvert_{s^{\prime}_{1},T_{2}}\gg\delta^{O(1)}
for some
s^{\prime}_{1}\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
∎

The seminorm smoothing argument was developed by Frantzikinakis and the author to prove Theorem
3.15
(
i
), seminorm estimates for pairwise independent integer polynomials. At that time, the quantitative concatenation arguments from
[
45
,
112
,
114
]
were not yet known, and so the proof concluded with the qualitative conclusion from Theorem
3.15
(
i
). The fully quantitative Host-Kra seminorm estimates were provided by Donoso, Koutsogiannis, Sun, Tsinas, and the author
[
45
, Theorem 1.16]
for pairwise independent Hardy sequences. This result used essentially the same seminorm smoothing argument, but with a much more robust PET argument and quantitative rather than qualitative concatenation.

Theorem 5.20

(Quantitative Host-Kra seminorm estimates for pairwise independent Hardy sequences)
.

For any pairwise independent
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{H}}
, we can find
C>0
and
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
so that for any system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
and any 1-bounded functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left(\limsup_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{{\lfloor a_{1}(n)\rfloor}}f_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{{\lfloor a_{\ell}(n)\rfloor}}f_{\ell}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\mathclose{}\right)^{C}\leq C\min_{1\leq j\leq\ell}\lvert\!|\!|f_{j}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{j}}.

5.2.4.
Pairwise dependent case and relative concatenation

For ergodic averages along pairwise independent sequences, one can typically hope for Host-Kra seminorm control in accordance with Heuristic
3.3
. For pairwise dependent sequences, not only is this normally not possible, but in fact there seem to be no “optimal” seminorm estimates. For instance, the works of Donoso, Ferré-Moragues, Koutsogiannis, and Sun
[
43
]
as well as Donoso, Koutsogiannis, Sun, Tsinas and the author
[
44
]
establish two different box seminorm estimates for averages along pairwise dependent polynomials.

Example 5.21

(Different seminorm estimates for
(T_{1}^{n^{2}},T_{2}^{n^{2}},T_{3}^{n^{2}+n})_{n}
)
.

As a special case of Theorem
5.18
, it follows that

(47)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{n^{2}}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{n^{2}}f_{2}\cdot T_{3}^{n^{2}+n}f_{3}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0

whenever
\lvert\!|\!|f_{3}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{e}}_{3}^{\times s},({\mathbf{e}}_{3}-{\mathbf{e}}_{2})^{\times s},({\mathbf{e}}_{3}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1})^{\times s}}=0
for some
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
. By contrast,
[
44
, Theorem 4.5]
gives (
47
) whenever
\lvert\!|\!|f_{3}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{e}}_{3}^{\times s^{\prime}},({\mathbf{e}}_{2}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1})^{\times s^{\prime}}}=0
for some
s^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
The two estimates are not comparable as each box seminorm involves some vectors absent from the second one.

Any of these two seminorm estimates (and analogous estimates for
f_{1},f_{2}
) can be used to show that if
{\mathcal{I}}(T_{1}^{-1}T_{2})={\mathcal{I}}(T_{1})\cap{\mathcal{I}}(T_{2})
, i.e. the only sets invariant under
T_{1}^{-1}T_{2}
are those simultaneously invariant under
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
, then the average in (
47
) admits Host-Kra seminorm control. Different proofs of this assertion can be found in
[
67
]
and
[
44
]
. This condition is essentially sharp, in the sense that if there exists a
T_{1}^{-1}T_{2}
-invariant
f
that is not
T_{1},T_{2}
-invariant, then on taking
f_{1}=f
,
f_{2}=\overline{f_{2}}
,
f_{3}=1
, we get

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{n^{2}}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{n^{2}}f_{2}\cdot T_{3}^{n^{2}+n}f_{3}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{n^{2}}|f_{1}|^{2}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)},

which is controlled by
\lvert\!|\!||f_{1}|^{2}|\!|\!\rvert_{2,T_{1}}
, but in general not by
\lvert\!|\!|f_{1}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{1}}
for any
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

In
[
67
]
, Frantzikinakis and the author developed a more sophisticated variant of the seminorm smoothing argument with the goal of obtaining Host-Kra seminorm control for averages involving pairwise dependent polynomials under some necessary invariance assumptions. The Host-Kra seminorm control result mentioned in Example
5.21
is a special case. Unfortunately, this argument relies on a delicate induction that breaks for sequences that are pairwise dependent with irrational coefficients, like
(T_{1}^{{\lfloor\sqrt{2}n^{2}\rfloor}},T_{2}^{{\lfloor\sqrt{3}n^{2}\rfloor}},T_{3}^{n^{2}+n})_{n}
. In
[
44
, Section 4]
, Donoso, Koutsogiannis, Sun, Tsinas, and the author found a more robust way of running the seminorm smoothing argument in the pairwise dependent context, one that extends to Hardy sequences. As already mentioned above, this method gives different box seminorm estimates for averages along Hardy sequences than those provided by Theorem
5.18
. Those new estimates played a key role in the proof of Theorem
4.7
, the difficult direction of the joint ergodicity classification problem for Hardy sequences.

One important technique that the smoothing argument from
[
44
]
added to the toolbox is
relative concatenation
. In their foundational work on concatenation, Tao and Ziegler
[
139
, Theorem 1.16]
showed that for any subgroups
G_{1},\ldots,G_{s},G_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,G_{s}^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}
,

(48)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Z}}(G_{1},\ldots,G_{s})\cap{\mathcal{Z}}(G_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,G_{s^{\prime}}^{\prime})\subseteq{\mathcal{Z}}(G_{i}+G^{\prime}_{j}\colon\;1\leq i\leq s,\;1\leq j\leq s^{\prime}),

i.e. an intersection of two box factors lies in a box factor of higher degree but larger groups. For instance, if
s=s^{\prime}=2
, then

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Z}}(G_{1},G_{2})\cap{\mathcal{Z}}(G_{1}^{\prime},G_{2}^{\prime})\subseteq{\mathcal{Z}}(G_{1}+G_{1}^{\prime},G_{1}+G_{2}^{\prime},G_{2}+G_{1}^{\prime},G_{2}+G_{2}^{\prime}).

Such a result is particularly useful if the groups
G_{i}+G_{j}^{\prime}
are much larger than
G_{i},G_{j}^{\prime}
individually, e.g. if
G_{i}+G_{j}^{\prime}={\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}
for all
i,j^{\prime}
. For instance, if
\ell=2
,
G_{i}=\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{1}\rangle
and
G_{j}^{\prime}=\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\rangle
for all
i,j^{\prime}
, then (
48
) gives that

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Z}}_{s-1}({\mathbf{e}}_{1})\cap{\mathcal{Z}}_{s^{\prime}-1}({\mathbf{e}}_{2})\subseteq{\mathcal{Z}}_{s+s^{\prime}-1}({\mathbb{Z}}^{2}).

An important corollary of (
48
) is
[
139
, Corollary 1.21]
, which asserts that any
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
can be decomposed as
f_{1}+f_{2}+f_{3}
, where

\displaystyle\E(f_{1}|{\mathcal{Z}}(G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}))=0,\quad\E(f_{2}|{\mathcal{Z}}(G_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,G_{s^{\prime}}^{\prime}))=0

\displaystyle\E(f_{3}|{\mathcal{Z}}(G_{i}+G_{j}^{\prime}\colon\;1\leq i\leq s,\;1\leq j\leq s^{\prime}))=0.

In
[
44
, Theorem 3.1]
, Donoso, Koutsogiannis, Sun, Tsinas, and the author obtain a relative version of this result: for any subgroups
H_{1},\ldots,H_{r}\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}
, we get the following generalization of (
48
):

(49)

{\mathcal{Z}}(H_{1},\ldots,H_{r},G_{1},\ldots,G_{s})\cap{\mathcal{Z}}(H_{1},\ldots,H_{r},G_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,G_{s^{\prime}}^{\prime})\\
\subseteq{\mathcal{Z}}(H_{1},\ldots,H_{r},G_{i}+G_{j}^{\prime}\colon\;1\leq i\leq s,\;1\leq j\leq s^{\prime}).

For instance,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Z}}(H,G_{1},G_{2})\cap{\mathcal{Z}}(H,G_{1}^{\prime},G_{2}^{\prime})\subseteq{\mathcal{Z}}(H,G_{1}+G_{1}^{\prime},G_{1}+G_{2}^{\prime},G_{2}+G_{1}^{\prime},G_{2}+G_{2}^{\prime}).

While (
49
) may seem like a small improvement over (
48
), the corresponding decomposition result for functions found a vital application in the seminorm smoothing argument for pairwise dependence sequences from
[
44
]
. We demonstrate its utility with the following example; for a more robust example, we encourage the reader to consult
[
44
, Section 4.2]
.

Example 5.22
.

We want to show how the second estimate from Example
5.21
can be deduced from the first. Suppose that we know that for some values
s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we have (
47
) whenever

(50)

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f_{3}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{e}}_{3}^{\times s_{1}},({\mathbf{e}}_{2}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1})^{\times s_{2}},({\mathbf{e}}_{3}-{\mathbf{e}}_{2})^{\times s_{3}}}=0.

(Such an estimate forms an intermediate step of passing between two estimates from Example
5.21
. We arrive at it after removing all the vectors
{\mathbf{e}}_{3}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1}
at the cost of getting some vectors
{\mathbf{e}}_{2}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1}
and increasing the number of vectors
{\mathbf{e}}_{3}
.)
To obtain the desired estimate, we want to get rid of the vectors
{\mathbf{e}}_{3}-{\mathbf{e}}_{2}
altogether. In the process of running the
ping
and
pong
step of the seminorm smoothing argument, we establish that (
47
) also holds whenever

(51)

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f_{3}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{e}}_{3}^{\times s_{1}^{\prime}},({\mathbf{e}}_{2}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1})^{\times s_{2}^{\prime}},({\mathbf{e}}_{3}-{\mathbf{e}}_{2})^{\times(s_{3}-1)},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}^{\times s_{4}^{\prime}}}=0

for some
s_{1}^{\prime},s_{2}^{\prime},s_{3}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Without loss of generality, we can assume that
s_{1}=s_{1}^{\prime}
and
s_{2}=s_{2}^{\prime}
; if not, we simply replace them with
\max(s_{1},s_{1}^{\prime}),\max(s_{2},s_{2}^{\prime})
respectively and use the monotonicity property of box seminorms.

We now want to use relative concatenation to get that (
47
) holds whenever

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f_{3}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{e}}_{3}^{\times(s_{1}+s_{4}^{\prime})},({\mathbf{e}}_{2}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1})^{\times s_{2}},({\mathbf{e}}_{3}-{\mathbf{e}}_{2})^{\times(s_{3}-1)}}=0.

Since
{\mathbf{e}}_{3}\in\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{3}-{\mathbf{e}}_{2},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\rangle
, the claim will follow from (
66
) if we can show that (
47
) holds whenever

(52)

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f_{3}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{e}}_{3}^{\times s_{1}},({\mathbf{e}}_{2}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1})^{\times s_{2}},({\mathbf{e}}_{3}-{\mathbf{e}}_{2})^{\times(s_{3}-1)},\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{3}-{\mathbf{e}}_{2},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\rangle^{\times s_{4}^{\prime}}}=0.

Indeed, if (
52
) holds, then using

{\mathcal{Z}}({\mathbf{e}}_{3}^{\times s_{1}},({\mathbf{e}}_{2}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1})^{\times s_{2}},({\mathbf{e}}_{3}-{\mathbf{e}}_{2})^{\times s_{3}})\cap{\mathcal{Z}}({\mathbf{e}}_{3}^{\times s_{1}},({\mathbf{e}}_{2}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1})^{\times s_{2}},({\mathbf{e}}_{3}-{\mathbf{e}}_{2})^{\times(s_{3}-1)},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}^{\times s_{4}^{\prime}})\\
\subseteq{\mathcal{Z}}({\mathbf{e}}_{3}^{\times s_{1}},({\mathbf{e}}_{2}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1})^{\times s_{2}},({\mathbf{e}}_{3}-{\mathbf{e}}_{2})^{\times(s_{3}-1)},\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{3}-{\mathbf{e}}_{2},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\rangle^{\times s_{4}^{\prime}}),

a special form of (
49
), we can split
f_{3}=f_{3}^{\prime}+f_{3}^{\prime\prime}
, where
f_{3}^{\prime}
satisfies (
50
) while
f_{3}^{\prime\prime}
satisfies (
51
). Using our previous two estimates, we thus deduce that both
f_{3}^{\prime}
and
f_{3}^{\prime\prime}
contribute 0 to the average, and hence does
f_{3}
.

Despite sharing many features, the proofs and statements of concatenation of factors differ markedly from those for quantitative concatenation. It would be interesting to find a uniform perspective on both types of results, leading to the rather open question below.

Problem 23

(Uniform perspective on concatenation)
.

Can we find a single concatenation result that implies both the results on concatenation of factors, proved in
[
44
,
139
]
and presented in Section
5.2.4
, and the results on quantitative concatenation, proved in
[
45
,
112
,
114
,
130
,
132
]
and presented in Section
5.2.2
?

6.
Beyond joint ergodicity

Joint ergodicity is the dream scenario, one where the limit is as simple and explicit as it could be. But what if it is not there? This section aims to briefly summarize the main results and open problems on the limits of multiple ergodic averages outside the realm of joint ergodicity.

6.1.
Identities for arithmetic progressions and corners

We start with a case that is as far from joint ergodicity as possible: when all the sequences are the same, or at least multiples of each other. In this case, we can hope to describe a limit neatly by comparison as explained in Section
1.2.3
. The properties that we shall investigate in this subsection are summarized in the following definition.

Definition 6.1

(Good behavior along APs and corners)
.

We say that
a:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
is:

(i)

well-behaved along
\ell
-APs
14
14

14

Of course, “
\ell
-APs” stand for “
\ell
-term arithmetic progressions”.
for the system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
if

(53)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}T^{ja(n)}f_{j}-\E_{n\in[N]}\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}T^{jn}f_{j}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0

holds for all
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
;

(ii)

well-behaved along APs for the system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
if it is well-behaved along
\ell
-APs for the system for any
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
;

(iii)

well-behaved along
\ell
-corners for the system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
if

(54)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}T_{j}^{a(n)}f_{j}-\E_{n\in[N]}\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}T_{j}^{n}f_{j}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0

for all
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
;

(iv)

well-behaved along corners for the system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
if it is well-behaved along
\ell
-corners for the system for any
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
;

(v)

well-behaved along (
\ell
-)APs/corners
if it is well-behaved along (
\ell
-)APs/corners for any system.

In
[
68
]
, a property very similar to good behavior along
\ell
-APs was called “being good for mean convergence along
\ell
-APs”.

This section deals with the following rather general problems.

Problem 24
.

Let
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
. When is a sequence
a:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
well-behaved along
\ell
-APs for a system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
?

Problem 25
.

Let
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
. When is a sequence
a:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
well-behaved along
\ell
-corners for a system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
?

We can consider many variants of Problems
24
and
25
: we can fix
\ell
and the system, or we may want the good behavior property to hold for all
\ell
, all systems, or both.

In general, good behavior along
\ell
-APs does not imply good behavior along
(\ell+1)
-APs: to see this, take
a(n)
to be the indicator function of
\{n\in{\mathbb{N}}\colon 1/4<\left\|\sqrt{2}n^{\ell+1}\right\|<1/2\}
(see
[
70
, Theorem A]
or the remark below
[
68
, Theorem 3.4]
for explanation).

6.1.1.
Good behavior along APs for specific sequences

For integer polynomials and primes, i.e. sequences not uniformly distributed in residue classes, Frantzikinakis, Frantzikinakis-Host-Kra, as well as Moreira-Richter proved the following results.

Theorem 6.2

(
[
51
, Theorem A]
)
.

Any nonconstant
a\in{\mathbb{Z}}[t]
is well-behaved along APs for any totally ergodic system.

For the necessity of total ergodicity, recall Example
1.3
.

Theorem 6.3
.

The sequence of primes is well-behaved along APs for any totally ergodic system.

For 2-APs, Theorem
6.3
was proved by Frantzikinakis, Host, and Kra
[
60
, Theorem 5]
, who also outlined the proof of the general case conditional on the Gowers uniformity of the modified von Mangoldt function. A slightly different proof of the general case was provided by Moreira and Richter
[
127
, Theorem 2.9]
.

For Hardy sequences that are good for equidistribution, Frantzikinakis established the following.

Theorem 6.4

(
[
53
, Theorem 2.2]
)
.

Any
a\in{\mathcal{H}}
satisfying

(55)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathopen{}\left|\frac{a(t)-cp(t)}{\log t}\mathclose{}\right|=\infty\quad\textrm{for\; all}\quad c\in{\mathbb{R}},\;p\in{\mathbb{Q}}[t]

is well-behaved along APs.

Koutsogiannis and Tsinas combined Theorem
6.4
with Theorem
3.40
to obtain an analogous result when the Hardy sequence is sampled along primes.

Theorem 6.5

(
[
108
, Theorem 1.3]
)
.

Let
a\in{\mathcal{H}}
satisfy (
55
). Then
a(p_{n})
is well-behaved along APs, where we recall that
{\mathbb{P}}=\{p_{1}<p_{2}<\cdots\}
.

Both Theorems
6.4
and
6.5
also hold when
a\in{\mathcal{H}}
is replaced by
a\in{\mathcal{T}}
; see
[
53
, Section 2.1.5]
and
[
108
, Theorem 7.4]
.

Since Theorems
6.4
and
6.5
make no assumptions on the system, they yield new extensions of the Szemerédi theorem (a similar comment holds for Theorem
6.13
below).

Note that the assumption (
55
) is stronger than (
18
), as in (
55
) we require
a
to stay logarithmically away from all
real
multiples of rational polynomials. This assumption is there to prevent
irrational obstructions
to equidistribution, and its necessity is evidenced by the following example.

Example 6.6
.

Take
a(n)=\sqrt{2}n
, and consider
Tx=x+\sqrt{2}
on
X={\mathbb{T}}
. Setting
f(x)=e(x)
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}f(T^{{\lfloor\sqrt{2}n\rfloor}}x)

\displaystyle=f(x)\cdot\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}e(\sqrt{2}{\lfloor\sqrt{2}n\rfloor})

\displaystyle=f(x)\cdot\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}e(-\sqrt{2}\{\sqrt{2}n\})

\displaystyle=f(x)\cdot\int_{0}^{1}e(-\sqrt{2}x)\;dx\neq 0

by Weyl’s equidistribution theorem whereas the pointwise ergodic theorem gives

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}f(T^{n}x)=0.

As shown by Moreira and Richter, the sequence
\sqrt{2}n
is well-behaved along APs as long as particular irrational obstructions do not arise in the system.

Theorem 6.7

(
[
127
, Theorem 2.5]
)
.

Let
a(n)=cn+d
for
c\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+},d\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Then the sequence
a
is well-behaved along APs for any system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
satisfying the spectral condition
\spec(T)\cap\frac{1}{c}{\mathbb{Z}}=\{0\}
.

It would be interesting to know whether Theorem
6.7
extends to other real polynomials not covered by Theorem
6.4
, or to multiples of primes.

Problem 26
.

Let
a(n)=cp(n)+d
for some
c\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+},d\in{\mathbb{R}}
, and nonconstant
p\in{\mathbb{Q}}[t]
. Is the sequence
a
well-behaved along APs for any system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
satisfying the spectral condition
\spec(T)\cap\frac{1}{c}{\mathbb{Q}}=\{0\}
?

Problem 27
.

Let
a(n)=cp_{n}+d
for some
c\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+},d\in{\mathbb{R}}
, where
(p_{n})_{n}
is the sequence of primes. Is the sequence
a
well-behaved along APs for any system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
satisfying the spectral condition
\spec(T)\cap\frac{1}{c}{\mathbb{Q}}=\{0\}
?

Moreira-Richter’s method proceeds by analyzing the spectrum of the multicorrelation sequences arising on a given system. Specifically, their main technical tool is the following spectral refinement of Bergelson-Host-Kra’s decomposition
[
18
, Theorem 1.9]
. We refer the reader to Appendix
B.4
for definitions.

Theorem 6.8

(Spectral refinement of BHK decomposition
[
127
, Theorem 2.1]
)
.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
be a system and
f_{0},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
. For every
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a decomposition

\displaystyle\int f_{0}\cdot T^{n}f_{1}\cdots T^{\ell n}f_{\ell}\;d\mu=\phi(n)+\omega(n)+\gamma(n),

where
\omega
is a null-sequence,
\left\|\gamma\right\|_{\infty}\leq\varepsilon
, and
\phi(n)=F(S^{n}y)
for some nilmanifold
(Y,{\mathcal{Y}},\nu,S)
with
\spec(S)\subseteq\spec(T)
,
y\in Y
, and
F\in C(Y)
.

It is unclear to what extent Theorem
6.8
extends to sparse sequences.
The following question was hinted at in
[
127
]
, at least for integer polynomials.

Problem 28
.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
be a system and
f_{0},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
. Let
a:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
be any of the following: (i) the sequence of primes; (ii) a nonconstant integer polynomial; (iii) (the integer part) of a Hardy sequence. Can we find for every
\varepsilon>0
a decomposition

\displaystyle\int f_{0}\cdot T^{a(n)}f_{1}\cdots T^{\ell a(n)}f_{\ell}\;d\mu=\phi(n)+\omega(n)+\gamma(n),

where
\omega
is a null-sequence,
\left\|\gamma\right\|_{\infty}\leq\varepsilon
, and
\phi(n)=F(S^{n}y)
for some nilmanifold
(Y,{\mathcal{Y}},\nu,S)
with
\spec(S)\subseteq\spec(T)
,
y\in Y
, and
F\in C(Y)
?

6.1.2.
General criteria for good behavior along APs

The results mentioned so far address Problem
24
for rather concrete examples of sequences. In
[
68
]
, Frantzikinakis and the author provide an equivalent characterization for good behavior along APs for general sequences.

Theorem 6.9

(Equivalent characterization of good behavior along APs
[
68
, Theorem 3.11]
)
.

Let
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
. A sequence
a:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
is well-behaved along
\ell
-APs for the system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
if and only if the following two conditions hold:

(i)

the sequences
a,2a,\ldots,\ell a
admit Host-Kra seminorm control for
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
;

(ii)

a
is well-behaved along
\ell
-APs for
(X,{\mathcal{Z}}_{\ell}(T),\mu,T)
.

Theorem
6.9
amounts to saying the following: if

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T^{a(n)}f_{1}\cdots T^{\ell a(n)}f_{\ell}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}

admits Host-Kra seminorm control, then the identity (
53
) holds for all functions if and only if it holds for
{\mathcal{Z}}_{\ell}(T)
-measurable functions. While the assumption of Host-Kra seminorm control reduces the task of verifying (
53
) to asserting it for all
{\mathcal{Z}}_{s}(T)
-measurable functions for some possibly large
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
, Theorem
6.9
says that it suffices to consider
{\mathcal{Z}}_{\ell}(T)
-measurable functions.

Theorem
6.9
gives two global corollaries that reduce the good behavior along APs to equidistribution on nilsystems.

Theorem 6.10

(Criteria for convergence along APs - general systems
[
68
, Theorem 3.1]
)
.

Let
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
a\colon{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
be a sequence. Assume that
a,2a,\ldots,\ell a
admit Host-Kra seminorm control for all systems
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
. Then the following properties are equivalent:

(i)

a
is well-behaved along
\ell
-APs for all systems.

(ii)

a
is well-behaved along
\ell
-APs
for all ergodic
\ell
-step nilsystems.

(iii)

a
equidistributes on all
\ell
-step nilsystems
(Y,{\mathcal{Y}},\nu,S)
.

Theorem 6.11

(Criteria for convergence along
\ell
-term APs - totally ergodic systems
[
68
, Theorem 3.2]
)
.

Let
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
a\colon{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
be a sequence. Assume that
a,2a,\ldots,\ell a
admit Host-Kra seminorm control for all systems
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
. Then the following properties are equivalent:

(i)

a
is well-behaved along
\ell
-APs for all totally ergodic systems.

(ii)

a
is well-behaved along
\ell
-APs for all totally ergodic
\ell
-step nilsystems.

Furthermore, both properties are implied by the following one:

(iii)

a
equidistributes on all totally ergodic
\ell
-step nilsystems
(Y,{\mathcal{Y}},\nu,S)
.

6.1.3.
Good behavior along corners

While there are many results towards Problem
24
, very little is known for Problem
25
. The main reason is that it is notoriously hard to find useful inverse theorems for the box seminorms that arise in the study of the averages

\displaystyle\limsup_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{a(n)}f_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{a(n)}f_{\ell}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

In particular, the following problem stands unresolved.

Problem 29
.

Show that any Hardy sequence
a\in{\mathcal{H}}
satisfying (
55
) is well-behaved along
\ell
-corners for any
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}.

It is not even known if

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{{\lfloor n^{3/2}\rfloor}}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{{\lfloor n^{3/2}\rfloor}}f_{2}-\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{n}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{n}f_{2}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0;

nor even if the average on the left converges in norm.

Recently, Daskalakis made the following progress towards Problem
29
.

Theorem 6.12

(
[
40
, Theorem 1.1]
)
.

For any
b\in(1,23/22)
, the sequence
n^{b}
is well-behaved along 2-corners.

In fact, the result holds if
n^{b}
is replaced by any
b
-regularly varying function with
b
in this range (see
[
40
, Definition 1.3]
). Daskalakis’ method is very finitary; it proceeds by expressing the original average as a weighted ergodic average

\displaystyle\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{{\lfloor n^{b}\rfloor}}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{{\lfloor n^{b}\rfloor}}f_{2}=\E_{n\in[N^{c}]}w_{N,n}\cdot T_{1}^{n}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{n}f_{2},

and then establishing the Gowers uniformity of the difference
w_{N,n}-1
. The fact that the weight
w_{N,n}
is far from being 1-bounded is one reason why the author can only consider
b
just slightly better than 1 to make sure that all the quantitative estimates yield cancellations.

Soon after, Frantzikinakis and the author removed the restriction on
b
from Theorem
6.12
at the cost of taking a weaker mode of convergence.

Theorem 6.13

(Limiting formula for Hardy corners
[
66
, Theorem 1.1]
)
.

For every Hardy sequence
a\in{\mathcal{H}}
satisfying (
55
), every system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},T_{2})
, and all functions
f_{0},f_{1},f_{2}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have the identity

(56)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\,\int f_{0}\cdot T_{1}^{{\lfloor a(n)\rfloor}}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{{\lfloor a(n)\rfloor}}f_{2}\,d\mu=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\,\int f_{0}\cdot T_{1}^{n}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{n}f_{2}\,d\mu.

Interestingly, Theorem
6.13
only establishes an identity of
weak
limits, which is enough to yield recurrence along Hardy corners. The method very quickly breaks when applied to strong limits or longer corners (see
[
66
, Section 3.4]
for discussion). This is one of the rare examples in the study of multiple ergodic averages when a result for weak limits cannot easily be upgraded to strong limits.

The same paper establishes a double limiting identity for polynomials.

Theorem 6.14

(Limiting formula for polynomial corners
[
66
, Theorem 1.2]
)
.

For every nonconstant
a\in{\mathbb{Z}}[t]
with zero constant terms, every system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},T_{2})
, and all functions
f_{0},f_{1},f_{2}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have the identity

(57)

\lim_{k\to\infty}\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\,\int f_{0}\cdot T_{1}^{a(k!n)}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{a(k!n)}f_{2}\;d\mu\\
=\lim_{k\to\infty}\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\,\int f_{0}\cdot T_{1}^{k!n}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{k!n}f_{2}\,d\mu.

A slight modification of the left-hand side of (
57
) yields a double limiting identity for all intersective polynomials; see
[
66
, Theorem 1.2]
for the details. Combined with a result of Chu
[
37
, Theorem 1.1]
, Theorems
6.13
and
6.14
establish a variant of the popular common difference results for sparse corners
[
66
, Theorems 1.4 and 1.5]
.

The limitations of the method leading up to Theorem
6.13
motivate the following two subcases of Problem
29
.

Problem 30
.

Extend (
56
) to an identity for
L^{2}(\mu)
limits.

Problem 31
.

Extend (
56
) and (
57
) to 3-corners.

The proof of Theorem
6.13
proceeds by a variant of the degree lowering argument. It gives rather general sufficient conditions on a sequence
a:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
so that the identity

(58)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}\int f_{0}\cdot T_{1}^{a(n)}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{a(n)}f_{2}\,d\mu=\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}\int f_{0}\cdot T_{1}^{n}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{n}f_{2}\,d\mu

holds for all systems. Before we state this result, we need one more definition.

Definition 6.15

(Box seminorm control)
.

We say that
a\colon{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
:
admits box seminorm control for the system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
if there exists
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that for all
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{a(n)}f_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{a(n)}f_{\ell}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0

whenever
\lvert\!|\!|f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{T_{\ell}^{\times s},(T_{\ell}T_{\ell-1}^{-1})^{\times s},\ldots,(T_{\ell}T_{1}^{-1})^{\times s}}=0
.

Theorem 6.16

(
[
66
, Theorem 3.3]
)
.

Suppose that
a\colon{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
admits box seminorm control on every system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},T_{2})
and equidistributes on nilsystems
(Y,{\mathcal{Y}},\nu,S)
. Then the identity (
58
) holds for every system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},T_{2})
and all functions
f_{0},f_{1},f_{2}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
.

A variant of Theorem
6.16

[
66
, Theorem 3.4]
gives Theorem
6.14
.

It is unclear whether the two conditions from Theorem
6.16
will suffice for

(59)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}\int f_{0}\cdot T_{1}^{a(n)}f_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{a(n)}f_{\ell}\,d\mu=\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}\int f_{0}\cdot T_{1}^{n}f_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{n}f_{\ell}\,d\mu

for
\ell>2
. While the box seminorm control assumption will surely be needed, it is likely that equidistribution on nilsystems may not be enough to guarantee (
59
). This is an interesting open problem, as it ties with the important question of understanding inverse theorems for box seminorms.

Problem 32
.

If
a\colon{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
admits box seminorm control on every system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu
,
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
and equidistributes on nilsystems
(Y,{\mathcal{Y}},\nu,S)
, does it necessarily satisfy (
59
) for all
f_{0},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
?

There are grounds to believe that even for
\ell\geq 3
, we may need to deal with proper “multidimensional” obstructions that go beyond nilsystems. See
[
66
, Theorem 3.5]
for a flavor of those.

6.2.
Optimal characteristic factors in the single-transformation case

As explained in Section
1
, in the absence of joint ergodicity and nice identities, the best we can do is to identify a factor or a seminorm that controls an average of interest. As a consequence of the seminorm estimates presented throughout this survey, we know that large classes of multiple ergodic averages are controlled by some Host-Kra factor. Since these factors are nested (see (
70
)), and the first two of them admit a much more explicit description than the rest, it is often useful to identify the
optimal
characteristic factor for a given average (this can be used, e.g., to get new results on popular common differences). For polynomial averages of a single transformation, variants of this question have been studied by Bergelson, Leibman, and Lesigne
[
22
]
, Leibman
[
121
]
, Frantzikinakis
[
51
]
, and the author
[
113
]
. Before presenting open questions and conjectures, we introduce several definitions to organize the presentation.

Definition 6.17

(Host-Kra and Weyl complexities of polynomial families)
.

Let
s\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
. We say that essentially distinct polynomials
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[t]
have

(i)

Host-Kra complexity
s
if
{\mathcal{Z}}_{s}(T)
the smallest Host-Kra factor that is characteristic for
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
along any totally ergodic system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
;

(ii)

Weyl complexity
s
if
{\mathcal{Z}}_{s}(T)
the smallest Host-Kra factor that is characteristic for
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
along any totally ergodic Weyl system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
.

The nomenclature employed in Definition
6.17
comes from
[
113
]
; previous works called the aforementioned notions differently, in some cases also shifting the indices (e.g. Weyl complexity 2 according to Definition
6.17
corresponds to Weyl complexity 3 in
[
51
]
).
We assume total ergodicity in the definition so that affinely independent polynomials have Weyl complexity 0, which would fail due to local obstructions if we considered only ergodic systems.

A
Weyl system
is an ergodic system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
, where
X
is a compact abelian Lie group and
T
is a
unipotent affine transformation
on
X
, i.e.
Tx=\phi(x)+a
for
a\in X
and an automorphism
\phi
of
X
satisfying
(\phi-\rm{Id}_{X})^{s}=0
for some
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
. The examples to have in mind are skew products

\displaystyle T(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})=(x_{1}+\alpha,x_{2}+x_{1},\ldots,x_{d}+x_{d-1})

on
X={\mathbb{T}}^{d}
for some
\alpha\notin{\mathbb{Q}}
. The motivation to study complexity over Weyl systems comes from
Leibman’s equidistribution theorem, which asserts that a polynomial orbit is equidistributed on a connected nilmanifold if and only if it is equidistributed on its maximal-factor torus
[
119
,
120
]
. Frantzikinakis and Kra then showed that for a connected, ergodic (i.e. totally ergodic) nilsystem, its maximal factor torus is a Weyl system
[
64
, Proposition 3.1]
. Thereafter, it has been conjectured on many occasions
[
121
, Section 0.10]
,
[
22
, Section 0.B]
,
[
56
, Section 5.1.1]
,
[
113
, Conjecture 1.9]
that these two notions of complexity should be the same.

Problem 33
.

Show that for any essentially distinct polynomials, Host-Kra and Weyl complexities are the same.

For 3 polynomials, Problem
33
has been resolved by Frantzikinakis
[
51
]
, but it remains open even for 4 polynomials.

Informed by works of Gowers and Wolf on true complexity in additive combinatorics
[
77
,
78
,
79
,
80
]
, the author introduced the notion of algebraic complexity
[
113
]
.

Definition 6.18
.

Essentially distinct polynomials
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[t]
have
algebraic complexity
s
if
s
is the smallest nonnegative integer such that the progression

(60)

\displaystyle m,\;m+a_{1}(n),\;\ldots,\;m+a_{\ell}(n)

is
algebraically independent of degree
s+1
, in that there are no polynomials
Q_{j}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[t]
with
\max\limits_{0\leq j\leq\ell}\deg Q_{j}=s+1
such that
15
15

15

The work of Bergelson, Leibman, and Lesigne
[
22
]
has an alternative presentation of this condition in terms of a matrix involving the polynomials
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
rather than the progression (
60
). We believe, however, that the formulation using algebraic relations (
61
) is conceptually clearer. To motivate how such progressions arise out of the polynomial family, we expand the average as

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1}\cdots T^{a_{\ell}(n)}f_{\ell}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}\int f_{0}\cdot T^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1}\cdots T^{a_{\ell}(n)}f_{\ell}\;d\mu

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{m,n\in[N]}\int T^{m}f_{0}\cdot T^{m+a_{1}(n)}f_{1}\cdots T^{m+a_{\ell}(n)}f_{\ell}\;d\mu

for some
f_{0}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
using measure invariance.

(61)

\displaystyle Q_{0}(m)+Q_{1}(m+a_{1}(n))+\cdots+Q_{\ell}(m+a_{\ell}(n))=0.

Example 6.19
.

The algebraic complexity equals:

(i)

0 for affinely independent polynomials like
n,\;n^{2},\;\ldots,\;n^{\ell}
;

(ii)

\ell-1
for
n,\;2n,\;\ldots,\;\ell n
; for instance, for
\ell=3
, we have the relationship

\displaystyle m^{2}-3(m+n)^{2}+3(m+2n)^{2}-(m+3n)^{2}=0;

(iii)

1 for
n,\;n^{2},\;n+n^{2}
because they satisfy the linear relation

\displaystyle m-(m+n)-(m+n^{2})+(m+n+n^{2})=0;

but no quadratic relation.

(iv)

2 for the family
n,\;2n,\;n^{2}
because of the quadratic relation

\displaystyle(m^{2}+2m)-2(m+n)^{2}+(m+2n)^{2}-2(m+n^{2})=0.

This relation is “inhomogeneous” in that it is quadratic in the first three terms and linear in the last one. Studying complexity for such families is difficult, as the corresponding polynomial orbits on nilmanifolds cannot be as neatly described as for the previous three examples.

In
[
113
, Theorem 1.18]
, the author showed that algebraic and Weyl complexities are the same.
This is important insofar as it
reduces Problem
33
to
a purely algebraic question of studying algebraic relations of the form (
61
). The author then resolved Problem
33
for a broad class of polynomial families called
homogeneous

[
113
, Theorem 1.11]
, which includes among others all progressions of algebraic complexity 0 and 1. However, the general case
of Problem
33
remains open.

A second major problem, mentioned e.g. in
[
121
, Section 0.9]
,
[
22
, Section 5]
,
[
56
, Problem 9]
,
[
113
, Conjecture 1.9]
is to upper bound Weyl/algebraic complexity uniformly in terms of the length of the progression.

Problem 34

(Upper bound on complexity)
.

Show that for any
\ell
essentially distinct polynomials, their algebraic complexity is at most
\ell-1
.

The upper bound is sharp, as evidenced e.g. by
n,2n,\ldots,\ell n
.
Despite the semblance of a simple algebraic exercise, Problem
34
is notoriously difficult. Although it has been resolved for
\ell\leq 4
in
[
51
, Proposition 3.5]
and
[
125
, Theorem 1.1]
, the proofs consist of tedious case-by-case computations and offer no insight into the general case.

There remain many other questions where characteristic factors are not known. For instance, the following problem has been proposed by Moreira (private communication).

Problem 35
.

Let
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{R}}
be distinct and nonzero. What is the optimal Host-Kra factor controlling the average along
{\lfloor\alpha_{1}n\rfloor},\ldots,{\lfloor\alpha_{\ell}n\rfloor}
for all ergodic systems
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
? In particular, if
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},1
are
{\mathbb{Q}}
-independent, is the average along
{\lfloor\alpha_{1}n\rfloor},

{\lfloor\alpha_{2}n\rfloor}
controlled by the Kronecker factor
{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}(T)
?

For integer polynomials, Problem
33
inquires whether all the obstructions to uniformity come from rational algebraic relations (
61
). Problem
35
asks whether irrational obstructions also play a role.

We conclude this section with a result of Frantzikinakis and the author that gives general conditions for when a particular Host-Kra seminorm/factor control a given average.

Theorem 6.20

(General degree reduction property
[
68
, Theorem 3.11]
)
.

Let
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\colon{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
be sequences, and
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
be a system. Then the Host-Kra seminorm
\lvert\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T}
(equivalently, the factor
{\mathcal{Z}}_{s-1}(T)
) controls
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
on
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
if and only if the following two conditions hold:

(i)

a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
admit Host-Kra seminorm control for the system;

(ii)

\lvert\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T}
controls
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
on
(X,{\mathcal{Z}}_{s}(T),\mu,T)
.

Thus, Theorem
6.20
shows that under the assumption of Host-Kra seminorm control, to check for
{\mathcal{Z}}_{s-1}(T)
-control, it suffices to verify it “one level up”, i.e. for
{\mathcal{Z}}_{s}(T)
-measurable functions. In particular, when
s=1
, Theorem
6.20
reduces to the single-transformation case of Theorem
2.8
.

6.3.
Obstructions in the commuting case

In contrast to the single-transformation case, extremely little is known regarding optimal seminorms and characteristic factors in the commuting case. Even the following simple problem remains open.

Problem 36
.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},T_{2},T_{3})
be a system. What are the optimal seminorms/ factors for the ergodic average along
(T_{1}^{n},T_{2}^{n^{2}},T_{3}^{n^{2}+n})_{n}
?

The family
n,\;n^{2},\;n^{2}+n
is the simplest polynomial family whose terms are pairwise independent but not affinely independent. If
T:=T_{1}=T_{2}=T_{3}
, we know thanks to Frantzikinakis
[
51
, Theorem B]
that
{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}(T)
is characteristic. By the seminorm control from Theorem
3.15
, we know that the Host-Kra factors
{\mathcal{Z}}_{s}(T_{j})
will be characteristic for some
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
; but it is not clear whether
s=1
would do. The study of Problem
36
would likely involve equidistribution problems on nonergodic nilsystems, which can be quite challenging (see
[
38
, Section 7]
for a foretaste).

Another important open problem concerns the structure of multicorrelation sequences of commuting transformations.

Problem 37

(Decomposition of polynomial multicorrelation sequences)
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[t]
. Show that for any system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
and any
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we can decompose

(62)

\displaystyle\int f_{0}\cdot T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{a_{\ell}(n)}f_{\ell}\;d\mu=\phi(n)+\omega(n),

where
\phi
is a nilsequence and
\omega
is a null-sequence.

When
T_{1}=\cdots=T_{\ell}
, such a decomposition is known due to Bergelson-Host-Kra for
a_{j}(n)=jn

[
18
, Theorem 1.9]
and Leibman for general polynomials
[
122
, Theorem 3.1]
. In the commuting case, Frantzikinakis and the author resolved Problem
37
for pairwise independent polynomials
[
69
, Theorem 2.17]
. For pairwise dependent polynomials, only several cases are known. Recently, Leng established (
62
) for
a_{1}(n)=\cdots=a_{\ell}(n)=n

[
123
, Theorem 1.6]
, and later Frantzikinakis and the author extended this to
\ell=2
and arbitrary
a_{1}=a_{2}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[t]

[
66
, Theorem 1.6]
.

In a different direction, Frantzikinakis showed that for every
\varepsilon>0
we can get (
62
) with a weaker condition
\limsup\limits_{N\to\infty}\E\limits_{n\in[N]}|\omega(n)|^{2}\leq\varepsilon
regardless of the choice of polynomials
[
55
, Theorem 1.1]
. But with
\varepsilon=0
, Problem
37
remains unresolved for general polynomials, even for tuples
(T_{1}^{n^{2}},T_{2}^{n^{2}},T_{3}^{n^{2}})_{n}
and
(T_{1}^{n^{2}},T_{2}^{n^{2}},T_{3}^{n^{2}+n})_{n}
.

6.4.
Multiple recurrence

Given that multiple ergodic averages originate in the study of multiple recurrence, it is only fitting to record here several problems on the latter topic that still remain open. The most important one concerns the extension of the multidimensional polynomial Szemerédi theorem to jointly intersective polynomials.

Definition 6.21

(Joint intersectivity)
.

Polynomials
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[t]
are called
jointly intersective
if for every
r\in{\mathbb{N}}
there exists
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
such that
r
divides
a_{1}(n),\ldots,a_{\ell}(n)
.

For background regarding intersective and jointly intersective polynomials, we refer the reader to
[
23
]
.

Problem 38

(Multidimensional polynomial Szemerédi conjecture for jointly intersective polynomials
[
23
, Conjecture 6.3]
)
.

Show that for any jointly intersective polynomials
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[t]
, any system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
, and any
E\in{\mathcal{X}}
with
\mu(E)>0
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{N-M\to\infty}\E_{n\in[M,N)}\mu(E\cap T_{1}^{-a_{1}(n)}E\cap\cdots\cap T_{\ell}^{-a_{\ell}(n)}E)>0.

In fact,
[
23
, Conjecture 6.3]
is stated for more general polynomials over
{\mathbb{Z}}^{k}
; we leave the extension to the interested reader.

When
T_{1}=\cdots=T_{\ell}
, the problem has been resolved by Bergelson, Leibman, and Lesigne
[
23
, Theorem 1.1]
. For commuting transformations, the case
\ell=2
has been addressed by Frantzikinakis and the author
[
66
, Theorem 1.3]
. The general case remains open. Just like with many other open problems on commuting commuting transformations in this survey, a major stumbling block is the absence of usable structure theory for box seminorms and factors.

The next question inquires about the multiple recurrence counterpart of Problem
29
.

Problem 39

(Multiple recurrence for Hardy corners)
.

Let
a\in{\mathcal{H}}
be a Hardy sequence satisfying (
55
) (such as
a(n)=n^{3/2}
). Show that for any system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
and any
E\in{\mathcal{X}}
with
\mu(E)>0
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}\mu(E\cap T_{1}^{-{\lfloor a(n)\rfloor}}E\cap\cdots\cap T_{\ell}^{-{\lfloor a(n)\rfloor}}E)>0.

Obviously, the successful resolution of Problem
29
would automatically address Problem
39
. Alternatively, one could aim for a proof along the lines of Frantzikinakis and Wierdl
[
71
]
that does not require the full identity (
54
).
The fact that Problem
39
remains unresolved demonstrates how far we are from having a satisfactory picture of multiple recurrence for real polynomials, fractional polynomials and more general Hardy sequences.

Lastly, we record one problem regarding popular common differences. In what follows, we use the following definition.

Definition 6.22

(Popular common differences)
.

We say that
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[t]

admit popular common differences
if for every ergodic system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
, set
E\in{\mathcal{X}}
, and
\varepsilon>0
, the set

\displaystyle\left\{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}\colon\mu(E\cap T^{-a_{1}(n)}E\cap\cdots\cap T^{-a_{\ell}(n)}E)>\mu(E)^{\ell+1}-\varepsilon\right\}

is syndetic.

Whereas multiple recurrence is a very general phenomenon, popular common differences constitute a peculiarity that arises only for special patterns. For instance, for nonzero
c_{1}<c_{2}<c_{3}
, the pattern
c_{1}n,\;c_{2}n,\;c_{3}n
admits popular differences if and only if
c_{3}=c_{1}+c_{2}
. Furthermore, linear families of length 4 do not admit them at all. The next problem aims to fully classify those families that admit this behavior.

Problem 40

(Polynomial families admitting popular common differences)
.

Prove or disprove: essentially distinct polynomials
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[t]
with zero constant terms do not admit popular common differences unless they fall into one of the following classes:

(i)

they have algebraic complexity at most 1;

(ii)

they are of the form
c_{1}a(n),\;c_{2}a(n),c_{3}a(n)
for some nonconstant
a\in{\mathbb{Z}}[t]
and nonzero
c_{1}<c_{2}<c_{3}
satisfying
c_{3}=c_{1}+c_{2}
.

The exceptional cases listed above are known to contain popular common differences thanks to Bergelson-Host-Kra
[
18
, Theorem 1.2]
, Frantzikinakis-Kra
[
65
, Theorem 1.3]
, Frantzikinakis
[
51
, Theorem C]
, and the author
[
113
, Theorem 1.16]
. To the reader interested in this topic, we also highly recommend the additive combinatorial work of Sah, Sawhney, and Zhao
[
136
]
that classifies all linear patterns admitting popular common differences and has extensive references to other combinatorial works on the subject.

6.5.
Asymptotic discorrelation

We conclude with one generalization of joint ergodicity that we find worth exploring. We start with a rather immediate restatement of joint ergodicity.

Lemma 6.23
.

Sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
are jointly ergodic for the system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu
,
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
if and only if the following two conditions are satisfied:

(i)

the actions
(T_{j}^{a_{j}(n)})_{n}
are ergodic on
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
for all
1\leq j\leq\ell
;

(ii)

the actions
(T_{j}^{a_{j}(n)})_{n}
are
asymptotically discorrelated
in the sense that

(63)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\prod_{1\leq j\leq\ell}T_{j}^{a_{j}(n)}f_{j}-\prod_{1\leq j\leq\ell}\E_{n\in[N]}T_{j}^{a_{j}(n)}f_{j}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0

for all
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
.

Thus, joint ergodicity = ergodicity + asymptotic discorrelation. Given that we by now understand well joint ergodicity, it makes sense to reach a similar level of understanding for asymptotic discorrelation. We propose one possible characterization of this phenomenon in the spirit of Theorem
2.8
.

Problem 41

(Characterization of asymptotic discorrelation)
.

Suppose that the sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
admit Host-Kra seminorm control for the system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
. Prove or disprove the following: they are asymptotically discorrelated for that system if and only if (
63
) holds whenever
f_{j}
is a nonergodic eigenfunction of
T_{j}
for every
1\leq j\leq\ell
.

Since nonergodic eigenfunctions of
T_{j}
generate the entire
{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}(T_{j})
factor, Problem
41
inquires whether asymptotic discorrelation for all functions can be inferred from (
63
) holding just for
{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}(T_{j})
-measurable functions. It may well be that the characterization proposed by Problem
41
is too good to hope for, and to get asymptotic discorrelation, we need it to hold at least on
{\mathcal{Z}}_{2}(T_{j})
. However, we do not have any explicit counterexamples showing the need for a larger factor than
{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}(T_{j})
.

7.
Beyond commutativity

The entire discussion up to now focused on systems in which the transformations commute with each other. In this section, we dispose of this assumption,
presenting the most important results and open questions for
nilpotent
systems and beyond.

Definition 7.1

(Nilpotent systems)
.

Let
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
. We call
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
a
k
-step nilpotent system
if
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
generate a
k
-step nilpotent group of measure-preserving transformations on a standard probability space
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
. We call a system
nilpotent
if it is
k
-step nilpotent for some
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

7.1.
Nilpotent heuristic

For nilpotent systems, we have two foundational results of Leibman and Walsh.

Theorem 7.2

(Leibman’s nilpotent multiple recurrence theorem
[
117
, Theorem 0.4]
)
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[t]
have zero constant terms. For every nilpotent system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
and every
E\in{\mathcal{X}}
with
\mu(E)>0
, there exists
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that

\displaystyle\mu(E\cap T_{1}^{-a_{1}(n)}E\cap\cdots\cap T_{\ell}^{-a_{\ell}(n)}E)>0.

Theorem 7.3

(Walsh’s norm convergence theorem
[
143
, Theorem 1.1]
)
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[t]
. For every nilpotent system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
and all
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, the average

\displaystyle\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{a_{\ell}(n)}f_{\ell}

converges in
L^{2}(\mu)
.

Both Theorems
7.2
and
7.3
hold for more general expressions, but we have opted to just state the simpler case for the clarity of presentation. Before Theorem
7.3
, the only truly nilpotent (i.e. involving non-commutative actions) average for which norm convergence was known was the double ergodic average

(64)

\displaystyle\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{n}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{n}f_{2},

a result due to Bergelson and Leibman
[
20
]
.

Walsh’s method for proving norm convergence requires rather minimal understanding of the structure of multiple ergodic averages under consideration compared to other results in this survey. Despite its incredible scope, its reach outside polynomials remains rather unexplored - hence the following problem.

Problem 42

(Norm convergence along fractional polynomials)
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
be fractional polynomials. Does the average

\displaystyle\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{a_{\ell}(n)}f_{\ell}

converge in
L^{2}(\mu)
for every nilpotent system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
and all
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
?

Problem
42
remains open even in the commuting case.

We conclude this section’s introduction with a heuristic that guides our thinking on nilpotent actions.

Heuristic 7.4

(Nilpotent heuristic)
.

All good properties (multiple recurrence, norm and pointwise convergence, structural properties) that hold for multiple ergodic averages for commuting systems should extend to nilpotent systems.

7.2.
Groups of superpolynomial growth

While nilpotent actions are good for the purpose of multiple recurrence and norm convergence, the same cannot be said for solvable actions of exponential growth. In
[
20
, Section 4]
, Bergelson and Leibman constructed examples of such actions which are neither good for multiple recurrence nor for norm convergence.
On the other hand, it is unclear what happens for groups of intermediate (i.e. superpolynomial but subexponential) growth.

Problem 43

(Multiple recurrence in groups of intermediate growth)
.

Suppose that
T_{1},T_{2}
generate a group of intermediate growth of measure-preserving transformations on a standard probability space
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
. Does this system admit the
multiple recurrence property
, i.e. given
E\in{\mathcal{X}}
with
\mu(E)>0
, does there exist
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
for which
\mu(E\cap T_{1}^{-n}E\cap T_{2}^{-n}E)>0
?

Problem 44

(Norm convergence in groups of intermediate growth)
.

Suppose that
T_{1},T_{2}
generate a group of intermediate growth of measure-preserving transformations on a standard probability space
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
. Does this system admit the
norm convergence property
, i.e. the average (
64
)
converges in
L^{2}(\mu)
for all
f_{1},f_{2}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
?

An example of an amenable group of intermediate growth is the Grigorchuk group. The study of multiple ergodic averages for groups of intermediate growth encounters obstructions somewhat similar to those faced by sequences of superpolynomial growth (see Section
3.7
): finitely many applications of the van der Corput inequality are incapable of bringing the average to one sufficiently simple to be amenable to known techniques.

For more discussion and conjectures on multiple ergodic averages in solvable groups, we direct the reader to
[
20
, Section 5]
.

7.3.
Independent and distinct-growth sequences

As is the case with commuting systems, the ergodic averages are easier to understand when the sequences are pairwise independent, and even better - if they have distinct growth. Perhaps the strongest validation of Heuristic
7.4
would come from resolving affirmatively the following two problems, which extend Heuristics
3.2
and
3.3
to nilpotent systems.

Problem 45
.

Show that any affinely independent polynomials
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[t]
