admit rational Kronecker factor control for any nilpotent system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
.

Problem 46
.

Show that any pairwise independent polynomials
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[t]
admit Host-Kra seminorm control for any nilpotent system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
.

Problems
45
and
46
seem out of reach of current methods given that both the degree lowering and seminorm smoothing methods used to establish their commutative counterparts rely on commutativity (specifically, the dual-difference interchange step uses commutativity in an essential way). The experience from the commutative case suggests that sequences of distinct growth should do much better. For that reason, we record separately the following two problems, the first of which is a special case of Problems
45
and
46
.

Problem 47
.

Show that any nonconstant polynomials
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[t]
of distinct degrees admit Host-Kra seminorm control/rational Kronecker factor control for any nilpotent system.

While rational Kronecker factor control is a priori harder to establish than Host-Kra seminorm control, obtaining the latter for distinct-degree polynomials and nilpotent systems should give the former when combined with the equidistribution results of Chu, Frantzikinakis, and Host
[
38
, Section 7.4]
- at least for monomials
n,n^{2},\ldots,n^{\ell}
.

Problem 48
.

Let
0<b_{1}<\cdots<b_{\ell}
. Show that the sequences
n^{b_{1}},\ldots,n^{b_{\ell}}
admit Host-Kra seminorm control/invariant factor control for any nilpotent system.

Given that virtually nothing is known on Problems
47
and
48
, we explicitly present two simplest open cases.

Problem 49
.

Show that
n,n^{2}
are jointly ergodic for any 2-step nilpotent system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},T_{2})
with
T_{1},T_{2}
totally ergodic/weakly mixing.

Problem 50
.

Show that
n,n^{3/2}
are jointly ergodic for any 2-step nilpotent system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},T_{2})
with
T_{1},T_{2}
ergodic/weakly mixing.

7.4.
Joint ergodicity on nilpotent actions

We conclude the discussion of nilpotent actions with a joint ergodicity result of Bergelson and Leibman and its potential generalization.

Theorem 7.5

(Characterization of joint ergodicity on nilpotent systems
[
20
, Theorem B]
)
.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},T_{2})
be a
k
-nilpotent system. Then
(T_{1}^{n},T_{2}^{n})_{n}
is jointly ergodic for
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
if and only if the following two conditions hold:

(i)

T_{1}\times T_{2}
is ergodic on
(X\times X,{\mathcal{X}}\otimes{\mathcal{X}},\mu\times\mu)
;

(ii)

the group
\langle T_{1}^{n}T_{2}^{-n}\colon 1\leq n\leq k\rangle
acts ergodically on
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
.

A glimpse at the proof of Theorem
7.5
shows that it exploits heavily the fact that the authors deal with two transformations. Therefore, a more sophisticated method would probably be required to extend it to an arbitrary number of transformations.

Problem 51

(
[
20
, Conjecture 5.5]
)
.

Prove or disprove the following: for any nilpotent system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
, the action
(T_{1}^{n},\ldots,T_{\ell}^{n})_{n}
is jointly ergodic for
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
if and only if the following two conditions hold:

(i)

T_{1}\times\cdots\times T_{\ell}
is ergodic on
(X^{\ell},{\mathcal{X}}^{\otimes\ell},\mu^{\ell})
;

(ii)

for any distinct
1\leq i,j\leq\ell
, the group
\langle T_{i}^{n}T_{j}^{-n}\colon n\in{\mathbb{Z}}\rangle
acts ergodically on
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
.

8.
Other topics

Despite the size of this survey, there are way too many topics related to joint ergodicity that we have not managed to cover. This section aims to list such topics with relevant references but without going into too detailed discussion.

(i)

Interactions of additive and multiplicative systems.
In
[
28
]
, Bergelson and Richter initiated an interesting study of the mutual interactions between additive and multiplicative systems. Charamaras
[
36
]
and Xiao
[
146
]
then proved a number of joint ergodicity and asymptotic discorrelation results for averages involving both additive and multiplicative actions, an example of which is the average

\displaystyle\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{n}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{\Omega(n)}f_{2}

for (not necessarily commuting) measure-preserving transformations
T_{1},T_{2}
.

(ii)

Total joint ergodicity.
Koutsogiannis and Sun
[
107
]
introduced the notion of
total joint ergodicity
, i.e. joint ergodicity along all arithmetic progressions. They proved a number of results and posed several questions for the total joint ergodicity of real polynomials and Hardy sequences.

(iii)

Multiple ergodic averages with entropy assumptions.
Frantzikinakis
[
57
]
and Frantzikinakis-Host
[
63
]
proved norm convergence and structural results on averages

\displaystyle\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{n}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{a(n)}f_{2},

where
a(n)
is a nonlinear polynomial or a fractional power,
T_{1},T_{2}
do not commute, and
T_{1}
has zero entropy. In contrast to earlier works on multiple ergodic averages, they proceeded by investigating Furstenberg systems of sequences
(f(T^{a(n)}x))_{n}
and applying disjointness arguments.
This line of research was carried on by Huang-Shao-Ye
[
91
,
94
,
95
,
96
]
, Austin
[
5
]
, Ryzhikov
[
135
]
, and Kosloff-Sanadhya
[
100
,
101
]
. Most of these works construct counterexamples to the phenomena observed by Frantzikinakis and Host when either
n
is replaced by a nonlinear polynomial or
a(n)=n
. Their upshot is that the behavior distilled by Frantzikinakis and Host is very rare and breaks for even slight modifications of the averages.

(iv)

Topological analogs of joint ergodicity.
Many questions on multiple ergodic averages have natural topological analogs; joint ergodicity is one of them. In the topological setting, the point is to show that for fixed sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
and under suitable assumptions on
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
, homeomorphisms of a compact metric space
X
, the orbit

(T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}x,\ldots,T_{\ell}^{a_{\ell}(n)}x)_{n}

is dense in
X^{\ell}
for some
x\in X
. Starting with Glasner’s topological analog of Theorem
3.4

[
76
]
, this question has been extensively studied e.g. by Moothathu
[
126
]
, Kwietniak-Oprocha
[
116
]
, Huang-Shao-Ye
[
93
]
, Zhang-Zhao
[
147
]
, Qiu
[
133
]
, and Donoso-Koutsogiannis-Sun
[
48
]
.

(v)

Joint ergodicity of piecewise monotone maps.
Bergelson and Son
[
30
,
31
]
proved interesting (norm and pointwise) joint ergodicity results for various piecewise monotone maps on
{\mathbb{T}}
, including beta-transformations, Gauss maps, and interval exchange transformations. These results have applications to the problems of joint normality of numbers and independence of their digit and continuous fraction expansions.

(vi)

Pointwise convergence of multiple ergodic averages.
Starting with the Krause-Mirek-Tao
[
110
]
breakthrough, recent years have seen an outbreak of activity on pointwise convergence of multiple ergodic averages due to Daskalakis
[
41
]
, Kosz-Mirek-Peluse-Wan-Wright
[
102
]
, Krause-Mirek-Tao-Teräväinen
[
111
]
, Teräväinen
[
140
]
, and Wan
[
144
]
. While not directly related to joint ergodicity, they nonetheless imply pointwise versions of many results described in this survey. Earlier results on the topic include the classical work of Bourgain
[
34
]
on double recurrence, as well as the works of Huang-Shao-Ye
[
92
]
and Donoso-Sun
[
49
]
that cover pointwise convergence on distal systems.

(vii)

Asymptotic total ergodicity.
Recent joint ergodicity results appeared in parallel to analogous quantitative discorrelation estimates in the discrete setting, starting with the result of Bourgain and Chang
[
35
]

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left|\E_{m,n\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}f_{0}(m)f_{1}(m+n)f_{2}(m+n^{2})-\E_{m\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}f_{0}(m)\cdot\E_{m\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}f_{1}(m)\cdot\E_{m\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}f_{2}(m)\mathclose{}\right|\ll p^{-1/10}

for 1-bounded functions
f_{0},f_{1},f_{2}:{\mathbb{F}}_{p}\to{\mathbb{C}}
. More results on this form were proved by Peluse
[
128
,
129
]
, Dong-Li-Sawin
[
42
]
, Han-Lacey-Yang
[
84
]
, Bergelson-Best
[
14
]
, and the author
[
114
,
115
]
. Due to parallels with joint ergodicity, Bergelson and Best refer to this phenomenon as
asymptotic joint ergodicity

[
15
]
.

(viii)

Joint ergodicity for general group actions.
Bergelson and Rosenblatt
[
29
]
as well as Son
[
137
]
investigated variants of Theorem
1.11
for general group actions.
In a different direction, Ackelsberg and Bergelson
[
1
]
as well as Best and Ferré-Moragues
[
32
]
extended Theorem
3.8
to actions by more general number fields and rings respectively. The Ackelsberg-Bergelson work followed the Old Joint Ergodicity Strategy, and specifically derived joint ergodicity from an equidistribution result on Weyl systems. By contrast, Best and Ferré-Moragues extended Theorem
2.8
to second-countable locally compact abelian groups and then used it to deduce their result for polynomials.

(ix)

Joint ergodicity for flows.
In largely overlooked
[
59
, Chapter 7]
, Frantzikinakis proved pointwise joint ergodicity results for not necessarily commuting flows
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
. Specifically, he showed that if
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{H}}
are Hardy sequences of “distinct but comparable growth”, then

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\int_{0}^{N}f_{1}(T_{1}^{a_{1}(t)}x)\cdots f_{\ell}(T_{\ell}^{a_{\ell}(t)}x)\;dt=\E(f_{1}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{1}))(x)\cdots\E(f_{\ell}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{\ell}))(x)

almost everywhere.
The method fails for sequences whose growth is either “too similar” (like
t^{2},\;t^{2}+t
and
t,\;t\log t
) or “too different” (like
t,\;2^{t}
). The case of distinct noninteger fractional powers was previously established by Bergelson, Boshernitzan, and Bourgain
[
16
]
.

Appendix A
Notation, conventions, and preliminaries

A.1.
Notation

The symbols
{\mathbb{C}},{\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathbb{Q}},{\mathbb{Z}},{\mathbb{N}}_{0},{\mathbb{N}},{\mathbb{P}}
respectively stand for the sets of complex numbers, reals, positive reals, rationals, integers, nonnegative integers, positive integers, and primes. With
{\mathbb{T}}
, we denote the one dimensional torus, and we often identify it with
{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}
or with
[0,1)
. Likewise,
\mathbb{S}^{1}
stands for the unit circle inside
{\mathbb{C}}
. For
p
prime,
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
stands for the finite field with
p
elements. For
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we denote
[N]:=\{1,\ldots,N\}
and
[\pm N]:=\{-N,\ldots,N\}
. If
E\subseteq G
is a subset of a group
G
, then
\langle E\rangle
is the subgroup generated by
E
.

For a finite set
E,
we define the average of a sequence
a:E\to{\mathbb{C}}
as

\E_{n\in E}a(n):=\frac{1}{|E|}\sum_{n\in E}a(n).

We let
{\mathcal{C}}z:=\overline{z}
be the complex conjugate of
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
.

For a set
E
, we define its indicator function by
1_{E}
.

We denote
\ell
-tuples with the bold notation
{\mathbf{h}}=(h_{1},\ldots,h_{\ell})
. Given a system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,

T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
and
{\mathbf{h}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}
, we let
T^{\mathbf{h}}:=T_{1}^{h_{1}}\cdots T_{\ell}^{h_{\ell}}
.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
be a probability space. We call a function
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)

1
-bounded
if
\left\|f\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mu)}\leq 1
.

We denote the space of complex-valued continuous functions a topological space
Y
by
C(Y).

For a
\sigma
-algebra
{\mathcal{A}}\subseteq{\mathcal{X}}
and
f\in L^{1}(\mu)
, we let
\E(f|{\mathcal{A}})
be the conditional expectation of
f
with respect to
{\mathcal{A}}
.

Throughout, all the equalities between functions and sets are understood to hold up to sets of measure 0.

Given two functions
a,b:(t_{0},\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
for some
t_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we write

(i)

a\ll b
or
a=O(b)
if there exists
C>0
such that
|a(x)|\leq C|b(x)|
for all
t\geq t_{0}
;

(ii)

a\asymp b
if
a\ll b
and
b\ll a
;

(iii)

a\prec b
or
a=o(b)
if
\lim\limits_{x\to\infty}\frac{a(x)}{b(x)}=0
.

A.2.
Van der Corput inequality

The van der Corput inequality is a ubiquitous tool in ergodic theory and additive combinatorics. Here is one version.

Lemma A.1

(Van der Corput inequality)
.

Let
(v_{n})_{n}
be a 1-bounded sequence in a Hilbert space. Then

\displaystyle\limsup_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}v_{n}\right\|^{2}\leq\limsup_{H\to\infty}\E_{h\in[H]}\limsup_{N\to\infty}\mathopen{}\left|\E_{n\in[N]}\langle v_{n},v_{n+h}\rangle\mathclose{}\right|.

For an extensive discussion on the role of Lemma
A.1
in ergodic theory, consult the survey of Bergelson and Moreira
[
26
]
.

Appendix B
Host-Kra theory

This section is dedicated to the basic properties of box seminorms, defined by Host
[
87
]
, and their special but important subclass: Host-Kra seminorms originating in
[
89
]
. We refer the reader e.g. to
[
46
,
67
,
89
,
90
,
139
]
for the proofs of their various properties listed in this section.

B.1.
Box seminorms: definition and basic properties

Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system and
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
. For
{\mathbf{h}}=(h_{1},\ldots,h_{\ell})\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}
, we set
T^{\mathbf{h}}:=T_{1}^{h_{1}}\cdots T_{\ell}^{h_{\ell}}
.
We define the
multiplicative derivative
of
f
along
{\mathbf{h}}
via

\Delta_{{\mathbf{h}}}f:=f\cdot T^{\mathbf{h}}\overline{f}

and for
{\mathbf{h}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{h}}_{s}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}
, we denote the
iterated multiplicative derivative
via

\Delta_{{\mathbf{h}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{h}}_{s}}f:=\Delta_{{\mathbf{h}}_{1}}\cdots\Delta_{{\mathbf{h}}_{s}}f=\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}T^{\epsilon_{1}{\mathbf{h}}_{1}+\cdots\epsilon_{s}{\mathbf{h}}_{s}}f.

For subgroups
G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}
, we define the
box seminorm of
f
along
G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}
by

(65)

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}^{2^{s}}:=\E_{{\mathbf{h}}_{s}\in G_{s}}\cdots\E_{{\mathbf{h}}_{1}\in G_{1}}\int\Delta_{{\mathbf{h}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{h}}_{s}}f\;d\mu.

Above, we set

\displaystyle\E_{{\mathbf{h}}\in G}:=\lim_{H\to\infty}\E_{{\mathbf{h}}\in G\cap[\pm H]^{\ell}},

noting that the limit is well-defined,
(G\cap[\pm H]^{\ell})_{H}
can be replaced by any Følner sequence on
G
, and we can combine several limits into one (e.g. we can replace
\E_{{\mathbf{h}}_{s}\in G_{s}}\cdots\E_{{\mathbf{h}}_{1}\in G_{1}}
by
\E_{({\mathbf{h}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{h}}_{s})\in G_{1}\times\cdots\times G_{s}}
). Whenever convenient, we use the following shorthand notations:

(i)

if each of
G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}
appears
k_{1},\ldots,k_{s}
times respectively, we write the seminorm as
\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1}^{\times k_{1}},\ldots,G_{s}^{\times k_{s}}}
;

(ii)

if
G_{j}=\langle{\mathbf{v}}_{j}\rangle
for
1\leq j\leq s
, we also write
\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}=\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{v}}_{s}}
;

(iii)

if additionally
R_{j}=T^{{\mathbf{v}}_{j}}
for
1\leq j\leq s
, we denote
\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}=\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{R_{1},\ldots,R_{s}}
;

(iv)

if additionally
R:=R_{1}=\cdots R_{s}
, we write
\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}=\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{s,R}
.

In the latter case, we call
\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{s,R}
the
degree-
s
Host-Kra seminorm of
f
along
R
.

Box seminorms satisfy several standard properties freely used throughout the paper:

(i)

(Permutation invariance) for
any permutation
\sigma:[s]\to[s]
,

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}=\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{\sigma(1)},\ldots,G_{\sigma(s)}};

(ii)

(Monotonicity) we have

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1}}\leq\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1},G_{2}}\leq\cdots\leq\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}};

(iii)

(Inductive formula) for any
1\leq s^{\prime}\leq s
,

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}^{2^{s}}=\E_{{\mathbf{h}}_{s}\in G_{s}}\cdots\E_{{\mathbf{h}}_{s^{\prime}+1}\in G_{s^{\prime}+1}}\lvert\!|\!|\Delta_{{\mathbf{h}}_{s^{\prime}+1},\ldots,{\mathbf{h}}_{s}}f|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s^{\prime}}}^{2^{s^{\prime}}};

(iv)

(Scaling) for any subgroups
G^{\prime}_{1}\subseteq G_{1},\ldots,G^{\prime}_{s}\subseteq G_{s}
,

(66)

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}\leq\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,G_{s}^{\prime}};

conversely, if
s\geq 2
and
|G_{i}/G_{i}^{\prime}|=r_{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq s
, then

(67)

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,G_{s}^{\prime}}\leq|r_{1}\cdots r_{s}|^{1/2^{s}}\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}.

B.2.
Box factors and dual functions

Once again, let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system and
G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}
be subgroups. Set
\{0,1\}^{s}_{*}:=\{0,1\}^{s}\setminus\{{0}\}
. For
(f_{\underline{\epsilon}})_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}_{*}}\subseteq L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we define the
dual function of
(f_{\underline{\epsilon}})_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}_{*}}
along
G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}
via

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}((f_{\underline{\epsilon}})_{\underline{\epsilon}}):=\E_{{\mathbf{h}}_{s}\in G_{s}}\cdots\E_{{\mathbf{h}}_{1}\in G_{1}}\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}_{*}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}T_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}{\mathbf{h}}_{1}+\cdots+\epsilon_{s}{\mathbf{h}}_{s}}f_{\underline{\epsilon}}.

We call
s
the
degree
of the dual function. If
f_{\underline{\epsilon}}=f
for all
{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}_{*}
, we simply denote

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}(f):={\mathcal{D}}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}((f_{\underline{\epsilon}})_{\underline{\epsilon}}),

and when
G:=G_{1}=\cdots=G_{s}
, we let

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}((f_{\underline{\epsilon}})_{\underline{\epsilon}})={\mathcal{D}}_{s,G}((f_{\underline{\epsilon}})_{\underline{\epsilon}}).

Dual functions arise in the identity

(68)

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{R_{1},\ldots,R_{s}}^{2^{s}}=\int f\cdot{\mathcal{D}}_{R_{1},\ldots,R_{s}}(f)\,d\mu,

which should be seen as a
weak inverse theorem
for box seminorms: if
\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{R_{1},\ldots,R_{s}}
is large, then
f
correlates with a dual function along
R_{1},\ldots,R_{s}
.
While too weak for applications regarding norm convergence, this inverse theorem is nevertheless sufficient for the analytic manipulations performed in the seminorm smoothing argument in order to show Host-Kra seminorm control.

While far from obvious, the
L^{2}(\mu)
-closed linear span of dual functions
{\mathcal{D}}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}((f_{\underline{\epsilon}})_{\underline{\epsilon}})
forms an algebra
Z(G_{1},\ldots,G_{\ell})
invariant under
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
. Using the usual identification between closed invariant subalgebras of
L^{2}(\mu)
and invariant sub-
\sigma
-algebras of
{\mathcal{X}}
, there exists a factor
{\mathcal{Z}}({G_{1},\ldots G_{s}})\subseteq{\mathcal{X}}
, called the
box factor
along
G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}
, such that
Z(G_{1},\ldots,G_{\ell})=L^{2}({\mathcal{Z}}({G_{1},\ldots G_{s}}))
.

The factor
{\mathcal{Z}}({G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}})
satisfies the well-known property

(69)

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}=0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\E(f|{\mathcal{Z}}(G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}))=0;

one direction follows from (
68
) while the other one is a consequence of the Gowers-Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. In addition, box factors satisfying the following properties that can be directly deduced from the similar properties for seminorms:

(i)

(Permutation invariance)
{\mathcal{Z}}(G_{1},\ldots,G_{s})={\mathcal{Z}}(G_{\sigma(1)},\ldots,G_{\sigma(s)})
for
any permutation
\sigma:[s]\to[s]
;

(ii)

(Monotonicity) we have

(70)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Z}}(G_{1})\subseteq{\mathcal{Z}}({G_{1},G_{2}})\subseteq\cdots\subseteq{\mathcal{Z}}({G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}});

(iii)

(Scaling) for any subgroups
G^{\prime}_{1}\subseteq G_{1},\ldots,G^{\prime}_{s}\subseteq G_{s}
, we have

{\mathcal{Z}}(G_{1},\ldots,G_{s})\subseteq{\mathcal{Z}}(G_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,G_{s}^{\prime}),

with equality if
s\geq 2
and every
G_{i}/G_{i}^{\prime}
is finite.

When
G:=G_{1}=\cdots=G_{s}
, we denote
{\mathcal{Z}}_{s-1}(G):={\mathcal{Z}}(G_{1},\ldots,G_{s})
and call it
Host-Kra factor
along
G
of degree
s-1
. The rather unfortunate choice of indices is justified by the
Host-Kra structure theorem
(Theorem
1.7
), which uncovers a deep connection between the (ergodic) Host-Kra factor of a fixed degree to nilsystems of the same degree. The Host-Kra structure theorem is the main reason why Host-Kra seminorms/factors are vastly preferable to box seminorms/factors. Except in a few special cases, the structure theory for the latter is completely unknown. The simplest possible open case is provided in the following problem.

Problem 52

(Structure theory for box factors)
.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,
​
T_{1},T_{2})
be a system. Find a useful structure theorem for the factor
{\mathcal{Z}}(T_{1},T_{2},T_{1}T_{2})
(possibly on a suitable extension).

The additive combinatorial version of Problem
52
is currently the major stumbling block for proving reasonable bounds in the multidimensional Szemerédi theorem - see
[
131
, Section 4.2]
for the discussion.

B.3.
Low-degree Host-Kra factors

We now specialize to the case of a single transformation. Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
be a system; we summarize the properties of the factors
{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(T),{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}(T)
.
For
{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(T)
, we observe that
{\mathcal{D}}_{1,T}(f)=\E(\overline{f}|{\mathcal{I}}(T))
; hence

(71)

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{1,T}^{2}=\int f\cdot\E(\overline{f}|{\mathcal{I}}(T))\;d\mu,

and
{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(T)={\mathcal{I}}(T)

is the
\sigma
-algebra of
T
-invariant functions/sets.

To describe the
{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}(T)
factor, we need the notion of nonergodic eigenfunctions.

Definition B.1

(Nonergodic eigenfunctions)
.

A function
\chi\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
is called a
nonergodic eigenfunction
of
T
if it satisfies the following properties:

(i)

T\chi=\lambda\chi
for some
T
-invariant
\lambda\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
(which we call the
nonergodic eigenvalue
of
f
);

(ii)

|\chi(x)|\in\{0,1\}
for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in X
, and
\lambda(x)=0
whenever
\chi(x)=0
.

For ergodic systems, a nonergodic eigenfunction is either the zero function or a classical unit-modulus eigenfunction. For general systems, each nonergodic eigenfunction
\chi
satisfies

\chi(Tx)=1_{E}(x)\cdot e(\phi(x))\cdot\chi(x)

for some
T
-invariant set
E\in{\mathcal{X}}
and measurable
T
-invariant function
\phi\colon X\to{\mathbb{T}}
.

Example B.2
.

Consider the nonergodic system
X={\mathbb{T}}^{2}
,
T(x,y)=(x,y+x)
. All its classical eigenfunctions are constant; by contrast, functions
\chi(x,y)=e(kx+ly)
are nonergodic eigenfunctions for any
k,l\in{\mathbb{Z}}
since
\chi(T(x,y))=e(lx)\chi(x,y)
.

By
[
62
, Theorem 5.2]
, the factor
{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}(T)
is generated by nonergodic eigenfunctions.

Nonergodic eigenfunctions can be used to give a
quantitative
inverse theorem for the degree-2 Host-Kra seminorm. The version below is used in the proof of Proposition
5.7
; since it is stronger than those stated in the literature, we provide a full proof.

Proposition B.3
.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
be a system. For every 1-bounded
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
and every
\varepsilon>0
we can find a nonergodic eigenfunction
\chi
such that

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{2,T}^{4}\leq\int f\cdot\chi\;d\mu+\varepsilon,

and moreover
\E(f\cdot\chi|{\mathcal{I}}(T))\geq 0
.

Proof.

By
[
69
, Proposition 4.3]
, we can find a nonergodic eigenfunction
\chi_{0}
such that

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{2,T}^{4}\leq\reel\mathopen{}\left(\int f\cdot\chi_{0}\;d\mu\mathclose{}\right)+\varepsilon.

Observe that

\displaystyle\reel\mathopen{}\left(\int f\cdot\chi_{0}\;d\mu\mathclose{}\right)=\reel\mathopen{}\left(\int\E(f\cdot\chi_{0}|{\mathcal{I}}(T))\;d\mu\mathclose{}\right)\leq\int\mathopen{}\left|\E(f\cdot\chi_{0}|{\mathcal{I}}(T))\mathclose{}\right|\;d\mu.

Then there exists a function
\lambda\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
for which

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left|\E(f\cdot\chi_{0}|{\mathcal{I}}(T))\mathclose{}\right|=\lambda\cdot\E(f\cdot\chi_{0}|{\mathcal{I}}(T)),

and which takes value on the unit circle whenever
\E(f\cdot\chi_{0}|{\mathcal{I}}(T))\neq 0
and equals 0 otherwise. Moreover, the definition clearly shows that
\lambda
is
T
-invariant. Hence

\displaystyle\int\mathopen{}\left|\E(f\cdot\chi_{0}|{\mathcal{I}}(T))\mathclose{}\right|\;d\mu=\int\lambda\cdot\E(f\cdot\chi_{0}|{\mathcal{I}}(T))\;d\mu=\int f\cdot(\chi_{0}\cdot\lambda)\;d\mu,

and so the result follows with
\chi:=\chi_{0}\cdot\lambda
, which can be easily seen to be be a nonergodic eigenfunction.
∎

When
T
is ergodic,
{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}(T)
equals the
Kronecker factor
, the largest factor of
{\mathcal{X}}
measure-isomorphic to a group rotation, generated by
eigenfunctions
of
T
, i.e. functions
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
satisfying
Tf=e(\beta)f
for some
\beta\in{\mathbb{T}}
(called the
eigenvalue
of
f
).
Many properties of
{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}(T)
can then be read off from its
spectrum
, i.e. the subgroup of
{\mathbb{T}}
consisting of all the eigenvalues of
T
.

B.4.
Nilsystems

While the point of modern joint ergodicity tools is to avoid proving complicated equidistribution results on nilsystems, the latter nevertheless arise in many places throughout the survey. Therefore, we state the most important definitions, directing the reader to
[
90
, Chapters 10 & 11]
for definitions and major properties.

An
s
-step nilmanifold
is a compact homogeneous space
Y=G/\Gamma
where
G
is an
s
-step nilpotent Lie group and
\Gamma
is a discrete cocompact subgroup. For
b\in G
, the transformation
S\colon Y\to Y
defined by
Sy=by
is called an
s
-step nilrotation
on
S
and the system
(Y,{\mathcal{Y}},m,S)
, where
{\mathcal{Y}}
is the Borel
\sigma
-algebra and
m
is the Haar measure on
Y
, is called an
s
-step nilsystem.

Example B.4

(Examples of nilsystems)
.

Three classical examples of nilsystems are:

(i)

Rotations on
{\mathbb{T}}
;

(ii)

Skew products
T(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})=(x_{1}+\alpha,x_{2}+x_{1},\ldots,x_{d}+x_{d-1})
on
{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
;

(iii)

Left-multiplication on the Heisenberg nilmanifold
\begin{pmatrix}1&{\mathbb{R}}&{\mathbb{R}}\\
0&1&{\mathbb{R}}\\
0&0&{\mathbb{R}}\end{pmatrix}/\begin{pmatrix}1&{\mathbb{Z}}&{\mathbb{Z}}\\
0&1&{\mathbb{Z}}\\
0&0&{\mathbb{Z}}\end{pmatrix}
.

Following
[
18
]
, we say that
\psi:{\mathbb{Z}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
is a
nilsequence
if it is a uniform limit of sequences of the form
n\mapsto F(b^{n}y)
for some
b\in G
,
y\in Y
, and
F\in C(Y)
(we note a notational disparity between the ergodic and combinatorial works on the subject: additive combinatorists call such functions
generalized nilsequences
, referring only to functions like
n\mapsto F(b^{n}y)
as nilsequences). We call
\omega:{\mathbb{Z}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
a
null-sequence
if
\lim\limits_{N\to\infty}\E\limits_{n\in[N]}|\omega(n)|^{2}=0
.

Example B.5

(Examples of nilsequences)
.

The nilsystems in Example
B.4
give rise to the following three classes of nilsequences:

(i)

(Linear phases)
\psi(n)=e(\alpha n)
for any
\alpha\in{\mathbb{T}}
;

(ii)

(Polynomial phases)
\psi(n)=e(p(n))
for any
p\in{\mathbb{R}}[t]
;

(iii)

(Bracket/generalized polynomials)
\psi(n)=e(\alpha n{\lfloor\beta n\rfloor})
.

Appendix C
Hardy fields

Let
B
be the collection of equivalence classes (“germs”) of real valued functions defined on some halfline
(t_{0},\infty)
for
t_{0}\geq 0
, where we identify two functions that eventually agree. A
Hardy field
is a subfield of the ring
(B,+,\cdot)
that is closed under differentiation
[
85
]
. We call
a\colon(t_{0},\infty)\to{\mathbb{R}}
a
Hardy-field function
if it belongs to some Hardy field, in which case we call
(a(n))_{n}
a
Hardy sequence
.
By abuse of notation, we speak of “functions” rather than “germs”, understanding that all the operations defined and statements made for elements of
{\mathcal{H}}
are considered only for sufficiently large values of
t\in\mathbb{R}
.
We say that
a\in{\mathcal{H}}
has
polynomial growth
if there exists
d>0
such that
a(t)\ll t^{d}
.
Basic properties of Hardy-field functions can be found in
[
33
,
53
,
99
]
.

A typical example of a Hardy field is the Hardy field
\mathcal{LE}
of
logarithmico-exponential functions
, i.e., functions constructed using a finite combination of symbols
+,-,\times,\div
,
\exp,

\log
acting on the real variable
t
and on real constants. These include, for example, the functions
t^{b}(\log{t})^{c}
, where
b,c\in{\mathbb{R}}
. For many results stated in this survey, we need the Hardy field to be closed under composition and compositional inverses (and occasionally also under variable shifts
t\mapsto t+c
). Since there exists a Hardy field satisfying these closure properties and containing
{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{E}}
, we fix such a field throughout the survey, and we let
{\mathcal{H}}
be the collection of Hardy functions of polynomial growth lying inside this field.
